Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? 
A Study of the Roads to and Effects of the Divisor-Based 
Biproportional Apportionment Method in Switzerland
Thesis 
presented to the Faculty of Arts and Social Sciences 
of the University of Zurich 
for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy 
by 
Sandro Remo Lüscher 
Supervisory Committee: 
Prof. Dr. Thomas Widmer (principal supervisor), 
Prof. Dr. Lucas Leemann (co-supervisor) 
Specimen Copy for the Doctoral Committee 
Zurich, August 28, 2024
Faculty of Arts and Social Sciences 

=== PDF PAGE 2 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
i 
Abstract
Electoral systems are the backbone of democratic governance. They structure po￾litical competition, distribute formal power, and influence voter behavior. The 
legal design of these systems is therefore central to the stability and functioning 
of democracy. However, electoral systems are not just mechanical frameworks; 
they are dynamic arenas where high democratic ideals often collide with the prag￾matic interests of political actors, making them a captivating subject for research. 
In Switzerland, the introduction of the biproportional apportionment method in 
cantonal parliamentary elections marks a significant innovation. This method, 
designed to enhance vote-seat proportionality while preserving existing constit￾uency structures, has been implemented in nine Swiss cantons since 2005. De￾spite its widespread adoption, there has been a notable absence of research on its 
broader political implications. This study addresses this gap by investigating the 
impact of biproportional apportionment on the political landscapes of Swiss can￾tons. It examines whether the anticipated increase in proportionality has affected 
key political structures, including party competition, voter turnout, and the over￾all party landscape. Furthermore, the study delves into the political processes that 
shaped the reform, analyzing how the positions, alliances, and conflicts among 
political actors influenced the final design. 
The findings reveal that the biproportional apportionment reform has indeed led 
to a significant improvement in proportionality of parliamentary elections. How￾ever, its impact on other political dimensions has been limited. While the reform 
succeeded in reducing electoral misrepresentation of parties, it did not produce a 
marked increase in voter turnout or significantly alter the competitive dynamics 
among parties as initially anticipated. Furthermore, the reform did not result in a 
proliferation of legislative parties as widely assumed. The analysis of the political 
processes behind the reform adoptions shows that the final reform designs were 
heavily influenced by the patterns of conflict during decision-making and often 
lead to compromises that mitigated the full reform potential. 
Key words: electoral systems ▪ proportional representation ▪ electoral reform ▪
biproportional apportionment ▪ divisor method ▪ counterfactual estimation

Acknowledgements 
This dissertation was completed during my employment as an assistant at the Chair of Policy Analysis and 
Evaluation, under the supervision of Prof. Dr. Thomas Widmer, at the Department of Political Science, 
University of Zurich, from July 2019 to August 2024, with an interruption due to illness in early 2023. 
I would like to take this opportunity to express my sincere gratitude to everyone who has accompanied and 
supported me on this enriching, though occasionally challenging, journey. First and foremost, I wish to 
thank my doctoral advisor, Prof. Dr. Thomas Widmer, for his extremely helpful and constructive feedback 
on drafts of both my dissertation and scientific papers. His original ideas and genuine interest in my research 
topic were greatly appreciated. I truly value the considerable freedom I was granted in both my research and 
working conditions. His support during my extended illness are also something for which I am sincerely 
grateful. 
I would also like to thank Prof. Dr. Lucas Leemann for his valuable feedback, as well as all the members of 
my doctoral committee, including Prof. Dr. Patrick Emmenegger as the external reviewer, Prof. Dr. Daniel 
Kübler as the chair, and Dr. Benjamin Schlegel as a committee member. Their voluntary engagement is 
highly appreciated. 
My thanks extend to my departmental colleagues for their valuable feedback during seminars, and to those 
from various social science disciplines with whom I had the pleasure of sharing both professional exchanges 
and cheerful moments throughout my coursework. I do not want to miss the opportunity to thank Dr. 
Doreen Spörer-Wagner, the faculty’s highly appreciated PhD coordinator, for her assistance with all kinds 
of technical and administrative questions. 
Beyond the department, I am especially grateful to Dr. Thomas Milic, whose outstanding seminars during 
my master’s studies significantly deepened my interest in Swiss politics and influenced my decision to pur￾sue the doctorate. My sincere thanks also go to Claudio Kuster, with whom I had many enriching and often 
controversial discussions. I greatly benefited from his insights, advice, and deep knowledge of Swiss politics. 
I am also indebted to everyone who assisted me during the data collection phase, whether by providing 
expert information or supplying data. The cantonal authorities promptly provided the required information 
in the necessary formats, and I received excellent support during my on-site research from archivists and 
librarians. 
On a personal note, I wish to extend my heartfelt thanks to my family, who have always been a source of big 
support and a crucial anchor during the challenging phases of my doctoral journey. I am particularly thank￾ful to my grandmother, Dosolina, for the joyful moments we shared and for her significant mental support. 
This thesis is dedicated to my friend Mikael, who passed away on August 10, 2024. Rest in peace. 
Zurich, August 2024 
Sandro Lüscher

“It is (…) necessary that the institutions be so constructed so as not to be 
biased in favor of any voter (or group of voters) or in favor of any alternative. 
We will find that this situation is the exception rather than the rule among 
countries that are commonly referred to as democracies.” (McGann 2006: 5) 
0. Introduction 2,3
 
“Periodic, universal, free, equal, and secret” elections form the bedrock of liberal democracy (Kley 2016). 
Democratic elections are rule-based procedures that legitimize political authority, structure political com￾petition and determine “who gets what and how” (Charvát 2023: 1). The “how” falls into the domain of 
electoral systems, which act as the manual for the election process. They are grounded in specific legal prin￾ciples and define the mechanism by which formal political power is distributed among the competitors. 
They are “the cogs that keep the wheels of democracy properly functioning”, as Farrell (2001: 2) allegorically 
put it. However, their impact extends beyond mere technicalities. They also have implications for the parties 
and the party system, for the formation of government and for voter behaviour (Cincea 2013: 176). This 
relevance explains why electoral systems are a focal point of intense political debate. As Sartori (1994: 27) 
wryly observed: “(...) if electoral systems were of little consequence why on earth would politicians fight so 
bitterly about them? And why would reformers fight so persistently to have them changed?” 
0.1 Background 
Modern parliamentary elections emphasize proportionality, grounded in the principles of electoral and po￾litical equity (Nohlen 2014: 43-45). This principle dictates that legislative seats should be distributed in pro￾portion to the share of votes each party receives (Lijphart 1994, Cincea 2013, Nohlen 2014). Proportional 
representation is a multilayered concept with a hierarchical structure: votes can be counted, weighted, and 
translated into seats with varying degrees of equity. While all apportionment methods ensure equal vote 
counting as a fundamental prerequisite of representative systems (“one man, one vote”), only PR-based sys￾tems achieve higher levels of electoral equity, as illustrated in Table 1 (Kley 2016). 
Equity in… …count value … voting power … electoral success 
MR/Plurality Yes No No 
Unidimensional PR Yes Yes No 
Bidimensional PR Yes Yes Yes 
Table 1: Representation formulae and the different layers of electoral equity.
Typically, electoral areas are subdivided into smaller districts to bring representatives closer to voters (Ramí￾rez et al. 2008). However, this subdivision creates barriers, as parties have to obtain a certain vote share to 
be included in seat allocation. Votes for parties falling below these district-induced thresholds are effectively 
wasted, undermining proportional representation and conflicting with democracy’s promise of equal polit￾ical representation. This challenge highlights a fundamental conflict between two competing concepts of 
representation: the representation of parties versus the representation of territories (Pukelsheim 2017: 259). 
The quest to resolve the dilemma of balancing district and party representation has engaged scholars and 
electoral engineers for decades, with the mixed-member electoral system emerging as the most prominent 
solution, often hailed as the “best of both worlds” (Shugart & Wattenberg 2001, Batto et al. 2016). In 2004, 
2
 Contact details of the author: Sandro Lüscher, sandro.luescher@gmail.com, +41 79 761 94 20. 
3
 Parts of this and the following chapter draw on two previously published papers (cf. Lüscher 2021, Lüscher 2023). 

=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
3 
the German mathematician Friedrich Pukelsheim introduced a new approach in the context of subnational 
Swiss parliamentary elections. His biproportional (or double-proportional) divisor method with standard 
rounding, which debuted in Zurich and has since spread throughout Switzerland, ensures proportional rep￾resentation of parties while preserving electoral districts (Pukelsheim & Schuhmacher 2004).
The new method ensures optimal vote-seat proportionality by closely mapping voter preferences onto par￾liamentary seats. It guarantees that every voter has nearly the same impact on the election outcome and the 
composition of parliament, irrespective of their constituency or party affiliation. According to its develop￾ers, this is achieved by cross-district vote tallying (Pukelsheim & Schuhmacher 2011). The new method re￾fines traditional apportionment by shifting the initial allocation from the lower tier (district level) to the 
upper tier, thereby minimizing systemic biases and the threshold effects associated with small districts.4
 It 
employs the Sainte-Laguë divisor method with standard rounding of non-integer quotients, rather than 
rounding them down, to ensure equitable representation. Furthermore, the biproportional approach in￾cludes a sub-apportionment step that balances both party and territorial representation. In this respect, bi￾proportionality is similar to mixed-member electoral systems, though it addresses the allocation problem 
differently. By closely approximating ideal vote-seat proportionality and minimizing the loss of votes in the 
allocation process, the biproportional method, along with mixed-member and at-large PR systems, stands 
out as one of the few apportionment methods that advances to the highest levels of political equity in dem￾ocratic elections (as empirically possible). The combination of directives from the FSC and positive appli￾cation experiences has led to the adoption of the new seat allocation method in nine cantons, starting in 
2005 in the Canton of Zurich. In the Canton of Zurich, the method is also used for sub-cantonal elections 
to city parliaments. 
0.2 Research gap, main arguments and research questions 
Despite extensive research on the mathematical aspects5
 and simulative applications6
 of the biproportional 
divisor method, there is a lack of studies examining its broader implications beyond the distributional di￾mension. This is surprising given the high public expectations and hopes placed on the new apportionment 
formula. Specifically, small parties disadvantaged under prior electoral law were anticipated to benefit from 
the change, potentially stimulating party competition and spurring voter turnout. However, despite its use 
in over a third of all Swiss cantons, no studies have yet examined how the new procedure effectively impacts 
cantonal political systems. 
This dissertation fills this gap by examining the effects of electoral reforms on political dynamics. It investi￾gates whether the anticipated increase in overall proportionality has influenced political parameters, and if 
so, how and to what extent. The Swiss cantons provide a unique research opportunity for analyzing the 
short- and medium-term aggregate-level effects of biproportional reforms. Using Swiss cantons that have 
implemented the new method as a case group and the non-reformed as controls, I argue that the prospect 
of a more proportional translation from votes into seats boosts local democracy in a manner similar to MR￾PR transitions. Drawing on a vast body of literature on electoral system effects rooting in the pioneering 
4
 By “small districts” I refer exclusively to the number of allocated seats, not the geographical size. 
5
 Cf. Bacharach 1970, Balinski & Demange 1989a, Balinski & Demange 1989b, Gassner 1991, Balinski & Young 2001, 
Pukelsheim 2004, Maier et al. 2010, Demange 2011, Pukelsheim 2017, Pukelsheim 2019. 
6
 Cf. Balinski 2002, Bochsler 2005, Pennisi et al. 2006, Zachariassen & Zachariasen 2006, Ramírez et al. 2008, Oelber￾mann & Pukelsheim 2011, Hartmann 2023.

=== PDF PAGE 15 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
4 
work of Maurice Duverger (1954), I hypothesize that party system fragmentation and voter turnout will 
increase, and more candidates and parties are encouraged to participate in elections. 
To avoid endogeneity and acknowledge that electoral systems are not neutral but rather highly instrumental 
institutions shaped by political maneuvering, this dissertation also looks at the different contexts of reform 
formulation and implementation. In this part, electoral systems and their reforms are viewed as products of 
political decision-making and the efforts of actors seeking to influence these processes to their benefit. While 
existing research predominantly focuses on transitions from MR to PR, there is very limited research on 
“within-PR” transitions. Taking a process-oriented view, I argue that theories linking electoral institutions 
and their design to political decision-making processes – typically in the context of MR-PR transitions – are 
also suited to explain transitions from unidimensional PR to bidimensional PR. More specifically, I contend 
that the parties’ stances on reform are largely determined by their anticipated post-reform status. Parties 
expecting an improvement in their electoral standing after reform are likely to advocate for radical reform, 
while those anticipating a decline in their post-reform electoral standing are expected to resist or dilute re￾form. Additionally, if the reform process was contentious – whether due to partisan opposition or direct 
democratic challenges – the final reform design is likely to be less distorted compared to a reform arising 
from a cross-party consensus, where the main parties may introduce distortive features as parts of political 
horse-trading. 
Thus, this dissertation examines the innovation of biproportional apportionment from two different angles: 
as a consequence of political conflicts over electoral law and as a factor inducing changes in the political 
landscape. This approach combines two different branches in electoral systems research. First, the long￾established tradition that views electoral systems as exogenously “given” and analytically focuses on how 
they affect political outcomes. However, given that electoral systems are shaped by the parties they are meant 
to regulate or the incumbents they help re-elect, there are doubts about the causal independence of these 
rules (Benoit 2007, Riera 2013). Since political actors are cognizant about the effects of electoral rules, they 
will seek to shape them to their advantage. Consequently, fully understanding the effects of electoral reforms 
requires considering and analyzing their antecedents. The second tradition of electoral systems research 
addresses this by treating electoral systems and their reforms as endogenous phenomena, focusing on the 
underlying factors that drive electoral system choice. Adopting this dual perspective, the thesis is guided by 
the following research questions: 
1. How did political actors influence the reform process, what patterns emerged?
2. How did conflict patterns during decision-making affect the final reform design?
3. What is the political impact of the reforms in the cantons concerned? 
0.3 Analytical scope of the study 
The analysis focuses, although not exclusively, on Swiss cantons currently applying biproportional appor￾tionment, which are (year of enactment in parentheses): Zurich (2005), Aargau (2008), Schaffhausen (2008), 
Nidwalden (2013), Zug (2013), Schwyz (2015), Valais (2017), Uri (2019), and Graubünden (2021).7
 The first 
empirical section of the dissertation examines the reform processes in these cantons, including those where 
7
 See also Table 41 in the appendix. 

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
5 
the reform was proposed but not implemented (Obwalden, Bern, Thurgau). This part explores party posi￾tioning, conflict patterns, and the legal design of the reform policies, using a qualitative research design with 
document analysis and selective quantification of qualitative data. 
The second part of the dissertation investigates the impact of electoral law reform on various aggregate-level 
variables, such as vote-seat disproportionality, the effective number of legislative parties, voter turnout, and 
political competition. This section makes causal claims about the reform effects and isolates non-reform￾related changes through counterfactual estimation based on all cantons. Methodically, this approach roots 
in the difference-in-differences technique. This part follows a quantitative, large-N design and analyzes data 
from all cantonal parliamentary elections between 1976 and 2023. The second part is therefore a complete 
survey. Although the second empirical part includes constituency information, the data set does not provide 
constituency-level data. Consequently, certain potentially significant and insightful changes, such as strate￾gic behavior by party actors in list submissions, local voter turnout, and other relevant details, are not cap￾tured. However, collecting this information for the entire study period and across all cantons would require 
an impractically high research effort, given the limited resources available. 
0.4 Contributions 
This study offers several contributions to the field: 
First, it provides the first comprehensive analysis of the biproportional apportionment method, adopted by 
numerous Swiss cantons since the turn of the millennium to address demands for a fairer electoral system. 
The method is exclusive to Switzerland and is critically assessed in the dissertation, highlighting both its 
innovative aspects and the practical and conceptual challenges it presents. The study also includes a thor￾ough documentation of cantonal reform processes, analyzing decision-making patterns and testing hypoth￾eses to explain variations in reform designs. 
Second, the study evaluates the potential political ramifications of electoral reforms within the PR family. 
While previous research has focused primarily on transitions from majoritarian to proportional systems, 
this study explores the less examined domain of reforms within different PR systems. By leveraging the bi￾proportional method as a case study, it investigates how electoral reforms influence party competition and 
political participation, providing insights into how various PR formulas impact the political landscape. 
Third, the research employs a novel counterfactual estimation method for causal inference in time-series 
cross-sectional data to assess the effects of electoral reforms on cantonal political systems. This advanced 
methodology is designed to address challenges such as incomplete data and varying intervention timings, 
offering a more precise analysis and advancing the field by providing deeper insights into the causal rela￾tionships between electoral reforms and political outcomes. 
Fourth, the study introduces three new data sets with detailed information on cantonal elections from 1976 
to 2023. The first data set (Lüscher 2024a) provides data on party representation in cantonal parliamentary 
elections. The second data set (Lüscher 2024b) offers aggregated data on all cantonal elections, covering 
aspects such as the electoral system and characteristics of the political landscape. The third data set (Lüscher 
2024c) includes detailed information on constituency structures. While the second data set builds upon ex￾isting data with added variables, the third data set is entirely original, filling a crucial gap as, to the best of 
my knowledge, there are no similar publicly available data sets yet.

August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher

0.5 Main findings 
This dissertation examines the adoption and implications of biproportional apportionment in Swiss can￾tons, providing an in-depth analysis of its achievements, challenges, and broader consequences for electoral 
fairness. Since its initial implementation in Zurich in 2005, biproportional apportionment has constituted 
a pivotal shift in Switzerland’s electoral framework, aiming to achieve a more accurate reflection of voter 
preferences across constituencies while significantly reducing effective electoral thresholds. By enhancing 
proportionality, this reform has sought to advance Swiss elections toward a more representative and equi￾table system, particularly benefitting smaller parties that were previously marginalized under the former 
electoral rules. 
However, the adoption of biproportionality has not been without significant challenges. The allocation pro￾cess requires complex calculations. The high degree of technical complexity makes it difficult for the general 
public to understand the new method. Despite the development of tools such as the BAZI software, designed 
to facilitate the allocation process and uphold democratic scrutiny, the inherent complexity of the system 
raises concerns regarding its accessibility and the broader implications for public engagement and confi￾dence in the electoral process. Additionally, the cross-district vote tallying integral to biproportional systems 
has led to instances of discordant seat allocations, where the distribution of seats within constituencies does 
not align with actual vote counts. This misalignment can be particularly problematic when it impacts influ￾ential candidates, potentially eroding voter trust and confidence in the electoral system. 
The dissertation also provides a critical examination of the strategic behavior of political parties during the 
electoral reform process. The empirical findings largely support the hypothesis that political parties tradi￾tionally advantaged by existing electoral laws tend to resist or attenuate reform efforts, while those disad￾vantaged by the status quo advocate for more comprehensive changes. This strategic behavior underscores 
the complex dynamics of power within electoral politics, where parties maneuver to preserve or enhance 
their electoral advantages. Nonetheless, the study also identifies instances where parties deviate from ex￾pected patterns, influenced by factors such as legal imperatives, political ideals, and democratic norms. No￾tably, the research reveals that reforms imposed against the interests of advantaged parties often result in 
less distorted outcomes, indicating that political conflict, rather than consensus, can be a critical driver in 
achieving more equitable outcomes. 
In evaluating the impact of these reforms on cantonal politics, the dissertation finds that while bipropor￾tionality has significantly improved vote-seat proportionality, the anticipated secondary effects – such as 
increased voter turnout, heightened political competition, and an expansion in the number of effective po￾litical parties – have not manifested as expected. Instead, the reforms have contributed to a reduction in the 
number of electoral lists submitted, reflecting strategic adaptations by political parties. This finding under￾scores the substantial strategic implications of seemingly technical adjustments in electoral systems, as po￾litical actors navigate these changes to optimize their resources. Furthermore, the study suggests that the 
expected psychological impact on voters, a critical component of the reform’s objectives, has been limited, 
potentially due to a lack of awareness or other countervailing factors. In conclusion, while biproportional 
apportionment represents a significant advancement in the pursuit of electoral fairness in Switzerland, it 
also introduces new complexities and challenges.

August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher

0.6 Structure 
The thesis is structured into three core chapters (Chapters 1-3), each crafted to be read independently, pre￾ceded by an overarching introduction (Chapter 0). The thesis’ structure is shown in Table 2. 
Chapter 1 lays the groundwork by providing an in-depth overview of the context that led to the development 
of the new seat allocation method. It thoroughly examines the technical and conceptual foundations of the 
method, highlights its advancements, addresses a key conceptual dilemma and critically assess the limita￾tions associated with biproportional apportionment. 
Chapter 2 investigates the underlying causes of the biproportional reforms. Drawing on detailed documen￾tation of the cantonal reform processes, it explores the behavior of the involved actors and the characteristics 
of the decision-making processes, offering a cross-case analysis. This chapter delves into how partisan actors 
positioned themselves and influenced the reform process, as well as the conflict structures that emerged and 
their impact on the resulting reform designs. 
Chapter 3 shifts focus to the political implications of the new method. It examines the practical importance 
of proportionality and evaluates how and to what extent specific parameters altered by the reforms are likely 
to affect the mapping accuracy of the electoral system. The chapter concludes with the use of counterfactual 
estimation models to assess the changes the reforms have induced within cantonal political systems. 
Chapter 4 presents an overarching conclusion and provides a synthesis of the thesis’ key findings. Chapter 
5 compiles the references cited throughout the work, while Chapter 6, as the appendix, includes additional 
material and provides further context and supporting information.

1. Biproportional Apportionment: 
Context, Technicalities, Practice and Limitations 
 

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===

1.1 Introduction 
Electoral systems are sets of rules and mechanisms that govern how social needs and interests are translated 
into formal political power. As societal values and priorities evolve, so does the design of electoral systems. 
Thus, electoral reforms reflect shifts in social concepts and aim to realign the system with contemporary 
ideas and values. In Switzerland, several cantons have made a notable shift from traditional PR- and MR￾based apportionment methods to biproportionality. This change represents a significant and methodically 
unique advancement in enhancing the proportionality of vote-to-seat conversion. This chapter delves into 
the broader context of this transition and examines the implementation, practical application, and the chal￾lenges the new method presents in cantonal parliamentary elections. 
The chapter unfolds in several parts: Initially, the political and legal backdrop that led to the development 
of the new seat allocation method are discussed. This section outlines the key phases of the FSC’s evolving 
jurisprudence on electoral systems and its impact on cantonal reform efforts. Following this, the chapter 
offers a description of the new biproportional procedure, highlighting its conceptual and technical innova￾tions compared to other/earlier methods. It then explores the practical implementation of the new method, 
detailing where it has been applied and the changes it has introduced to institutional structures. The chapter 
concludes with a critical assessment of the new procedure, addressing its technical complexity and the ef￾fects of cross-district vote tallying. It explores how these factors might affect public acceptance and discusses 
potential conflicts between practical demands and the method’s intended goals. Finally, the chapter dis￾cusses the impact of additional design features, such as statutory quorums and majority clauses, within the 
context of biproportionality. It evaluates how these mechanisms, intended to mitigate political fragmenta￾tion and address representation imbalances, can conflict with the principles of pure, undistorted propor￾tional representation. 
By outlining these aspects, this chapter aims to deliver a comprehensive understanding of biproportional 
apportionment, acknowledging both its innovations and its limitations. This foundational discussion lays 
the groundwork for the subsequent sections of the dissertation (Chapters 2 and 3) by introducing the tech￾nical, practical, and theoretical dimensions of the new apportionment method. 
1.2 Balancing autonomy and equity: The Federal Court’s role in shaping cantonal electoral law 
Traditionally, Swiss cantons wield considerable political autonomy including the design of their electoral 
law. However, since the turn of the millennium, evolving legal precedents have restricted this autonomy. 
These rulings now mandate that cantonal electoral systems adhere closely to the constitutionally implied 
principle of electoral equity. This shift in case law traces back to a ruling of December 18, 2002, concerning 
the Zurich City Council elections of March 3, 2002 (BGE 129 I 185). The decision concerns a voting rights 
complaint against the election procedure for the Zurich City Council. Based on population figures, two of 
the 125 seats were allocated to the smallest constituency (Constituency 1) and 19 to the largest (Constituency 
11). The remaining seats were distributed among the other ten constituencies, with three having fewer than 
ten seats (four in Constituency 5, five in Constituency 7, and nine in Constituency 4). 
In smaller constituencies, a list needs a large(r) share of the total vote to win a seat. The apportionment 
method used requires approximatively 1/(M + 1) of the vote, where M is the district magnitude in terms of 
seats. For example, Constituency 1 with two seats requires 33% of the vote to secure a seat, whereas Constit￾uency 11 with 19 seats only requires about 5%. This discrepancy contributed significantly to the Court’s 
ruling. The Court criticized that voters do not contribute to the election results evenly due to significantly 

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
10 
varying district sizes. The Court’s scrutiny was guided by constitutional principles of democratic equity and 
proportional representation, as articulated in Articles 8 and 34 of the Federal Constitution. According to 
the Court’s interpretation, these articles guarantee that all citizens have the right to equal political participa￾tion and representation, ensuring that electoral systems do not favor or disadvantage any political group 
based on the size or location of their electoral base. The Court partially upheld the complaint and declared 
Zurich’s constituency division unconstitutional (cf. Pukelsheim & Schuhmacher 2004). This marks the first 
of four turning points in the FSC’s dynamic case law on cantonal PR-based electoral systems (cf. Grossen￾bacher 2018: 5-7): 
First turning point – the ten percent rule: In its ruling, the FSC established a precedent by declaring that 
electoral thresholds exceeding 10% are incompatible with the principles of proportional representation en￾shrined in the Swiss Constitution.8
 Additionally, the size of each constituency must not vary by more than 
one-third from the average constituency size in the canton. The Court emphasized that high thresholds 
undermine the fundamental rights of voters and violate the principle of electoral equity, which forms the 
bedrock of democratic governance. As a result of this landmark decision, the Court mandated cantonal 
governments, including Zurich, to reform their electoral systems to rectify these disparities. The ruling set 
a precedent for electoral reforms across Switzerland and prompted legislative action to ensure that electoral 
districts are configured in a manner that respects federal law. 
The Court instructed the city of Zurich to implement corrective measures but did not prescribe a specific 
method. In its statement, it suggested two options: a new constituency structure or the formation of constit￾uency associations. Subsequently, the canton of Zurich mandated the mathematician and statistician Frie￾drich Pukelsheim to devise a comprehensive and minimally invasive solution for all parliamentary elections 
in the canton. He proposed a new method and termed it “biproportional divisor method with standard 
rounding”. 
Second turning point – admissible exceptions to the rule: Although the FSC stressed that any votes must 
have approximatively the same chance of success, i.e. of being effective, it also indicated in related rulings 
that this standard is not absolute. The Federal Constitution does not mandate a “pure” PR system. However, 
any deviation from this principle requires convincing objective justifications. Historically, from at least 1993 
to 2014, the FSC deemed deviations from the equality of electoral success and proportional representation 
principles (i.e., surpassing the “ten percent rule”) justified if they resulted from small constituencies based 
on traditional territorial organizations (BGE 131 I 85, BGE 136 I 352, BGE 136 I 376, BGE 140 I 107). Such 
constituencies could be considered unique due to historical, federalist, cultural, linguistic, ethnic, or reli￾gious reasons, thereby warranting minority protection and representation, even at the expense of optimal 
proportional representation. The stronger the constituency’s identity, the more justified the deviation from 
axiomatic principles (BGE 129 I 185, BGE 131 I 74). 
Third turning point – abrogation of the exemption clause: Over time, the FSC increasingly argued that al￾ternative allocation methods like the biproportional apportionment method or constituency associations 
could realize proportional representation without altering traditional constituencies. This change of course 
became particularly clear in the decision of February 12, 2014, regarding constituency divisions in Valais 
(BGE 140 I 107). The FSC affirmed that through the biproportional apportionment method or constituency 
associations, a reasonably accurate reflection of party strength in parliament could be achieved, even in the 
8
 These 10% equate to nine seats when applying the formula Threshold = 1/(M + 1). 

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
11 
presence of small constituencies. The Court declared that if a canton opted not to use these corrective 
measures, small constituencies could no longer be justified under the principle of proportional representa￾tion, despite any historical or cultural significance. Essentially, the 2014 decision marked the Court’s termi￾nation of exceptions allowing traditional constituencies to compromise proportional representation, favor￾ing instead methods that maintain the integrity of constituencies while ensuring proportional outcomes (see 
also BGE 143 I 92). 
Fourth turning point – expansion of equity requirements to non-PR systems: In its decision of September 
26, 2014, regarding the parliamentary electoral system of Appenzell Ausserrhoden, the FSC clarified that 
Article 34 of the Federal Constitution mandates electoral equity across all systems – proportional, majori￾tarian, or mixed (BGE 140 I 394). While acknowledging that majoritarian systems may not fully achieve 
equality of electoral success, the Court recognized their validity under specific conditions. This expansion 
of equity requirements means that even in non-PR systems, the electoral processes must strive for fairness 
and equal representation, thus broadening the scope of constitutional mandates in cantonal electoral law. 
It should be noted that the case law of the FSC, even though it accounted for cantonal particularities, at least 
until 2014, also caused discord. Some cantons felt affronted by the Court’s decision, viewing it as an illegit￾imate and intrusive encroachment on their autonomy. This unease led the cantons of Zug and Uri to each 
submit a cantonal initiative in 2014, aiming to explicitly enshrine cantonal sovereignty over electoral law in 
the Federal Constitution. While both initiatives were ultimately rejected, they show that the judicial direc￾tives were not only met with approval on part of the cantonal authorities and legislatives. 
As evident, the FSC played a pivotal role in driving electoral reform processes in the cantons. However, 
alongside this legal debate about cantonal electoral law, there is also a political dimension. The push for 
reform originates from a broader public dissatisfaction with traditional, unidimensional PR, coupled with a 
growing demand for electoral processes to be “fair”, i.e., to generate politically unbiased results. As the Court 
highlighted, a constituency structure composed of small and variably sized districts hinders a proportional 
reflection of voters and their preferences in terms of seats and, resulting in a non-negligible portion of 
vote(r)s being ineffective and unrepresented. This is particularly (but not exclusively) evident in cantons 
with a highly fragmented electoral structure, characterized by a significant number of single-member or 
small multi-member districts. It is not implausible to assume that the Court’s response is, at least to some 
extent, influenced by this public discontent. 
1.3 How biproportional apportionment innovates conventional PR methods 
To recapitulate: the core problem lies in a constituency structure characterized by numerous small districts, 
i.e. less than nine seats, and a high variance in district magnitudes, resulting in uneven voting power for 
voters depending on the constituency in which they are registered. Coupled with the Hagenbach-Bischoff 
apportionment formula, which systematically disadvantages smaller parties in favor of larger parties 
through the rounding off of fractions in determining (integer) seat entitlements, this setup means that small 
parties and their voters in small constituencies face lower chances of winning seats compared to a) larger 
parties in the same constituencies and b) small parties with identical voter shares in larger constituencies. 
This effect is amplified across the entire electoral area due to the district-based allocation of seats to parties. 
Friedrich Pukelsheim proposed a solution that innovates traditional apportionment methods by optimizing 
the vote-seat proportionality across the entire electoral area while retaining the existing constituency struc￾ture. Two changes are at the core of this approach: firstly, splitting the seat allocation procedure into two 

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
12 
parts, and secondly, adopting the politically unbiased Sainte-Laguë allocation formula, which uses standard 
rounding for non-integer seat entitlements. The new procedure ensures that voters wield equal influence 
on the election result, irrespective of their constituency or the party they support, thus promoting fairness 
and equity in representation. Because the allocation of seats is based on both the territorial distribution of 
the electorate and the distribution of party votes (simultaneously rather than successively), the new method 
is termed bi-proportional. 
The two-step allocation process represents the centerpiece of the new method. Initially, seats are allocated 
to parties proportionally based on their total votes across the entire electoral area, a step known as “upper￾tier apportionment” or “super-apportionment”.9
 This means if a party garners an overall vote share of 8%, 
it ideally secures about 8% of the available parliamentary seats. Given that voters in different districts wield 
varying numbers of votes corresponding to district size, standardizing these voting scales is crucial to ensure 
equal voting power (Pukelsheim 2017: 261). By shifting the initial level of seat allocation from individual 
districts to the entire electoral area and by applying the unbiased Sainte-Laguë formula, disparities between 
a party’s vote share and its seat share are minimized, effectively neutralizing the distorting impact of small 
electoral districts.10
In the next step, the overall votes obtained by the parties get “re-distributed” or “sub-apportioned” to the 
parties at district level and must cumulatively satisfy the following conditions: 
(i) The total seats allocated to a party across all districts match its total number of seats. 
(ii) The total seats allocated to all parties in a district meet the district’s size. 
(iii) The total seats allocated across all districts match the overall number of party seats. 
(iv) Proportionality is ensured among parties within each district and among districts within each party. 
While the first three conditions pertain to the general setup of apportionment, the fourth condition adds 
proportionality as a criterion. The difficulty of allocating seats proportionally among different groups or 
constituencies based on multiple criteria simultaneously is known as the “biproportional apportionment 
problem” (Ramírez et al. 2008, Maier et al. 2010, Demange 2011, Serafini & Simeone 2012). Solving this 
problem manually is challenging, thus using a divisor-based iterative algorithm is typically recommended 
for efficiency.11 The algorithm aims to identify divisors for lists and districts that best meet condition (iv) 
while satisfying conditions (i)-(iii).12
However, achieving perfect two-dimensional proportionality remains elusive. Divisor and multiplier meth￾ods cannot ensure district-level proportionality unless there are only two parties and two districts. This lim￾itation sometimes leads to inconsistencies in specific districts, where the ranking of seat shares does not 
9

required to secure a seat (approximately ½ × votestot/seatstot). However, this threshold is considerably lower com￾pared to PR systems that allocate seats at the district level.

align with the ranking of vote shares, thereby violating criterion (iv). Such district-specific disproportional￾ities are commonly termed “discordant seat allocations” or “seat reversals”, as described in detail by Maier 
et al. (2010: 375) and will also be addressed further in this first part.13 Essentially, they are a consequence of 
a prioritization of achieving optimal overall proportionality over ensuring within-district proportionality. 
But how significant is the impact of the apportionment formula on the election outcome? To illustrate the 
effect, I use the 2016 Cantonal Council elections in Schwyz as a case study, where the new biproportional 
procedure was applied for the first time. To highlight the effect, I compare the actual results with the hypo￾thetical/counterfactual results using the previous Hagenbach-Bischoff formula. However, it is important to 
note that the counterfactual modeling of the election results according to Hagenbach-Bischoff has been 
simplified, abstracting from more complex circumstances. For instance, it does not assume any apparente￾ments within the constituencies. Additionally, the hypothetical model does not account for how voters and 
parties might adapt their behavior to the incentives of the electoral system, which, very probably, affects the 
election outcome. Furthermore, the results are not presented at district level.

Overall, the change in formula, holding all else constant, ac￾counts for eight seat shifts. It is also evident that electorally dominant parties, specifically the SVP and CVP,

caused by the apportionment formula. 
But why should a parliament ideally mirror the party preferences of the population? And, provocatively, 
why do we even need constituencies? The next chapter revisits the fundamental theoretical concepts under￾pinning modern notions of political representation and discusses how biproportionality relates to these 
concepts.
