1.4 Representing whom or what? 15
 
Contemporary theoretical thinking on political representation is strongly influenced by the notion that par￾ties act as the main link between the public and the political sphere (Pitkin 1967, Manin 1997, Rehfeld 2005, 
Dalton et al. 2011, Golosov 2017). The centrality of parties for the establishment and functioning of demo￾cratic institutions has been emphasized by various political theorists: Max Weber referred to parties as “the 
children of democracy” (1990: 35), John Aldrich described them as “endemic to democracy” (1995: 3) and 
for Richard Katz, party government is “a synonym for representative democracy” (1987: 2). However, the 
contemporary notion that parties are the main vehicle for formal political representation is historically quite 
young. 
1.4.1 Territorial roots of modern representative government 
Before the advent of parties in modern mass democracies, individual legislators located in small territorial 
constituencies served as primary linkages between the public and political decision-makers (Dalton 1985: 
268-271, Pukelsheim 2017). The principle of territoriality, which finds expression in personalized elections 
within politically confined single-member constituencies, forms the conceptual origin of modern repre￾sentative government (Urbinati and Warren 2008: 389). Democracy is organized and conceived as a local 
event, and voters are represented by an elect from their own “natural community of interest” (Golosov 2017: 
119). Grounded in this territorial understanding of political representation, a long-lasting debate has raged 
over whether representatives should act as delegates or trustees (Pitkin 1967, Dalton 1985, Dovi 2009). This 
controversy was sparked by Edmund Burke’s famous and disputed publication Reflections on the Revolu￾tion in France (1790). 
In both models, constituency representation is realized by an act of authorization by the constituents at the 
outset of the election. This authorization enables the representative to act “[on] behalf of, in the interest of, 
as the agent of, someone else” (Pitkin 1967: 113). However, the models place conflicting demands on the 
legislative behavior of a representative, resulting in different degrees of political agency. The delegate model 
of constituency representation requires legislators to mirror the expressed policy preferences of their con￾stituents. This leaves delegates no room for autonomy from their constituents. Their authority to act as rep￾resentatives is limited to making the represented literally present again. Such notions of binding political 
allegiance found application in the Saddle period in parts of France, the United States, and beyond in the 
form of instructions, pledges, imperative mandates, and the practice of discretionary revocability of repre￾sentatives (recall) (Manin 1997: 163-167). 
The trustee model, in contrast, requires representatives to make decisions based on their judgment and in 
accordance with their own views of right action. Within the limits of authorization, the trustee enjoys full 
autonomy even if they act against the interests of the constituents. This does not mean that the representa￾tive is not held accountable for their actions. Voters still have the right not to reelect the representative. 
Apart from that, they should not be able to exert any influence on the trustee (Dovi 2009: 4-6). In Hanna 
Pitkin’s account (1967), these two models of constituency representation should not be conceived as mutu￾ally exclusive. Instead, she suggests accepting the paradoxical nature of political representation and striving 
to safeguard both the autonomy of the representative and the autonomy of the represented. 
15 Parts of this subchapter have previously appeared in a similar form in a conference paper (Lüscher 2021). 

=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
15 
Regardless of which model for describing citizen-elite relations seems empirically more accurate or appears 
favorable from a normative stance, they both point to the territorial patterns of early representative democ￾racy. Electoral processes took place within territorially confined spaces and individual legislators established 
a corridor of formal representation between the constituents and the representative institutions of the polity. 
1.4.2 Parties as the pillars of modern democracy 
The national and industrial revolution at the end of the nineteenth century triggered a profound transfor￾mation of Western European politics. New political cleavages emerged, dividing society into different camps 
(Lipset & Rokkan 1967). At the same time, suffrage was extended to the working class and other segments 
of society. This dramatically changed the composition of electorates (Dalton 1985). The introduction of 
proportional representation resulting from mounting demands of these new voter segments for political 
recognition was another cornerstone that followed. All these episodes of modern democracy formation gave 
rise to the successful establishment and proliferation of mass parties. 
While political representation was long guaranteed by and through local representatives, this attachment 
has lost importance over time and was largely substituted by political parties (Golosov 2017: 119). Parties 
offer voters manifestos with distinct policy goals. Voters, in turn, opt for those parties that best fit their policy 
preferences. This shift illustrates how the logic of electoral choice has moved from the direct nomination of 
a candidate to a comparative assessment of abstract party manifestos. Voters now think of formal political 
representation primarily in programmatic terms. Moreover, evaluations of the fairness of electoral systems 
are usually measured by the congruence of voters and parties in seats, ideology, policy preferences, or other 
indicators (Cincea 2013: 178-179). Thus, parties did not only monopolize political power; they also deeply 
affected how we think about politics, political representation, and political equity. 
However, the territorial dimension of political representation is not a relic of the past. It still effectively 
shapes electoral processes today. Some countries, such as the United States or Great Britain, have never 
switched to proportional representation but still adhere to majoritarianism. In their candidate-centered vot￾ing systems, the principle of territoriality still prevails. Bicameral parliamentary systems devote a separate 
chamber of the national legislature to the territorial dimension of political representation. Additionally, 
countries such as Germany, New Zealand, Croatia, Albania, and Hungary have mixed their electoral systems 
by combining proportional with majoritarian or plurality voting elements to account for both dimensions 
of representation (Bochsler 2010a: 86-131). 
1.4.3 The challenge of simultaneously meeting requirements of “both worlds” 
The principle of territoriality persists subtly even within proportional electoral systems. Most democracies 
conduct elections at the district level, both nationally and sub-nationally, with few exceptions where legis￾lative representatives are appointed through at-large elections. The purpose of electoral districts extends 
beyond organizational efficiency. They provide local populations with representation in the legislative 
body.16 However, districts inherently conflict with the party dimension of political representation, often im￾peding fair, proportional representation, particularly for smaller parties. Research has consistently shown 
16 Even in countries like Israel and the Netherlands, where the entire nation serves as a single electoral district, the 
principle of territoriality remains important. Studies and polls indicate that geographic considerations continue to 
influence both voters and representatives, highlighting the ongoing importance of the territorial dimension of politi￾cal representation even in the absence of specific electoral regulations and incentives (Nagtzaam & Louwerse 2023). 

=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
16 
that the more parcelled an electoral area (i.e. the smaller the average electoral district), the greater the diffi￾culty for small parties to pass electoral thresholds, resulting in disadvantages for them and their electorates 
(Duverger 1954, Rae 1967, Sartori 1976, Taagepera & Shugart 1989, Lijphart 1994). Following a more radical 
notion of electoral equity, voters not only deserve equality but also have the right to equal representation. 
This implies that their votes must be converted into seats with equal chances of success – a normative re￾quirement incompatible with small districts in district-based elections. 
In summary, electoral districts remain the primary geopolitical units for parliamentary seat allocation in 
modern democracies. From a theoretical perspective, districts embody the principle of territoriality, crucial 
to the genesis of modern democracy. However, they pose obstacles to achieving perfect party representation, 
sometimes leaving significant portions of the electorate unrepresented. There are three main solutions to 
this theoretical puzzle, differing in radicality: 
˗ Abolish electoral districts and introduce a single constituency: This approach eliminates the territorial 
component and paves the way for a purely proportional representation system. However, it could neglect 
local interests and undermine the principle of territoriality. 
˗ Mix the electoral system: Combining proportional representation with majoritarian or plurality ele￾ments can address both dimensions of political representation (Shugart & Wattenberg 2001). Examples 
include the electoral systems in Germany, New Zealand, and Hungary, which balance local representa￾tion with overall proportionality. 
˗ Introduce a new apportionment method that reconciles the two antithetical concepts of representation: 
The biproportional apportionment method offers a novel solution. It ensures equitable representation 
across both political and geographical divisions. This method, implemented in several Swiss cantons, 
aims to reconcile the conflicting demands of party and territorial representation with minimal changes 
to the electoral system. 
The main analytical focus of this dissertation is on the third solution, although the other solutions are also 
discussed peripherally. The next chapter examines the spatial and temporal adoption of biproportionality 
and assess whether the diverse responses of the cantons to the FSC’s ruling align with the criticized aspects, 
particularly the small-scale and heterogeneous nature of constituency division. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
17 
1.5 Biproportional apportionment in practice 
The cantons’ responses to the new judicature by the FSC varied greatly. For instance, political stakeholders 
in the Canton of Zurich, the first affected, generally responded positively and pragmatically to the ruling. In 
contrast, a majority of political stakeholders in the Canton of Uri initially resisted the verdict and only re￾luctantly acknowledged the need for reform. However, it must be noted that cantonal political systems, de￾spite many overlaps and similarities, exhibit significant differences and unique characteristics both politi￾cally and culturally. Smaller, catholic, and traditionally conservative cantons in the original part of Switzer￾land maintain a distanced relationship with the Confederation and its institutions. This is evident in histor￾ical voting patterns, such as the fact that the founding cantons of Switzerland (along with Appenzell Inner￾rhoden and Valais) rejected the Federal Constitution of 1848 and the two total revisions of 1874 and 1999 
(Kley 2023). These cantons were correspondingly critical of the Court’s rebukes. 
Detailed analyses of individual cantonal reform processes (see Chapter 2) clearly show how closely cantons 
monitored both the dynamic case law and the responses of the reprimanded cantons. The media also played 
an important role, not from a legal perspective but in informing the public, shaping public opinion, and 
contributing to a discourse on electoral law. Over time, the issue became politicized as various parties ad￾vanced their agendas. This underscores the importance of viewing biproportional reforms as an outcome of 
a triangular interaction between legal jurisdiction, the media public sphere, and political decision-makers. 
The debut of the refined method took place in February 2006 in the election of the council of the City of 
Zurich. One year later, the new method had its premiere in the Canton of Zurich (2007). Eight cantons 
followed suit: Schaffhausen (2008), Aargau (2009), Nidwalden and Zug (2014), Schwyz (2016), Valais 
(2017), Uri (2020) and most recently Graubünden (2022). As of 2023, a total of 25 elections have been con￾ducted using the new method. Figure 1 illustrates the evolution of the application of different PR-based 
apportionment formulae in Switzerland from 1979 to 2023. 
Figure 1: The cantonal application of various apportionment formulae between 1979 and 2023. 

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
18 
Since 1979, there has been a steady increase in the adoption of proportional representation for cantonal 
parliamentary elections, gradually replacing majority voting systems. From the turn of the millennium on￾wards, biproportionality primarily replaced the single-proportional Hagenbach-Bischoff method. The only 
exception to this pattern is the Canton of Graubünden, which transitioned directly from majoritarianism to 
biproportionality. This shift underscores the broader movement towards more equitable and representative 
electoral practices in Switzerland. Figure 2 illustrates the spatial dimension of biproportionality in the Swiss 
cantons as of 2023. 
Figure 2: Spatial dissemination of biproportional apportionment in Switzerland. 
Implementation of electoral reforms among reformed cantons varies significantly. While some have com￾pletely foregone introducing electoral hurdles that distort proportionality, the majority have implemented 
statutory quorums at either the constituency or cantonal level, or both (refer to Table 41 in the appendix). 
Additionally, the Cantons of Zug, Schaffhausen and Graubünden have included a “majority clause” in their 
electoral law, ensuring that the party with the most votes in a district secures at least one seat, regardless of 
district size. This clause resembles the revised German federal election model and applies only if the total 
cantonal seat count is not exceeded (no overhang and leveling seats). The goal is to guarantee that the lead￾ing party in the numerous single-member districts always secures a seat. However, this intervention also 
comes with implications, which are discussed in detail in the subsequent chapter on the limitations of the 
new procedure. Furthermore, in Uri, the new procedure applies exclusively to PR constituencies. Non-PR 
constituencies continue to vote under MR. 
After nearly two decades of practical experience with the new allocation procedure prompted by FSC inter￾ventions, do we see evidence of reduced electoral thresholds resulting from changes in constituency divi￾sions? Figure 3 illustrates that the cantons have responded to the FSC’s mandates, directly or indirectly, 
resulting in a reduction of electoral hurdles that (implicitly) distort PR. This is evident in the significant 
decrease in the share of districts with fewer than nine seats (biproportional districts excluded). Since the 
1980s, the proportion of small intra-cantonal constituencies in Switzerland has decreased significantly. 
Whereas this proportion used to be over a third of all cantonal constituencies, it has gradually declined to 
less than 10%. Conversely, this indicates that there are still cantons where the constituency structure for 
cantonal parliamentary elections does not comply with federal law. The figure on the right, concerning the 
effective electoral threshold, illustrates the reduced systemic hurdles that electoral systems impose on parties 

=== PDF PAGE 30 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
19 
aiming to gain parliamentary representation. While this value peaked in the mid-2000s, it has since halved 
to around 4%. 
Figure 3: Left panel: Proportion of constituencies for PR-based cantonal parliamentary elections with fewer than nine 
seats (annual cantonal averages). Right panel: Effective electoral threshold (annual cantonal averages). 
These consistent declining trends over the past two decades are attributable to a variety of factors, not solely 
biproportional reforms. They also reflect territorial reforms and other adjustments to the electoral systems. 
Nonetheless, they clearly demonstrate how the Swiss cantons have collectively responded to the guidelines 
set by the FSC. 
1.6 All that glitters is not gold – biproportional apportionment and its limitations 
The previous chapters have demonstrated that biproportionality mitigates some of the disadvantages inher￾ent in unidimensional apportionment methods such as the Swiss Hagenbach-Bischoff method. Chief 
among these is the equalization of the effective voting power of voters of different parties in different con￾stituencies. This leads to a more precise representation of voters’ political preferences and eliminates the 
previous disincentive for those inclined to support fringe parties from abstaining. Additionally, implement￾ing biproportionality does not require a change in the constituency structure. Therefore, can biproportion￾ality be considered the pinnacle of electoral systems, reconciling both the territorial and political dimensions 
of electoral politics? 
While biproportionality undoubtedly represents an attempt at optimization, the biproportional allocation 
method, like any other, comes with certain limitations. Electoral systems incorporate various principles, 
ideals and norms, including representation and minority protection. While territorial representation is most 
common, electoral systems also represent specific segments of society and the ideas, values, and ideals of 
parties. Other principles include governability, accountability, preventing excessive fragmentation and po￾larization, and ensuring simplicity, comprehensibility, and transparency in the conversion from votes into 
seats (Linhart et al. 2019: 23). Since not all principles can be maximized concurrently, trade-offs become 
inevitable (cf. Federal Chancellery 2013: 30). In the following sections, I will examine the most significant 
limitations, including some that have been previously discussed in the literature and others that have not 
yet been explored. 

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
20 
1.6.1 Technical complexity 
One often-cited disadvantage of the new procedure, raised by both election experts and practitioners, is its 
technical complexity (see e.g. Demange 2011: 17-20). Optimally translating votes into seats both across the 
entire electoral area and within individual constituencies involves many calculation steps, especially for the 
distribution of seats within constituencies. The sub-allocation must always consider the applicable rounding 
rule and adhere to the prespecified marginals, i.e. the number of seats attributed to each constituency and 
the total number of seats for a party (Pukelsheim 2017: 264). Since adjusting the allocation in one constitu￾ency requires adjustments in others, an iterative algorithm is needed to efficiently navigate the complex 
calculations needed to resolve the allocation problem. 
The BAZI software, specially programmed for the biproportional procedure, presents matrices with the 
quotients used for seat allocation within constituencies. These quotients are published by the responsible 
electoral authority along with the election results, enabling third parties to understand and verify the results. 
This transparency is crucial for democratic legitimacy and the acceptance of the election results. 
However, critics argue that an election procedure should not be so complex that calculating the result re￾quires specialized IT and expert knowledge. Despite this criticism, elections based on unidimensional PR 
are not necessarily less complex. For instance, apparentements, previously allowed in most cantons that 
transitioned to biproportionality, are among the most intricate elements of electoral systems. Before an elec￾tion, it is impossible to predict the extent of their impact, or which allied party will benefit. Additionally, the 
distribution of seats within apparentements or sub-apparentements is unlikely to be familiar to the “average 
citizen”. The fact that other apportionment methods or specific aspects of them can be similarly complex 
somewhat mitigates the criticism of excessive complexity of biproportional apportionment. 
1.6.2 The pitfalls of cross-district vote tallying 
The purpose of biproportional apportionment is clearly defined: to ensure that voters impact election results 
with nearly equal weight and similar chances of success, regardless of party preferences or constituency of 
residence. Biproportional reforms are thus rooted in a strong, radically democratic, voter-centered principle 
of equality. Biproportionality achieves this by tallying the votes across all constituencies to determine par￾ties’ seat entitlements. This imitation of a canton-wide constituency in the upper-tier allocation of seats to 
parties ensures that (1) all votes are accounted for in the vote tally, minimizing the proportion of “weightless 
votes”, i.e., residual votes resulting from the rounding down of quotients and (2) that votes are mapped onto 
seats as proportionally as is empirically possible. However, the artificial expansion of the electoral area cre￾ates three side effects worthy of discussion. 
1.6.2.1 Violations of within-district proportionality 
One such issue is the phenomenon of “discordant seat allocations”, which occurs when “the ranking of seat 
numbers within a district deviates from the ranking of vote numbers” (Maier et al. 2010: 375). In the sub￾apportionment of seats to the parties within districts, it might occur that a party receives fewer seats than 
another party in the same district despite having more votes. Another scenario is that within a party, a list 
of that party in a district may receive more seats than the same list in another district despite having fewer 
votes proportionally. If the party with the most votes is affected, this is referred to as the “strongest seat 
discordant” (ibid. 2010: 386). These discordant seat assignments or “seat reversals” are an inevitable by￾product of the biproportional apportionment problem. To achieve optimal overall proportionality, it may 

=== PDF PAGE 32 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
21 
be necessary to sacrifice optimal proportionality within individual districts. Maier et al. who evaluated these 
discordant assignments, laconically described them as “the price one has to pay to achieve proportionality 
over the whole electoral region” (ibid. 2010: 386). 
In his monograph, Friedrich Pukelsheim (2017: 264) wrote that “discordant seat assignments are rare due 
to the excellent votes-seats image that is accomplished by double proportionality”. However, various anal￾yses, including his own from dealing with the Zurich Cantonal Council elections 2011 present a different 
perspective: “In the 2011 elections to the Zurich Cantonal Council, which were conducted on the basis of 
double proportionality, there are numerous discordant seat allocations” (Pukelsheim & Schuhmacher 2011: 
1591, translated from German).17 Similarly, the study by Maier et al., published a year prior, clearly illustrates 
that such seat reversals are not uncommon.18 The frequency of districts affected by these reversals typically 
ranges between one and four per election, contingent on institutional factors, including the electoral system 
(Maier et al. 2010). Presenting an unpublished paper, Irina Lehner evaluated biproportional elections in the 
Swiss cantons and found that, on average, approximately every fifth district is affected by reversals.19 But 
why should we be concerned about these seat reversals at all? 
From a broader perspective, distortions in local proportionality may seem a minor trade-off for enhancing 
overall proportionality across the electoral region. However, from the voter’s point of view, these distortions 
may raise concerns about the integrity of elections. In Switzerland, parliamentary elections are typically free 
list proportional elections. Thus, they entail a personal vote component, allowing voters to modify the pre￾determined party lists by crossing out, cumulating or adding candidates from all available lists.20 This means 
that party votes also reflect voter preferences for specific candidates. Therefore, if in a constituency a popular 
candidate from the leading party loses to an outsider from another party due to a seat reversal, this outcome, 
despite its technical correctness, carries significant weight for both the candidate involved and the affected 
electorate. 
This is why three cantons have co-implemented a “majority clause”, ensuring the party with the most votes 
in a constituency gets at least one seat. While this addresses the most blatant cases of proportionality viola￾tions within districts, it does not resolve the underlying problem. The clause merely relocates reversals from 
smaller to larger constituencies. Inevitably, incompatibilities persist and require compensation in the form 
of reversals. This poses a political dilemma regarding where this compensation should occur. 
1.6.2.2 Implications for territorial representation 
The cross-district vote tallying introduces a lesser-known side effect that carries significant implications. In 
the process of super-apportionment, votes first become effective at the superordinate level. To aggregate 
these votes effectively, each party’s vote count in constituencies is normalized by dividing it by the number 
17 Pukelsheim qualifies this observation by noting that similar deviations can occur in single-proportional elections 
under the Hagenbach-Bischoff system. However, applying the Hagenbach-Bischoff formula, these discordant seat 
assignments are not immanent to the apportionment method, but instead attributable to the feature of apparente￾ments. Therefore, this comparison is not fully convincing. 
18 Cf. Figure 3 (Maier et al 2010: 386). 
19 Presented at the Conference “Die verflixte Arithmetik des Verhältniswahlverfahrens” on June 17, 2022, hosted by 
the Center for Democracy in Aarau. 
20 Electoral authorities also provide a blank ballot paper, where voters can write in the names of their preferred candi￾dates. The number of pre-printed candidate lines matches the number of seats available in the corresponding constit￾uency. 

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
22 
of seats attributed to that constituency (based on population figures). This normalization approximates the 
number of voters supporting each party, which is then aggregated at the cantonal level. 
However, this approach, aimed at equalizing the influence of voters, renders the geographic origin of votes 
irrelevant in determining parties’ seat entitlements. While this might appear fair and desirable at first, it 
becomes problematic when considering that constituencies with higher voter turnout and a greater propor￾tion of eligible voters exert a disproportionately larger influence on the final electoral outcome than constit￾uencies with lower participation rates. Although constituencies are upheld as a pillar of territorial represen￾tation, they play no role in the process of vote aggregation. This highlights a key aspect of the new seat allo￾cation procedure: while constituencies retain a fixed seat entitlement based on census data, this entitlement 
is effectively undermined by cross-district vote tallying. Constituency electorates contribute to the election 
result not with the weight of the constituency’s share of seats but its share of votes (cf. Lüscher 2023). 
1.6.2.3 Persisting flaws in legislative representation 
A further implication of the biproportional apportionment method relates to the equality of voting power 
and is closely linked to the effect previously described. This principle asserts that voters across the entire 
electoral area should ideally affect the composition of the parliament with the same weight. This requires a 
consistent ratio between the number of inhabitants and the number of seats in a constituency, known as the 
“representation figure”, throughout the electoral area. This ensures that the parliament accurately represents 
the population. 
Interestingly, the FSC has historically paid relatively little attention to this aspect in its case law on cantonal 
electoral systems, seemingly considering it secondary in the evaluation of an electoral system’s equality/pro￾portionality. This is despite the fact that the requirement was explicitly mentioned in the Court’s landmark 
2002 judgment concerning the City Council elections in the Canton of Zurich and reiterated in all subse￾quent judgments. It was not until the case involving the Canton of Graubünden (BGE 145 I 259) that the 
FSC examined this aspect in detail. This case involved the small municipality of Avers, with a Swiss resident 
population of 160 at the time. Due to statutory guarantees, forming its own constituency, the municipality 
was assured a seat in parliament, despite the cantonal representation figure being 1342. The discrepancy, 
with Avers’ population being around one-eighth of the cantonal representation figure and one-fourth of the 
next smallest municipality, indicated substantial distortions in the equality of voting power. This distortion 
played a crucial role in the FSC’s considerations and the eventual annulment of Graubünden’s electoral sys￾tem. But how does this relate to biproportional reforms? 
The Canton of Graubünden switched from a majoritarian system to biproportionality in compliance with 
the FSC ruling. But what has actually changed in the Avers electoral district as a result of the reform? Because 
votes cast in Avers are primarily effective at the cantonal level and only subsequently at the constituency 
level, voters in Avers now have significantly less influence on the overall allocation of seats. Before the re￾form, they contributed around 0.83% to overall result.21 In the first post-reform elections in 2022, this con￾tribution dropped to approximately 0.16%.22 This shift optimized the equality in voting power, aligning it 
more consistently across constituencies. However, the representation figure for Avers has remained nearly 
21 Corresponding to a constituency weight of 1/120. 
22 Corresponding to a voter number weight of 81/51714 (cf. Canton of Graubünden 2022). 

=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
23 
the same, and due to a population decline by December 31, 2020, the parliamentary overrepresentation has 
even increased post-reform. 
In traditional unidimensional PR elections, there is a parity between the electoral area and the representative 
body: elections occur within constituencies, and elected representatives mirror their constituency in its po￾litical composition. Voters’ contributions to the overall result are proportionate to the constituency’s weight. 
However, in biproportional elections, this clear parity is somewhat blurred. Constituencies remain, but 
votes are primarily allocated at the upper-tier level before being considered at the lower-tier level. This sys￾tem aims to optimize equality in voting power and the efficacy of votes, but it leads to a conflation of political 
spaces with implications that extend beyond solving an allocation problem. 
1.6.3 Statutory quorums – a step back? 
In six of the nine cantons that transitioned to the new method, statutory quorums were established either 
concurrently with the reform or shortly thereafter. These quorums aim to prevent alleged excessive frag￾mentation of the party system, which, according to their proponents, could hinder the efficiency of council 
operations and complicate the process of reaching compromises.23
The introduction of quorums can be attributed to the first biproportional election in the canton of Aargau 
in 2009. This election, conducted without a threshold, resulted in four small parties – the BDP, EDU, GLP, 
and SD – gaining seats in the Aargau cantonal parliament for the first time. Much to the dismay of the 
established parties, who also feared that the new system would lead to further fragmentation of political 
forces (at their cost). Consequently, a quorum was introduced for the subsequent 2012 elections following 
a successful referendum, setting a precedent for other cantons. Although not all reforming cantons adopted 
a quorum, the issue was thoroughly debated in all legislative processes. 
However, such quorums are fundamentally at odds with the system’s intent. If the reform aims to optimize 
the accuracy of vote-seat conversion, as guaranteed by “pure”, i.e., undistorted biproportionality, any polit￾ical intervention that artificially diminishes this accuracy is detrimental to the goal. There are alternative 
methods to ensure efficiency in parliamentary operations – if this is the genuine concern: The key units of 
action in parliaments are parliamentary groups, which, in the Swiss context, generally require a minimum 
of five members. This requirement serves as a de facto quorum. Since parliamentary groups can include 
members from different parties, there is little justification for a priori excluding fringe parties from parlia￾mentary participation and representation based on statutory quorum requirements. 
Against this backdrop, increasing political fragmentation of legislatures can be viewed as not problematic 
in itself but rather a reflection of socio-political changes being mirrored in the party system due to the high 
permeability of the electoral system. A diverse party landscape is symptomatic of a diversification of political 
demands among voters, coupled with inclusively designed electoral rules. If a parliament should act as a 
mirror of society, a key principle of any PR-based electoral system, then deliberately obstructing an accurate 
translation of this diversity lacks theoretical justification. 
23 As will be demonstrated in Chapter 3, there is no evidence that the reform has led to party system fragmentation. 
Although there is a gradual inter-cantonal trend towards increased fragmentation, there is no indication that this 
trend is driven by biproportional reforms. However, it should be noted that statutory quorums in most reformed 
cantons might mitigate any potential positive effects.

=== PDF PAGE 35 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
24 
As previously mentioned, majority clauses are another alien element designed to prevent discordant seat 
assignments in very small constituencies. These reversals can be politically sensitive and are thus transferred 
to larger constituencies where their impact is less significant (and less apparent). Although these clauses 
follow a majoritarian logic and interfere with unaltered biproportionality, they only affect sub-apportion￾ment and do not compromise overall proportionality. Therefore, they are not considered a limitation here. 
1.7 Conclusion 
This chapter aimed to delineate the theoretical framework and practical backdrop of the biproportional 
apportionment method applied in nine Swiss cantons. Since its first introduction in Zurich in 2005, bipro￾portionality has marked a significant shift towards a more representative electoral system in Switzerland. 
The reform has made strides in improving electoral fairness. By addressing some of the drawbacks of previ￾ous systems, biproportionality has enhanced the proportionality of election outcomes, ensuring that voter 
preferences are more accurately reflected across different constituencies. The notable reduction in effective 
electoral thresholds and the decline in constituencies with fewer than nine seats underscore the reform’s 
success in fostering a more representative political environment. 
Yet, biproportionality is not without its challenges. The complexity of the allocation process – requiring 
calculations and iterative adjustments – has introduced a level of technical difficulty that can undermine 
transparency and accessibility. Despite the advancements provided by tools like the BAZI software, which 
facilitates the allocation process and promotes democratic scrutiny, the inherent complexity of the system 
remains a concern, particularly in its impact on public understanding and engagement. 
The cross-district vote tallying inherent in biproportional systems brings its own set of complications. Alt￾hough designed to enhance overall proportionality, the method can lead to discrepancies known as discord￾ant seat allocations. Such distortions occur when seat distributions within constituencies do not align with 
the actual vote counts, potentially undermining voter confidence, especially when influential candidates are 
affected. While majority clauses in some cantons attempt to address these issues, they merely transfer the 
distortions to larger constituencies. Although they address some of the more glaring issues of seat reversals, 
they often shift the problem rather than resolving it comprehensively. 
Moreover, the impact on territorial representation presents another critical issue. By aggregating votes 
across constituencies, the geographic origin of votes becomes less relevant in determining seat allocations, 
which can diminish the role of individual constituencies in the electoral process. This approach, aimed at 
balancing voter influence, risks eroding the direct link between local representation and overall electoral 
outcomes. 
The introduction of statutory quorums in several cantons further complicates the picture. These mecha￾nisms, intended to prevent excessive fragmentation and ensure effective governance, distort the very pro￾portionality that biproportionality seeks to achieve. While quorums, such as those introduced in Aargau 
following the 2009 elections, aim to stabilize the party system, they can compromise the accuracy of vote￾to-seat translations. 
In conclusion, while biproportional apportionment represents a significant leap forward in the pursuit of 
electoral fairness and proportionality, it also comes with its own set of challenges. The complexities of the 
system, the issues related to cross-district vote tallying, and the implications for territorial representation 

=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
25 
highlight the ongoing difficulties in optimizing an electoral system that balances fairness, representation, 
and practical governance. 
The potential for manipulation during reform processes must also be acknowledged. Electoral reforms are 
susceptible to political interference, as those who legislate the changes are also the ones primarily affected 
by them. This dual role provides incentives for parties to shape electoral laws to their advantage. Thus, to 
fully understand the design and effects of these reforms, it is essential to consider the interests and actions 
of the actors involved in the decision-making process, which will be addressed in the next part.

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
26 
Part II 
*** 
2. Biproportional Reforms and Their Origins: 
A Process-Oriented View on Reform Designs 
 

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
27 
2.1 Introduction 
There is a long and far advanced tradition in electoral systems research devoted to the study of the political 
effects of electoral rules (Duverger 1954, Taagepera & Shugart 1989, Cox 1990, Sartori 1997, Farrell & Gal￾lagher 1998, Grofman & Lijphart 2003, Gallagher & Mitchell 2005, Klingemann 2009, Nohlen 2014 among 
others). In recent years, there has been a resurgence of interest in a field that can be denominated as the 
“politics of electoral reform”. Instead of adhering to the traditional Duvergerian notion that essentially views 
electoral systems as determinative for the structures of a political system, this newer perspective suggests 
that understanding electoral institutions and reforms, along with their effects, requires considering the po￾litical configurations and strategic calculations of relevant party actors that have shaped them (Lundell 2005: 
6-10, Charvát 2023: 15-18). Accordingly, the analytical focus lies in identifying and understanding the po￾tential factors and mechanisms that trigger, facilitate, and motivate sudden changes in the “rules of the 
game” (cf. Cusack et al. 2007, Calvo 2009, Riera 2013, Leemann & Mares 2014, Walter & Emmenegger 2019). 
Drawing on this body of literature, the objective of this part of the thesis is to dismantle dynamics and pat￾terns and shed a light on the different pathways that led to the adoption of biproportional apportionment 
in several Swiss cantons. Drawing on recent literature examining the adoption of PR in Swiss cantons during 
the late 19th and early 20th centuries, I develop a theoretical framework to explain the various paths to and 
different faces of biproportional reform in Switzerland. 
Despite the successive establishment of the biproportional method as an alternative to (or supplement of) 
conventional, unidimensional apportionment formulae in Switzerland, there is notable variation in the spe￾cifics of individual reform designs across cantons. While some cantons have introduced artificial barriers to 
mitigate “undue” fragmentation of the party system, others have incorporated additional provisions to ac￾commodate cantonal peculiarities. Evidently, these differences did not emerge randomly, but are the result 
of lengthy and often highly complex and non-linear political decision-making processes. However, the dif￾ferent outcomes cannot exclusively be ascribed to the involved decision-makers. They also mirror distinct 
cantonal trajectories of electoral system evolution and diverging ideas about which goals and ideals the can￾tonal electoral system should serve and which standards it should meet. Moreover, the differences must also 
be viewed in the broader legal and political context, whose dynamic nature adds to the complexity. Hence, 
numerous factors are assumed not only to influence the outcome of the reform but also the specifics of its 
resulting design. The following research questions are addressed in this part of the thesis: (1) What conflict 
patterns surfaced during the decision-making processes, and (2) how did these patterns impact the resulting 
reform design? 
I argue that conflict patterns were predominantly influenced by parties’ self-interest in maximizing their 
seat shares. Parties anticipating a potential decline in their electoral status due to the reform are expected to 
resist or aim to mitigate its impact, while those poised to benefit are expected to advocate for more substan￾tial changes. Additionally, I argue that reform processes characterized by conflict yielded less distorted re￾form designs compared to those following a consensual pathway. 
The chapter is structured as follows: After this introduction, the subsequent chapter presents a theoretical 
framework on the basis of which the hypotheses are derived. This is followed by the specification of variables 
and discussion on data and methodology. The following chapter provides a comprehensive, process-ori￾ented documentation, covering successful reform processes as well as cases where reform attempts were 

=== PDF PAGE 39 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
28 
either rejected after a lengthier process or at an early stage of the decision-making process.24 Subsequently, 
I consolidate the findings and contrast them with the assumptions to disentangle the links between conflict 
patterns during decision-making, partisan strategizing, and the variations in biproportional reform designs. 
2.2 Taking a step back – the prosaic rationale behind electoral reforms 
When Arend Lijphart (1995a: 412) succinctly stated that “if one wants to change the nature of a particular 
democracy, the electoral system is likely to be the most suitable and effective instrument for doing so”, he 
highlighted two key aspects of an electoral system. First, he emphasized its significance in shaping the nature 
of democracy, and secondly, he pointed to its susceptibility to manipulation. Given the awareness among 
party strategists and electoral engineers regarding the political implications of electoral law, they are inher￾ently incentivized to customize electoral regulations in harmony with their interests (Kedar et al. 2016). 
Consequently, understanding of the effects of such reforms requires a thorough examination of their deter￾minants (Riera 2013). 
One of the most momentous political renovations in the early 20th century was the adoption and successful 
dissemination of electoral systems belonging to the PR family (Grofman & Lijphart 2003, Calvo 2009). 
There is no doubt that contemporary party systems belonging to this family would look radically different 
today had this change not taken place. To carry the thought a bit further, it can also be argued that a wide 
range of political outcomes (social spending, social welfare, inequality, etc.) can at least to some extent be 
linked to these reforms and their repercussions, directly or indirectly. Recent comparative research has thus 
intensely focused on unravelling the motives behind such electoral reforms (Leemann & Mares 2014). 
Historically, the adoption of PR intertwined closely with the expansion of suffrage, particularly to previously 
disenfranchised groups like workers and women. These expansions mirrored societal changes and, in turn, 
led to the emergence of new political cleavages and parties. In 1970, Stein Rokkan presented two comple￾mentary hypotheses aimed to dissect the origins of PR adoption, proposing the “minority protection hy￾pothesis” and the “socialist threat hypothesis”. The former posits that in culturally, ethnically, and denomi￾nationally diverse European nations (he mentions Denmark, Switzerland, Belgium, Moravia, and Finland), 
majority elections risked disrupting the political system. To fortify territorial unity within these diverse and 
politically fragile settings, the incorporation of measures to protect minority interests served as a strategic 
mechanism preserving the system’s continuity (Rokkan 2009: 157). Conversely, the latter contends that the 
ascent of the working class before and during World War I posed an existential threat to established parties, 
prompting them to confederate with the working class and other proponents of PR to safeguard their polit￾ical dominance. Though these hypotheses reflect different social dynamics, both underscore mounting pres￾sures that weakened the resistance of established parties, primarily driven by the prosaic motive of preserv￾ing their political power. 
Rokkan’s hypotheses did not lose popularity since his (re)formulation. Quite on the contrary, numerous 
recent studies inquiring the origins of proportional representation build upon his theoretical legacy and 
refine it with new empirical data (Calvo 2009, Ahmed 2013, Leemann & Mares 2014, Walter & Emmenegger 
2019 to mention only a few). While most of these studies investigate why reforms towards PR occurred, 
Walter and Emmenegger (2019) approached the subject from a new angle, considering how these reforms 
24 When the term “successful” is used to describe a reform process, it carries no inherent judgment. It does not imply 
desirability of the outcome but rather indicates that the reform has been effectively implemented. This interpretation 
remains consistent throughout the entire work.

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
29 
were conducted – with or against the support of advantaged parties – and the degree of systemic distortions 
they yielded. They postulated that when PR adoption had the support of advantaged parties, resulting re￾forms exhibited higher vote-seat disparities. On the contrary, reforms forced upon these advantaged parties 
led to more proportional electoral systems (ibid. 2019: 66). Applying these hypotheses to Swiss cantons, 
their findings turned Rokkan’s minority protection hypothesis upside down, suggesting that PR adoption 
with cross-party consensus wasn’t primarily about minority inclusion but rather about safeguarding the 
political interests of parties benefiting from the prior electoral law.25 These findings align with research on 
the nexus between party systems and electoral systems. In his study analyzing 219 elections across 87 coun￾tries since the 19th century, Colomer (2005) concludes that party systems are not simply determined by 
electoral rules, as prominently suggested by Maurice Duverger. Rather, political parties often chose electoral 
rules that solidified or strengthened existing party configurations. His findings underscore how political 
parties strategically utilize electoral rules as instruments to achieve benign political outcomes. 
While the study of Walter and Emmenegger (2019) centered on transitions from majoritarian to propor￾tional representation in Swiss cantons since 1890, the aim here is to adapt their theoretical framework to 
transitions towards biproportionality since its initial adoption in Switzerland in 2005. However, the contexts 
in which unidimensional and bidimensional proportionality were adopted are vastly different. The social 
dynamics underpinning the party system in contemporary Switzerland deviate considerably from those of 
the late 19th and early 20th century. Consequently, Walter and Emmenegger’s socialist threat hypothesis isn’t 
pursued in this analysis. Their modified minority protection hypothesis, however, remains a viable explan￾atory framework applicable to more recent electoral reforms, I argue. 
The biproportional reforms in Switzerland are set within a specific legal context. Notably, the FSC’s land￾mark decision in 2002 prompted PR cantons with small districts to adapt their electoral laws.26 Moreover, 
the presence of direct democracy as a means of bottom-up mobilization and radical transformation emerges 
as another pivotal contextual factor. Walter and Emmenegger (2019) highlighted the crucial role of direct 
democracy in PR adoption, particularly in cantons where staunch resistance from beneficiaries of the status 
quo thwarted reform efforts. In circumstances where conventional approaches proved ineffective, direct 
democracy emerged as the final recourse for instigating change. According to the authors, this conflictual 
path towards proportional representation, against resistance from parties benefiting from the former ma￾joritarian rules, led to the establishment of electoral systems with lower levels of distortion (ibid. 2019: 67). 
In light of this context, I argue that direct democracy emerges as a historical continuum in Swiss history, 
playing a pivotal role in both “waves of proportionalization”. Splitting Walter and Emmenegger’s “majority 
protection” argument into two parts, the first part (Hypothesis 1) emphasizes conflict patterns in the deci￾sion-making process, while the second part (Hypothesis 2) addresses the design of reform policies. 
H1: Parties previously favored by electoral law tended to oppose or dilute reform during the decision￾making process, while potential reform beneficiaries aimed for the most radical reform. 
25 The widespread reception of Rokkan’s minority protection hypothesis, interpreting the inclusion as an act of altru￾ism of traditional elites to protect local minorities, is one way of reading him. Strictly speaking, he does not elaborate 
the motives of incumbents to improve minority representation. In my interpretation, traditional parties in multicul￾tural contexts had vital, self-directed interests to move away from MR when political upheavals were a likely scenario. 
26 However, the Court did not say how the requirement of nine seats per districts should be met. Extending the sizes 
of districts or legislatures or even introducing a single constituency (as, for example, the Canton of Neuchâtel did) are 
alternative routes to meet the requirement. 

=== PDF PAGE 41 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
30 
H2: Reforms enforced against the will of advantaged parties (either through partisan opposition or pop￾ular initiatives) resulted in less distorted policies compared to reforms arising from cross-party con￾sensus. 
2.3 Variables, data and methods 
This section delineates the variables, data sources, and methodological approaches employed to test the hy￾potheses 1 and 2. 
Since the biproportional method is only implemented in Switzerland, the analysis concentrates on the nine 
cantons where this method is currently in use (ZH, SH, AG, NW, ZG, SZ, VS, UR, GR). In the initial part of 
the analysis, which examines the extent of parties’ strategic maneuvering (Hypothesis 1), three additional 
cases where attempts to adopt biproportionality were unsuccessful are included (OW, BE, TG). This expan￾sion not only increases the case count but also allows for hypothesis testing regardless of the reform outcome 
(successful reform versus reform rejection). However, Graubünden as a former majoritarian canton is ex￾cluded from testing Hypothesis 1, and Valais, being a highly unique case that defies comparison for several 
reasons, is also omitted.27 The subsequent part, investigating the link between conflict structures during re￾form processes (Hypothesis 2) and the reform design, is limited to the nine cantons where reform adoption 
was successful. 
The relatively small sample size restricts the viability of a quantitative, large-N research design. Instead, a 
qualitative examination of cantonal reform processes is conducted. This offers a nuanced understanding of 
the factors and complexities driving the individual reforms. An in-depth description of the biproportional 
reform processes follows in the next chapter. These case descriptions then also provide the data source for 
testing the hypotheses. This approach aligns with the methodology adopted in analogous studies and ad￾heres to the recommended research practices for studying electoral reforms (Charvát, 2023: 21): 
“The politics of electoral reform (...) aims to test the relationship between the party and electoral systems in 
the broader analytical and contextual framework, also including the process of selecting and changing elec￾toral institutions by existing political actors, in particular political parties. As a result, the politics of electoral 
reform is studied as a complex process, which is determined and shaped by a range of factors, e.g., historical, 
cultural, institutional or contextual. It cannot be understood without a previous analysis of the mechanisms 
by which these factors interact, and which then lead to specific outcomes (...). Studies on electoral reforms are 
therefore not largely affected by statistical data, focusing rather on a detailed process tracing, and primarily 
consist of an in-depth examination of a small number of cases. Hence, qualitative analyses appear more ben￾eficial, while quantitative studies remain rather an exception to this approach.” 
Hypotheses 1 and 2 explore common conflict patterns during decision-making processes and their influ￾ence on the design of reforms. Hypothesis 1 suggests that support for reform (y) primarily depends on par￾ties’ self-directed utility expectations (x). Parties previously favored by electoral laws are likely to use their 
political leverage to either block reform altogether or shape it to their advantage (e.g., by advocating for legal 
thresholds), while those disadvantaged are expected to push for the most radical reforms possible. In con￾trast, Hypothesis 2 focuses on the nature of the reform design itself, positing that reforms enacted without 
the support of previously advantaged parties (x) result in less distorted outcomes (y) compared to those 
achieved through cross-party consensus. Data for this section of the thesis, with the exception of Table 30 
27 Detailed explanations for the omissions can be found in the case descriptions and in the evaluation section. 

=== PDF PAGE 42 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
31 
regarding the (dis)proportionality of cantonal parliamentary elections where pre-existing data sets were 
used, was independently collected and quantified or visualized as appropriate. 
The testing of the first hypothesis follows a multi-step analytical strategy: 
1. Estimate the parties’ positions on the reform based on their legislative (mis)representation prior to 
the reform (i.e., their presumed positions). 
2. Collect data on the parties’ actual positions regarding the reform as a whole or specific components 
of it (i.e., their true positions). 
3. Compare the presumed and true positions for each party to determine if there is alignment and 
assess whether parties actively attempted to influence the electoral law to their advantage. 
Before examining whether historically privileged parties show greater resistance to biproportional reform 
compared to those previously disadvantaged by electoral regulations, it is crucial to establish clear criteria 
for identifying a party as a “profiteer” or a “victim” of the existing electoral law. A party is considered a 
beneficiary when its seat share consistently exceeds its vote share. Conversely, when a party’s seat share is 
significantly and consistently lower than its vote share, it is disadvantaged by the electoral system. Given the 
volatility of elections, it is assumed that the direction, magnitude, and consistency of legislative misrepre￾sentation influence a party’s self-assessment as a “winner” or “loser”. Therefore, the classification of a party 
is based not on the most recent election before the reform process, but on the last four to five elections. 
When trends are weak (i.e., minor vote-seat differences) or inconsistent over time, making it difficult to 
estimate a party’s stance, the label “unclear” is used.28
In the next phase, the parties’ true stances on electoral reform are determined. To uncover these positions, 
content analyses of plenary minutes are conducted, specifically using valence analysis (Budge et al. 2001, 
Krippendorff 2004, Mayring 2010).29 Valence analysis is a content-based method that scales relevant text 
components according to two or three levels (Mayring 2010: 15f.). Each cantonal reform process is exam￾ined by systematically gathering the positional data of the involved parties (pro/contra/neutral). When 
available, voting tables are utilized to simplify position attribution. In other cases, plenary minutes are ana￾lyzed, and when debates were only available as audio, transcriptions are produced. 
The third step involves testing the first hypothesis. If the assumed and actual party positions align, it sup￾ports the notion that party stances on reform are primarily driven by self-directed utility expectations, aim￾ing to maximize their seat share. However, the diverse affiliations of parliamentary groups add complexity, 
as these often vary between cantons due to the heterogeneity of cantonal party systems. For instance, some 
cantons have multi-partisan groups, such as the Greens and the SP forming a joint group in Uri due to their 
relatively low seat count. To maintain comparability and coherence while addressing cantonal peculiarities, 
the mapped positions are shown for individual parties wherever feasible. The results are presented in a table. 
The second hypothesis explores the relationship between conflict patterns in the decision-making process 
and the final reform design, specifically focusing on the level of distortion within the reform. Building on 
Walter and Emmenegger (2019: 65f.), I hypothesize that reforms enacted despite opposition from previ￾ously favored parties or through direct democratic means will contain fewer distortive elements benefiting 
28 Consistent legislative underrepresentation = assumed reform support; consistent legislative overrepresentation = 
assumed reform rejection; weak or inconsistent trend in legislative misrepresentation = unclear assignment. 
29 A compilation of all primary sources consulted during this step can be found in Table 40 in the appendix. 

=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
32 
these privileged parties. Conversely, if a reform results from cross-party consensus, this consensus is likely 
achieved through concessions as part of political horse-trading, leading to a final reform design with ele￾ments that distort optimal proportionality. 
The analysis centers on the extent to which the final reform design is distorted. Existing literature on the 
selection of electoral systems during the initial “wave of proportionalization” views electoral distortions as 
deviations from ideal vote-seat proportionality. These distortions are influenced by factors such as the total 
number of mandates, constituency characteristics (size and shape), artificial electoral thresholds, and the 
number and strength of competing parties/lists (Taagepera & Shugart 1989, Boix 1999, Colomer 2007, 
Carey & Hix 2011). Various metrics have been developed to quantify these disproportionalities, including 
empirical measures like the Least Squares Index (Gallagher 1991) and the Effective Threshold proposed by 
Taagepera (1998), which assesses the “mechanical permeability” of electoral systems. While these metrics 
are useful for cross-sectional and longitudinal comparisons across different electoral environments, they 
offer limited insights into the specific mechanisms generating distortions. Additionally, metrics such as the 
Least Squares Index are sensitive to contextual factors not directly related to electoral design, such as the 
number of competing parties or the size of the parliament. Therefore, they do not isolate the effects of indi￾vidual reform policies effectively. Given these limitations, I opt for a qualitative assessment of distortion for 
each case, rather than relying on a statistical formula that may include significant extraneous information. 
In biproportional reform designs, distortions in vote-seat proportionality can arise from various elements, 
including local and global quorums, either separately, in conjunction, or in disjunction, as well as other 
features adversely affecting proportionality. The assessment of distortion in reform designs uses a scoring 
system (see Table 4). Points are allocated as follows: 
- 1 point for a statutory quorum of 5% or less. 
- 2 points for a statutory quorum exceeding 5%. 
- 2 points for designs incorporating a subdivision of the electoral area for super-apportionment. 
- 2 points for designs that include or maintain majoritarian elements, such as plurality elections in 
specific electoral districts. 
The total score is used to measure the overall distortion of the reform design, categorized into a four-level 
hierarchical gradation. 
Score(s) Distortedness
0 absent 
1 low 
2-3 moderate 
4-6 high 
Table 4: Scoring system for the assessment of the distortedness of a reform design. 
This classification precedes an evaluation of the reform process, distinguishing between conflictual pro￾cesses (involving opposition from advantaged parties or direct democracy) and consensual processes 
(achieved through cross-party agreement). A subsequent comparison assesses whether the final reform re￾flects the conflict structures observed during the reform process. The second hypothesis is confirmed if there 
is a consistent link between the conflict structure and the degree of distortion in the final reform design. 
Results from these analyses are presented in tabular form.

2.4 Successfully reformed cantons 
2.4.1 Canton of Zurich 
The Canton of Zurich, located in northern Switzerland, 
is a critical hub of political activity and innovation, char￾acterized by its high degree of urbanization and signifi￾cant economic and cultural influence. Its diverse politi￾cal landscape, encompassing a wide range of political 
parties, non-partisan groups, and social movements, re￾flects the canton’s complex and dynamic social struc￾ture. Zurich’s prominence in the national context often 
positions it as a trendsetter in political developments 
and a birthplace for new political parties. 
As the most populous canton in Switzerland, Zurich 
hosts the country’s largest cantonal legislature, with 180 seats. The SVP holds the relative majority of these 
seats, followed by the SP and the FDP (see Figure 4). Elections are conducted across 18 electoral districts 
using a biproportional representation system, a method Zurich was the first canton in Switzerland to imple￾ment. This system was introduced following a 2002 voting rights complaint concerning the election process 
for Zurich’s City Council, which led the FSC to partially uphold the complaint. The Court criticized the 
existing electoral districting as unconstitutional due to the small size of certain districts, a decision that has 
had lasting implications (BGE 129 I 185). 
Figure 4: Parties’ seat-shares in parliamentary elections in the Canton of Zurich, 1979-2023. 
The issue identified in the Court’s ruling was that, in smaller districts with high electoral thresholds, the 
principle of vote equality was compromised. Votes for smaller parties that did not secure a mandate in their 
district were effectively “weightless” and excluded from the final election results. The Court established a

34 
maximum threshold of 10% for securing a 
seat within a district, corresponding to a 
minimum of nine seats per district. How￾ever, the Court did not prescribe specific 
measures to achieve this, leaving the politi￾cal authorities with the discretion to devise 
appropriate compensatory measures. In re￾sponse, the city of Zurich expanded the size 
of its electoral districts for municipal elec￾tions. This step, however, might have been 
unnecessary given that the biproportional 
apportionment procedure subsequently de￾veloped was specifically designed to address 
disparities in electoral success probabilities 
due to varying district sizes. 
Simultaneously, the Cantonal Council initi￾ated a comprehensive revision of the can￾tonal law on political rights (KR-No. 4001a), 
which also governs the electoral provisions 
for all city parliaments within the canton, including the Zurich City Council.30 In response to the Federal 
Court’s ruling in late 2002, the Cantonal Council decided in April 2003 to separate the politically sensitive 
issue of electoral districting from the original legislative proposal.In a subsequent session, the Council opted 
to maintain the existing division of electoral districts while revising the seat allocation procedures under the 
new political rights law (KR-No. 4001c). The new procedure, based on biproportionality, was developed to 
ensure equal voter efficacy and was ultimately adopted. 
The reform was debated in two key sessions, on September 1, 2003, and November 17, 2003. Remarkably, 
the commission’s draft was widely accepted within the Cantonal Council, with broad cross-party support 
not only for the necessity of electoral reform but also for the introduction of the biproportional method (see 
Figure 5). The prevailing view was that the existing electoral system leaves a substantial portion of the elec￾torate underrepresented. The new method, while a relatively modest adjustment to the existing electoral 
framework, significantly enhances overall proportionality and eliminates the need for apparentements, 
which had been a source of criticism. These alliances, formed for strategic rather than substantive reasons, 
were seen as obscure tools of electoral competition. As Green MP Daniel Vischer observed, “Until now, 
apparentements were a necessary evil, a political nonsense, which will be rendered obsolete by the new 
method.” 31
30 According to §111 GPR ZH, a slightly simplified apportionment procedure applies for elections to single-district 
city parliaments. For multi-district city parliaments, however, cantonal provisions apply analogously (with the excep￾tion that they are free to deviate from the quorum regulation, see §111(4) GPR ZH).

The introduction of an electoral threshold, however, sparked a contentious debate. Five different models 
were proposed and rigorously discussed: 
1. No quorum. 
2. A local 3% quorum. 
3. A local 5% quorum, with an alternative global 2% quorum. 
4. A local 5% quorum (as recommended by the commission). 
5. A local 5% quorum combined with a global 3% quorum. 
This debate was marked by a thorough examination of the potential electoral implications of each model. 
Smaller parties and the Greens advocated for a system with minimal barriers to entry, supporting models 1, 
2 and 3. They argued that the primary advantage of the new biproportional method was its ability to trans￾late voter preferences into seats with minimal distortion. Introducing a quorum, they contended, would 
undermine the proportionality and representational accuracy of the new system. Concerns were also raised 
that any form of quorum would particularly disadvantage smaller groups, including youth parties, especially 
if apparentements were abolished. Despite these arguments, models 1 and 2 were eventually rejected. 
Larger parties, by contrast, argued for the necessity of electoral thresholds to prevent further fragmentation 
of the party system and to restrict the entry of small, single-issue groups.

The FDP’s divided stance on the issue is particularly noteworthy. While the majority of the party supported 
the commission’s model (model 4), a minority favored eliminating the quorum entirely, arguing instead for 
a 3% local threshold. The CVP initially backed model 4 but was persuaded by Martin Bäumle (GPS) to sup￾port model 3, which he argued was necessary to avoid discrimination against small parties with dispersed 
electorates. Bäumle’s model, which required exceeding either a 5% local or 2% global threshold, did not gain 
majority support. The SVP then introduced a stricter model 5, which required parties to meet both the local

and global thresholds, making it the most restrictive option. Ultimately, model 4, endorsed by the dominant

The issue of the electoral threshold resurfaced in 2016 when MPs Claudio Schmid (SVP) and Martin Romer 
(FDP) proposed replacing the local 5% quorum with a canton-wide 3% threshold (KR-No. 110/2016). The 
proponents argued that this change would prevent the entry of small parties with limited cantonal relevance, 
thereby enhancing the efficiency of the council. The Government Council, however, recommended main￾taining the existing system. The responsible commission proposed a compromise solution, combining both 
thresholds in an “either-or” clause, identical to the ones applying in the cantons of Aargau and Zug. This 
proposal aimed to reduce electoral barriers for parties with dispersed electorates, counter to the original 
proponents’ intent. The Cantonal Council adopted this compromise on November 30, 2020 (KR-No. 
110a/2016), and the new regulation came into effect on July 1, 2021. 
Although the reform was subject to a facultative referendum, no popular vote was held. Nonetheless, as part 
of the 2005 revision of the cantonal constitution, voters approved two amendments related to the Cantonal 
Council’s election (Art. 51(2-3) KV/ZH), thus indirectly legitimizing the new electoral system. 
In 2021, the Cantonal Council addressed an error in the upper-tier seat allocation process. It was noted that 
rounding voter numbers during super-apportionment could result in slight distortions in seat distribution 
(KR-No. 118/2018). The Council unanimously agreed that super-apportionment should be based on un￾rounded voter numbers, with rounding applied only to the final quotient.

2.4.2 Canton of Aargau 
The party system in the Canton of Aargau closely mir￾rors the national political landscape of Switzerland, with 
nearly identical parties represented in both the cantonal 
and national parliaments, and their relative strengths 
similarly aligned. Electoral trends in Aargau also 
strongly reflect those observed at the national level. As 
illustrated in Figure 6, which details the parties’ seat 
shares over time, Aargau’s party system is notably frag￾mented. Two dominant parties occupy the extremes of 
the political spectrum: the SVP and the SP, the latter in 
coalition with the Green Party. The canton’s 140-mem￾ber parliament is elected based on biproportionality, a 
reform that places Aargau alongside the Canton of 
Schaffhausen in Switzerland’s second wave of adopting biproportional representation. 
Aargau stands out for its vigorous pursuit of electoral reforms over a relatively short period. The implemen￾tation of biproportional apportionment must be understood within this broader context of institutional re￾formism, which encompasses both intra-cantonal vertical and inter-cantonal horizontal dimensions. Table 
9 summarizes the various reform steps undertaken during this period.

The period of heightened reform activity 
began in May 2003, when the size of the can￾tonal parliament was reduced from 200 to 
140 members. This reduction was the result 
of a successful popular initiative by the FDP 
titled “Slimming down the Grand Council!” 
The initiative, motivated by the desire for a 
“lean, efficient, modern, and cost-effective 
parliament” (as quoted in the official voting 
documents), passed after previous parlia￾mentary efforts to achieve the same had 
failed. However, reducing the size of the 
parliament inevitably led to higher electoral 
thresholds in smaller districts, a conse￾quence that was well anticipated by policy￾makers. To mitigate this, a second constitu￾tional amendment was proposed, allowing 
for the merger of small districts. The pro￾posals for constitutional revision were ap￾proved by a majority of voters, with 63.8% in 
favor. Despite this, the representatives chose not to exercise the option to merge smaller electoral districts, 
effectively accepting a violation of the principle of equality in electoral success, which is only tolerated in 
“objectively justifiable cases” according to legal jurisprudence. As a result, the reform led to electoral thresh￾olds exceeding 10% in the districts of Laufenburg, Muri, and Zurzach, marking a significant setback in terms 
of electoral equity. 
To address the challenges posed by the reduction in parliament size while still complying with the Federal 
Constitution, the cantonal government proposed two models to the Grand Council. The first model envi￾sioned merging districts, while the second proposed changing the apportionment method, as suggested by 
Friedrich Pukelsheim. Ultimately, the Grand Council decided to retain the existing regulations and refrain 
from changing the apportionment method. Nonetheless, the Grand Council pursued further electoral re￾forms, comprising three smaller revisions – none of which addressed the core issue of high district-induced 
electoral thresholds.33 This legislative package passed a popular vote in September 2004, with 74.7% approval. 
Just a month later, in October 2004, the FSC issued a verdict on a constitutional complaint filed by the Green 
Party and other co-petitioners from Aargau (BGE 131 I 74). The complaint challenged the inequalities in 
voting effectiveness caused by the small size and significant variance in district sizes, a status quo that alleg￾edly favored larger parties at the expense of smaller ones. The Court upheld the plaintiffs’ position and man￾dated the creation of a “constitutional electoral order” for future parliamentary elections, laying the ground￾work for subsequent reforms. 
33 The proposed changes included a switch from a list-based to a candidate-based voting system (1); a new method 
for allocating remainder seats that enables parties without a full seat entitlement to participate in the process (2) and 
a ban of candidate cumulations on pre-printed lists (3) (source: official voting documents).
