In September 2007, after two intense readings, the Grand Council decided to introduce biproportional ap￾portionment. Figure 7 shows the party positions on the final reform bill, revealing that a razor-thin majority 
(66:63, with 1 abstention and 10 absences) voted in favor of the bill, which included both an amendment to 
the law and the constitution. The figure also shows an unexpected coalition formed by the left (SP, GPS) and 
center parties (CVP, EVP) against the right-wing parties (FDP, SVP). 
Figure 7: Party positions on the final reform bill to introduce biproportional apportionment for parliamentary elec￾tions in the Canton of Aargau. The were no differences in the voting pattern between the legislative vote and the con￾stitutional vote. 
Contrary to what the final vote results might suggest, the reform was only marginally contentious during the 
plenary sessions, making it difficult to discern the exact motives behind the parties’ (often unexpected) po￾sitions. The most heated debates centered on the implementation of electoral thresholds and the potential 
for apparentements within the same party family. One faction argued that quorums are fundamentally in￾compatible with the principle of proportionality. A second faction was willing to accept quorums as part of 
a political compromise, while a third viewed them as a necessary institutional safeguard. This third camp 
argued that quorums prevented the emergence of extremist or single-issue groups, “lone wolves”, and also 
mitigates party fragmentation, thereby enhancing legislative efficiency, cost-effectiveness, and governability. 
During the debate on the introduction of electoral quorums, a related discussion emerged concerning 
whether political parties should be allowed to engage in internal apparentements (e.g., Young Socialists 
aligning with the SP). Some parliamentarians argued that, if quorums were to be established, allowing ap￾parentements would be prudent as it would facilitate the entry of youth parties into the parliament through 
electoral cooperation with their parent party. Additionally, this mechanism could provide a way for groups 
and parties that do not meet the quorum to circumvent it effectively. Ultimately, the Grand Council re￾frained both from establishing quorums and from establishing apparentements. 
However, the debate on quorums resurfaced following the first election held under the new electoral law on 
March 9, 2009. On June 1, 2010, the FDP submitted a motion to introduce a local 5% quorum, citing con￾cerns that a proliferation of political parties would reduce the Council’s efficiency. The Government Council 
countered with a proposal for a combined quorum: either 5% within a single district or 3% across all districts. 
In the final vote on June 7, 2011, the Grand Council endorsed the government’s combined quorum proposal 
by 68 to 53 votes (with 2 abstentions and 17 absences). Except for the Social Democrats, the largest parties 
voted in favor of the amendment, while smaller parties opposed it (see Figure 8). Notably, the CVP, which 
had previously opposed quorums, shifted its position and supported the introduction, tipping the balance. 
The amendment to the law was approved by 58% of voters on November 7, 2011.

2.4.3 Canton of Schaffhausen 
Despite the relatively small size of Schaffhausen’s can￾tonal parliament, with only 60 members, it exhibits con￾siderable party diversity and a highly competitive elec￾toral environment, reflecting a vibrant democratic cul￾ture. The two dominant parties in this context are the 
SVP and SP, with other significant parties including the 
FDP, GLP, GPS, and the “Alternative Liste” (AL), a pro￾gressive left-wing party active across northwestern Swit￾zerland and included in the FGA group. Legislative elec￾tions in Schaffhausen are held within districts under a 
proportional representation system, with seats allocated 
through the biproportional apportionment method. 
Alongside the Canton of Aargau, Schaffhausen was part 
of the second wave of national reforms introducing biproportional representation, following Zurich. Figure 
9 illustrates the seat shares of all parties contesting for the cantonal legislature between 1976 and 2020. 
Figure 9: Parties’ seat-shares in parliamentary elections in the Canton of Schaffhausen, 1976-2020.

August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher

The initial conditions in Schaffhausen 
closely mirrored those in Aargau. The 
FSC’s landmark decision concerning the 
constitutionality of Zurich’s City Council 
elections in 2002 was pivotal, emphasiz￾ing that electoral districts of varying sizes, 
particularly very small ones, must adhere 
to legal equality only if these districts rep￾resent units with a shared sense of be￾longing, whether for historical, cultural, 
linguistic, or other significant reasons. 
Concurrently, a popular initiative titled 
“60 members of the Cantonal Council are 
enough!”, launched in November 2003 
and approved by voters in August 2004, 
reduced the Cantonal Council’s size from 
80 to 60 members, with 70.2% voter sup￾port. These two factors created significant 
pressure for electoral reform. 
The arguments for reducing the size of the cantonal legislature in Schaffhausen paralleled those in Aargau, 
with proponents suggesting that a smaller assembly would enhance efficiency and reduce costs, as demon￾strated in similar reforms in Vaud and Lucerne. However, it was acknowledged that such a constitutional 
revision would not only require changes to parliamentary procedures but also necessitate a comprehensive 
overhaul of the electoral system. Two reform paths were outlined: one involving district mergers (for allo￾cation purposes) and the other focusing on biproportional apportionment. 
Following the plebiscite on reducing the cantonal parliament’s size and the submission of a legislative initi￾ative, the cantonal government proposed a reform draft. Consultation with stakeholders revealed broad 
support for biproportional apportionment, leading the Government Council to dismiss alternative reform 
models and advocate solely for the biproportional method, a decision endorsed by the “special commission” 
appointed by the government. 
The Cantonal Council discussed various reform concepts in two separate sessions. Plenary minutes indi￾cated a broad, cross-party consensus on the need for electoral reform, though disagreements persisted on 
the design of the reform. A majority favored the biproportional model, considering it the most suitable so￾lution for ensuring accurate voter representation while preserving the existing district structure. A minority, 
primarily from the right-wing SVP, proposed a less extensive regionalized model that merged small districts 
rather than simulating a single constituency, as in biproportional representation. The SVP opposed the bi￾Cornerstones during the reform process in the Canton of Schaffhausen August 29, 2004 A popular initiative to reduce the size of parliament from 80 to

proportional model, arguing it could produce anomalous results, reduce parliamentary efficiency, under￾mine regional representation, and centralize election campaigns, thereby threatening regional party sec￾tions. They preferred a model addressing the issue of small districts through specific mergers.34
A secondary discussion focused on whether an electoral quorum should be introduced. Despite intense de￾liberations in the special commission, the majority of the parliament found no compelling reason to intro￾duce any form of electoral hurdle. Florian Keller, an MP from the Alternative Liste, expressed the prevailing 
sentiment: “There is no reason to deny entry to the Cantonal Council to anyone who can muster enough 
electoral votes. A possible fragmentation of the parties reflects the voters’ will, and any attempt to suppress 
it with quorums is undemocratic.”35 The SVP was the only party advocating for a canton-wide 5% quorum, 
arguing it would prevent party fragmentation and discourage the entry of individuals with extremist views, 
while also enhancing the Council’s productivity and efficiency. However, this proposal was ultimately re￾jected by the majority of the Council. 
Following the extended debate on quorums, the final vote on adopting biproportional apportionment took 
place. Figure 10 and Figure 11 depict the final voting results on the bill and the constitutional amendment

2.4.4 Canton of Nidwalden 
The cantonal legislature of Nidwalden, consisting of 
60 seats, transitioned from a majoritarian to a propor￾tional representation system in 1981. Despite this 
change, the formerly dominant CVP and FDP parties 
retained significant influence. By the 2000s, the SVP 
emerged as a third major force, and later the GPS and 
GLP entered the political scene. The SP, with its mar￾ginal vote share, formed a faction with the GPS. Since 
2014, seat allocation has been determined by the bi￾proportional apportionment method. Figure 12 illus￾trates the development of seat shares from the first 
proportional elections in 1982 to the latest in 2022. 
The reform of the seat allocation process was initiated by a complaint from a group associated with the 
Green Party of Nidwalden regarding the 2010 elections. The Government Council’s decision on seat distri￾bution across districts was challenged as unconstitutional due to the varying sizes of districts, some of which 
were relatively small. Although the Cantonal Constitutional Court initially rejected the complaint, stating 
that the division of electoral districts was consistent with the constitution given historical circumstances, the 
FSC overturned this decision. The Court ruled that the political communes did not justify significant devi￾ations from proportional representation, compelling the canton to revise its electoral law.

In response, the Government Council

Government Council drafted a bill 
adopting this method and submitted it for 
further consultation, which saw broad support except for the planned introduction of a 5% quorum.
The Cantonal Council debated the bill in two readings, where arguments mirrored those in other cantons: 
the biproportional method was praised for better reflecting voter preferences without altering constituency 
structures, thus preserving municipal autonomy. The SVP’s alternative model of a regionalized Pukelsheim 
system, combined with the Hagenbach-Bischoff method, did not gain traction. The SVP and parts of the 
CVP criticized the biproportional system for its complexity and perceived distance from citizens. Despite 
this opposition, the biproportional method was ultimately adopted (see Figure 13). 
The initial bill included a 5% quorum, but this was removed in the revised version after failing to gain sup￾port during consultations. The two readings in the Cantonal Council did not see significant controversy 
over the quorum issue, as proponents agreed that no artificial electoral hurdle was necessary. 
Two “constructive referenda” were launched against the partial revision of the electoral law: one by the SVP, 
advocating district mergers, and another proposing a return to majoritarianism.37 However, both counter￾proposals were defeated by the biproportional variant, which received 60.4% approval.

2.4.5 Canton of Zug 
The Zug cantonal parliament consists of 80 members 
and cantonal elections take place on the basis of pro￾portional representation. Since 2014, the distribu￾tion of the seats to the parties follows the bipropor￾tional method. 
Until the 2006 elections, two parties formed the left 
camp: on the one hand, the Social Democratic Party 
and, on the other, the Alternative Left Party. In 2009, 
the Alternative Left Party together with the Green 
Party formed an alliance party “Alternative – die 
Grünen Zug” (henceforth classified as GPS). Apart 
from this change and the entry of the Green Liberal 
Party in 2010, the party landscape has remained relatively stable over the last decades. Figure 14 illustrates 
the seat shares of the cantonal parties between 1978 and 2022.

A first substantive point of contact with 
the biproportional seat allocation proce￾dure already occurred in 2006. On 6 July 
2006, the Cantonal Council discussed the 
total revision of the cantonal electoral law. 
A minority of the Advisory Committee 
proposed that the matter be rejected in or￾der to examine in detail the method of bi￾proportional apportionment. However, 
this proposal was rejected by the Cantonal 
Council by 54 votes to 15 (with 3 absten￾tions). 
Then, about four years later on 7 February 
2010, the cantonal parties of the Alterna￾tive Green Party, the Social Democratic Party, the Christian Social Party and the Independent Green Forum 
Hünenberg, as well as six private individuals, lodged an appeal with the FSC against the decision of the 
cantonal council of 28 January 2010 concerning the number of Cantonal Council mandates for each mu￾nicipality (which constitute the electoral districts) as well as the decision of the Cantonal Council of 27 Au￾gust 2009 concerning the amendment of electoral law of 28 September 2006. 
On 20 December 2010, the FSC upheld the appeal on the main point, i.e., with regard to the Cantonal Coun￾cil’s decision on the number of Council mandates attributed to the municipalities/districts (BGE 136 I 376). 
In line with its previous jurisprudence, the Court reasoned that the realization of proportional representa￾tion strongly depends on the size of the electoral district. Districts with a low number of seats correspond 
with a high natural quorum and any vote cast for a party or group below that quorum would be ineffective. 
Under such conditions, the chance of success of a vote (in the sense that it counts towards the result) varies 
between the districts and, consequentially, between voters. However, according to the Court, this infringes 
the constitutional requirement of article 34 paragraph 2 of the Federal Constitution, according to which 
“the free formation of will and uncorrupted voting are protected by the warranty of political rights”. 
In response to the Court’s decision, the Government Council drafted an amendment to the legal basis of the 
election procedure and sent it out for consultation. Subsequently, the government presented three different 
models to the advisory commission and the cantonal parliament: 
- Adjustment of the district structure (model 1): The first model envisaged a redistricting. To meet 
the FSC’s requirement of a natural quorum of less than 10%, existing districts would have had to be 
merged into districts of similar size. 
- Creation of constituency associations (model 2): The second proposed the formation of constitu￾ency associations and a change in the distribution procedure. Instead of distributing the legislative 
seats on district level, they should first be distributed at the level of constituency associations and 
then, in a second step, at the level of districts. Through this artificial partition of the seat allocation 
procedure, high district quorums can be effectively circumvented. 
- Change of the apportionment formula (model 3): The third model envisaged the adoption of the 
biproportional apportionment method. This model is essentially very similar to the second one, as 
Cornerstones during the reform process in the Canton of Zug July 6, 2006 Minority request by the Advisory Committee to consider the bi￾proportional apportionment method for parliamentary elec￾tions was rejected by the Cantonal Council. 
December 20, 2010 Ruling of the FSC that the electoral law of the Canton of Zug is 
not in conformity with the Federal Constitution. 
January 31, 2013 First parliamentary reading on the reform bill. 
Mai 2, 2013 Second parliamentary reading and final vote on the reform bill. 
July 10, 2013 Ruling of the FSC, which overturned the decision of the Can￾tonal Council to oppose the biproportional apportionment 
method with a second proposal (status quo). 
September 22, 2013 Popular vote on the introduction of biproportional apportion- ment. 
Table 15: Key reform stages in the Canton of Zug.

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
48 
it proposes an adjustment of the apportionment procedure. But instead of determining seat enti￾tlements at the level of constituency associations, the results at the level of the entire electoral area 
serve as the basis for seat allocation. In addition, a slightly different formula applies (divisor method 
with standard rounding). Since this model requires only a minimal intervention in the existing elec￾toral system, it was clearly favored by both the government and the consultation participants. 
The commission in charge was also in favor of the third model but suggested two changes to the govern￾ment’s proposal: First, a statutory quorum should be introduced. The commission opted for an “either-or” 
clause, as was already in force in then Canton of Aargau: To participate in the seat allocation, an electoral 
list must either garner 5% of the electoral votes in at least one district or 3% of all the votes cast throughout 
the entire electoral territory. A second amendment concerns the single-member districts. A “majority 
clause” is to ensure that in each district the list with the largest number of votes receives at least one seat. 
Due to discordant seat allocations, there is the possibility that the list with the largest number of votes in a 
single constituency would not win the seat. As this would be hard to comprehend and accept from the vot￾ers’ point of view, it was decided to include this additional clause in the commission’s draft. 
The reform of Zug’s electoral system was influenced by another decision-making process that took place in 
parallel, which shall be briefly described. At that time the guarantee of the constitution of the Canton of 
Schwyz by the National Council was pending. This was relevant because the constitution of the Canton of 
Schwyz contained provisions on the election of the Cantonal Council that conflicted with jurisdiction of the 
FSC. Specifically, the revision provided that the municipalities form the electoral districts, that each electoral 
district is entitled to at least one parliamentary seat and that the cantonal councilors are elected within the 
electoral districts. As these provisions were not compatible with the latest jurisdiction of the FSC, the Federal 
Council recommended to the FA not to guarantee the conflicting constitutional provisions. However, in its 
decision of 27 November 2012, the Council of States guaranteed the constitution integrally. The integral 
guarantee of the constitution of the Canton of Schwyz by both councils would have been interpreted as a 
strong political signal in favor of cantonal electoral autonomy and as a relativization of the previous juris￾diction of the FSC. Against this background, the Cantonal Council has formulated two antithetical requests, 
depending on the outcome: 
- If the Schwyz Constitution was to be guaranteed, the introduction of biproportional apportion￾ment should be constitutionally excluded. 
- If the Schwyz Constitution was not to be guaranteed, the Commission proposes the introduction 
of its own version of the biproportional apportionment. 
On 31 January 2013, the Cantonal Council commenced deliberations on revision of the electoral system. In 
the first session, the majority of the legislative assembly essentially followed the proposal of the Government 
Council. The CVP and SVP factions were fundamentally opposed to the biproportional apportionment 
method. In view of the SVP faction, the FSC’s decision interfered too much with cantonal autonomy. For 
this reason, they opposed both the revision of the law and the revision of the constitution. The CVP faction 
criticized the new procedure also on a material level: the new procedure presumably contained serious de￾ficiencies and led to even greater injustices than the election procedure that had applied until then. In par￾ticular, they were dissatisfied by the occurrence of discordant seat allocations. The FDP faction also had 
reservations about the biproportional method, but unlike the SVP and CVP saw a need for revision. How-

=== PDF PAGE 60 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
49 
ever, if the Schwyz cantonal constitution should be guaranteed by the FA, the FDP would reject the bipro￾portional method. Otherwise, it would support the draft submitted by the Advisory Committee, albeit less 
out of conviction than because of the futility of the situation. 
SP, GPS and GLP were on the side in favor of the new apportionment system. The arguments among the 
proponents were largely identical: Each vote should be considered and given equal weight in the outcome. 
And the only method that can optimally fulfill this condition without changing the district structure is bi￾proportional apportionment. However, the SP also expressed criticism of the biproportional method. The 
lack of transparency in the allocation of seats poses a major disadvantage. 
Regarding the introduction of a statutory quorum, the Council dissented from the governmental position. 
In contrast to the government, a majority of the Cantonal Council saw the need to implement an electoral 
threshold. Thus, the Cantonal Council followed the commission’s proposal to introduce a quorum follow￾ing the example of the Canton of Aargau. I.e., a party needs at least either 3% of all party votes in the canton 
or 5% of the party votes in a district in order to be included in the seat allocation process. Remarkably, there 
was little debate on the proposed statutory quorum, with only the SVP and SP commenting on it. The SVP’s 
stance was notably surprising; their criticism of the quorum was typically associated with left-leaning par￾ties. They argued that the quorum system, as outlined in § 52c para. 4, would disadvantage smaller parties 
in favor of larger ones.38 This perspective, articulated by SVP MP Beni Riedi, reflects a view usually found 
on the left, which traditionally opposes mechanisms perceived as favoring major parties at the expense of 
minorities.
Since, apart from the SVP and the SP, who opposed the implementation of a statutory quorum for identical 
reasons, the minutes do not provide any information on the other parties’ stances on that matter, no further 
conclusions can be drawn from the voting result shown in Figure 15. 
Figure 15: Positions on the proposal of the advisory commission to introduce a paragraph providing for a statutory 
quorum (3% globally or 5% locally) for parliamentary elections in the Canton of Zug.
A few months later, on 18 March 2013, it was the turn of the National Council to decide on the guarantee 
of the Schwyz Cantonal Constitution. The Council rejected the guarantee of the article in question for the 
second time, making the non-guarantee final. The decision was close however, with 100 votes against 91. 
For the abovementioned reasons, this decision also had an impact on the second reading of the Cantonal 
Council, which took place on 2 Mai 2013. 
Since the general discussion on the introduction of the biproportional apportionment method had already 
taken place in the first reading, the second reading was primarily concerned with clarifying the further

2.4.6 Canton of Schwyz 
The Cantonal Council of Schwyz has 100 members 
who are elected according to proportional representa￾tion. In 2016, the first election took place following the 
biproportional procedure. The Canton of Schwyz is 
the only canton in which electoral reform was trig￾gered not by the legislature or the executive, but by 
popular initiatives. This, of course, also affected the re￾form process, which was eminently multi-layered, 
with the various levels intertwining in complex ways.
The party landscape in the Canton of Schwyz is domi￾nated by the SVP, FDP, CVP and the SP (cf. Figure 18). 
In 2016, the GLP ran for the first time and entered par￾liament with 3 seats. In the last elections, they were able to double their seat share. The electoral landscape, 
with its 30 districts and a median district size of 2, is very markedly parcelled. 
As mentioned in the previous chapter on the reform process in the Canton of Zug, the reform of the Schwyz 
cantonal electoral system was preceded by a protracted and interruptive process.

The reform process in the Canton of 
Schwyz was initiated in response to a fed￾eral dispute, highlighting the intercon￾nectedness and reciprocity of the in￾volved processes. To fully understand the 
context of this reform, it is essential to ex￾amine the broader legislative and judicial 
dynamics that influenced its initiation. 
In 2011, the Canton of Schwyz adopted a 
new constitution, which was approved by 
59.8% of voters in a referendum held on 
May 15. This constitutional revision re￾tained the existing electoral system for 
the Cantonal Council. As part of the 
preparations for the 2012 cantonal par￾liamentary elections, the Government 
Council issued the necessary electoral de￾crees. However, these decisions faced 
three separate legal challenges before the 
FSC. On March 19, 2012, the Court ruled 
that the cantonal electoral law was not in compliance with the Federal Constitution. 
Subsequently, the FA reviewed the constitution of the Canton of Schwyz and opted to reject certain provi￾sions related to cantonal elections. Specifically, the FA declined to guarantee § 48(3) of the cantonal consti￾tution, which mandated proportional representation for cantonal parliamentary elections. This decision 
was influenced by § 48(2) of the constitution, which stipulates that each municipality forms its own electoral 
district and that seats are allocated proportionally based on population, with each municipality guaranteed 
at least one seat. As a result, 13 of the 30 municipalities, which each receive a single seat, effectively experi￾enced first-past-the-post elections. This practical outcome diverged from the proportional representation 
requirement outlined in § 48(3), leading to the FA’s decision not to guarantee this provision.41
Political responses to the FA’s decision were swift. On August 30, 2013, the cantonal branch of the SVP 
introduced a popular initiative titled “For a Simple and Comprehensible Electoral System”, advocating for 
the adoption of single-round plurality voting in cantonal parliamentary elections. This initiative sought to 
simplify the electoral process by eliminating proportional representation. 
In contrast, on October 17, 2013, a multi-party committee representing the EVP, GLP, SP, GPS, and the 
BDP presented a competing initiative titled “For Fair Proportional Representation – Yes to Diversity of 
Opinion in the Schwyz Cantonal Council”. This initiative aimed to establish a fair, proportional represen￾tation system for cantonal elections. It proposed removing the municipalities as defined electoral districts 
41 The decision caused great stir inside and outside of Schwyz. For it was only the fifth time in the entire history of the 
Swiss Confederation that the FA did not guarantee a cantonal constitution (or parts of it) (cf. Biaggini 2007: 567, 
Auer 2016: 240f.).

from the cantonal constitution and repealing the constitutional guarantee of seats for municipalities. How￾ever, the initiative did not specify the precise design of the proposed electoral procedure. 
The Schwyz Government Council reviewed the proposed initiatives and recommended that the Cantonal 
Council reject them, advising against their approval in a potential popular vote. The government supported 
maintaining proportional representation but found the PR initiative’s proposed constitutional changes too 
radical. It viewed the minimum seat guarantee for each municipality as a fundamental constitutional prin￾ciple to be preserved. 
Given the lack of legal grounds to present the two initiatives to voters simultaneously, the Government 
Council decided to counter the single-round plurality initiative with a direct counterproposal. Anticipating 
a possible withdrawal of the PR initiative, the counterproposal incorporated aspects favored by its propo￾nents. Specifically, it proposed adopting the biproportional method for party seat allocation while preserv￾ing the existing district structure and the guaranteed minimum seats for municipalities. The majority of the 
advisory commission endorsed the government’s counterproposal and recommended the rejection of both 
popular initiatives. 
The deliberation of the popular initiatives and the government’s counterproposal occurred in two separate 
readings. The first reading involved an initial discussion and detailed consultation of both the initiatives and 
the counterproposal. The second reading focused on decisions regarding the validity of the initiatives, rec￾ommendations for the popular vote, and legislative amendments related to the single-round plurality initi￾ative and the government’s counterproposal. 
The vote on the PR initiative was scheduled for a subsequent ballot, and it remained uncertain whether its 
proponents would maintain their stance, leading to no implementation law being drafted for it. The primary 
debate occurred during the first reading on November 19, 2014. This debate was broad in scope and in￾cluded aspects beyond the focus of this study. Consequently, the following summary of the parliamentary 
deliberations concentrates specifically on the discussion related to the apportionment method. 
Following the FA’s partial rejection of provisions from the Schwyz Constitution and related decisions by the 
FSC on cantonal electoral reforms, there was broad consensus among political parties that a change was 
necessary. However, significant disagreements emerged regarding the design of the new electoral system. 
All parties except the SVP supported proportional representation and largely endorsed the government’s 
proposed changes to the electoral law, which included the implementation of biproportional representation. 
The debate centered on the Government Council’s counterproposal, which allowed for significant legal ad￾justments and political influence. 
Proponents of proportional representation generally agreed that the parliament should reflect current po￾litical trends in the electorate. Within this group, there were differing opinions on the biproportional ap￾portionment method. The FDP preferred a system incorporating constituency associations with guaranteed 
seats for municipalities. The SP, while not enthusiastic about the biproportional method, accepted it as a 
compromise when combined with a seat guarantee for constituencies. 

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
55 
In contrast, the SVP, through its popular initiative, advocated for a majoritarian voting system rather than 
a PR-based system. The SVP viewed the FA’s partial rejection as an affront to cantonal autonomy and ar￾gued that a majoritarian system would provide greater transparency and simplicity, aligning better with the 
needs of the Canton of Schwyz. 
In addition to the core debate over the appropriate electoral system for the cantonal parliament, a further 
controversy emerged regarding the introduction of a statutory electoral quorum. The advisory commission 
proposed two options: the majority request called for a global 3% quorum, while the minority request sug￾gested a combined 1% global quorum with a 5% local quorum. 
The Government Council, guided by various legal opinions, opposed any form of quorum and advocated 
for maintaining an open electoral system. This stance was echoed by the FDP parliamentary group, which 
opposed an electoral quorum on principle. Christoph Pfister, a member of the FDP, argued that such thresh￾olds are mechanisms used by powerful entities to sustain their dominance: “Let’s not delude ourselves, an 
electoral quorum is a tool used by the powerful to maintain power. That’s wrong.”42
At the same time, some FDP members supported a statutory quorum of 3-5%. Xaver Schuler from the SVP 
cited evidence from the Canton of Aargau, arguing that a quorum enhances council efficiency.43 The SP, 
Greens, and Independents, while critical of quorums as they “arbitrarily set lower limits for entry into par￾liament” and are thus “undemocratic” (Luka Markić, MP SP), were not entirely opposed to the concept.44
They indicated a willingness to consider a constitutional provision for a quorum in the future if deemed 
necessary. The CVP and GLP supported a minimum threshold of 1% for a party or list to gain entry to 
parliament. Ultimately, four models were compared and subjected to a runoff vote: 
1. No quorum (as proposed by the government) 
2. 1% global quorum (proposed by MP Markus Ming) 
3. 1% global quorum combined with a 5% local quorum (minority proposal by the advisory commis￾sion) 
4. 3% global quorum (majority proposal by the advisory commission) 
As shown in Figure 19, MP Ming’s (GLP) motion prevailed in a three-round runoff vote. Hence, the major￾ity of the council opted for the canton-wide quorum of 1%.

In the second reading on December 17, 2014, the Cantonal Council conducted a detailed examination and

and the PR initiative. The MR initiative was first debated, confirmed as valid, and its implementation law 
was adopted. It was then decided to recommend the rejection of the MR initiative to the voters. Following 
this, the Council voted on whether to oppose the single-round plurality initiative with a counterproposal; 
this proposition was approved, and the implementation law for the counterproposal was adopted. The PR 
initiative was then discussed, confirmed as valid, and it was decided to recommend its rejection in a potential

initiative was rejected by 55.7% of voters, while the counterproposal was accepted with 53.6% of the vote.

did not occur. Consequently, the initiative committee for the PR initiative opted to withdraw their proposal.

2.4.7 Canton of Valais 
Valais, located in the southwestern part of Switzerland, 
is a bilingual canton with French and German as its of￾ficial languages. This linguistic divide significantly in￾fluences cantonal politics. The canton has a strong his￾torical connection to catholicism, which underscores 
the prominence of the CVP and its local branch CSP. 
The Grand Council of Valais, comprising 130 mem￾bers, is elected through PR using the biproportional 
apportionment method. The electoral reform of 2017, 
subsequently codified in the cantonal electoral law, in￾troduced several changes, including a redesign of the 
electoral district structure. Previously, electoral dis￾tricts aligned with administrative units between communes and the canton. Under the new system, these 
districts were restructured into 14 sub-districts, which were then organized into six broader districts. 
A distinctive feature of the Valais electoral system is that upper-tier apportionment is conducted not at the 
cantonal level but within the six newly formed districts. This parallel and separate apportionment process 
at the district level represents a unique approach among the cantons applying biproportionality. 
Figure 22: Parties’ seat-shares in parliamentary elections in the Canton of Valais, 1977-2021.

58 
For a long time, the CVP (and CSP) 
maintained an absolute majority in the 
cantonal legislature of Valais. However, 
with the rise of the GPS and SVP since the 
early 2000s, alongside the nationwide de￾cline of Christian democracy, the CVP 
lost this absolute majority in the 2013 
elections. 
The Canton of Valais has a complex re￾form history. The debate over a new elec￾toral system began in 2004. On May 24, 
2004, Susanne Hugo-Lötscher, then a 
member of the Grand Council for the So￾cial Democrats, lodged a constitutional 
complaint with the FSC (BGE 131 I 85). 
Hugo-Lötscher and her co-petitioners 
contested a decision by the Council of 
State from April 7, 2004, which had fixed 
the number of parliamentarians to be 
elected from each district for the upcom￾ing 2005-2009 legislature. 
The complaint argued that some electoral 
districts were excessively small and that 
the variance in district sizes was too pronounced. This led to significant quorum thresholds in certain dis￾tricts, effectively excluding not only small splinter groups but also substantial minorities from representa￾tion in the Grand Council. The petitioners contended that such practices were incompatible with the prin￾ciple of equal representation, asserting that proportional representation was impractical in small districts 
with high quorums. 
On October 27, 2004, the FSC ruled that the electoral system for the Valais Grand Council met federal re￾quirements for cantonal parliamentary elections. The Court found that the constituency division in Valais 
was historically justified and that deviations from “perfect” vote-seat proportionality were acceptable given 
the historical context. Furthermore, key provisions of the election procedure were enshrined in the cantonal 
constitution and thus beyond the Court’s review. This ruling effectively marked the provisional conclusion 
of the debate on electoral law in Valais. 
In response to the legal ruling and ongoing dissatisfaction with the electoral system, a multi-party alliance 
from the German-speaking part of the canton (Upper Valais) – including representatives from the SP, SVP, 
GPS, and FDP – initiated a political reform effort. On December 6, 2010, they proposed the popular initia￾tive “Every Vote Counts”, aiming to amend the constitution.45 The initiative called for the creation of three 
45 During the reform period, the Grand Council was composed of the following factions: Alliance de Gauche / 
Linksallianz (AdG/LA), CVP Oberwallis, (CVPO), PDC Valais Central (PDCC), PDC Bas-Valais (PDCB), CSP 
Oberwallis (CSPO), PLR, SVP Oberwallis (SVPO), UDC, Les Verts. 
Cornerstones during the reform process in the Canton of Valais October 27, 2004 FSC ruling that elections to the Valais Grand Council comply 
with federal constitutional law. 
December 6, 2010 Submission of the popular initiative “Every Vote Counts” by an 
alliance of different political groups. Among other things, the in￾itiative envisaged the introduction of biproportional apportion- ment. October 3, 2012 Final report of the extra-parliamentary “commission R21” on 
the reform of cantonal structures and institutions. 
February 12, 2014 Decision of the FSC that the electoral system for the Grand 
Council does not comply with requirements of the Federal Con￾stitution.

concerning the territorial organization and the cantonal institu￾tions (introductory debate).

institutions. 
June 14, 2015 Popular vote on the constitutional reform “R-21”. The reform 
fell just short of the required majority. 
November 29, 2015 Vote on the popular initiative “Every Vote Counts”. The initia￾tive was rejected by a vast majority of the voters.

Table 19: Key reform stages in the Canton of Valais.

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
59 
electoral districts and the implementation of the biproportional seat allocation procedure. Under this pro￾posal, seats would first be apportioned at the level of the three new districts and then sub-apportioned within 
the existing districts. 
The initiative sparked a broad reform debate and prompted the government to consider a fundamental re￾organization of cantonal institutions and territorial structure. Despite the initiative’s significance, it was not 
put to a vote until November 29, 2015. Both the parliament and the government opposed the initiative, and 
no legislative counterproposal was presented to avoid duplication with a parallel, more comprehensive re￾form effort. The initiative failed to pass, receiving 32% of the vote in favor, 64% against, and 4% blank or 
invalid. This outcome demonstrated clear opposition from the electorate to the proposed reform. 
On June 6, 2011, the Valais government created the extra-parliamentary commission “Territory and Insti￾tutions of the 21st Century”. The government saw a need for a comprehensive reform of the canton’s terri￾torial and institutional organization. The newly found commission was tasked with preparing and present￾ing a report with specific propositions how to modernize the communal and cantonal institutions as well as 
the territorial organization. Due to its mandate to develop reform proposals, the commission’s name be￾came established as “R21” (“Reforms in the 21st Century”). 
On October 3, 2012, the commission presented the final report with a variety of concrete reform recom￾mendations. A subset of these recommendations concerned the elections of the Grand Council. In the re￾port, the commission pointed to the previous case law of the FSC regarding the design of PR-based cantonal 
electoral systems for parliamentary elections. According to the FSC, a PR-based system is characterized by 
the prospect of representation for the various parties in proportion to their share of the electorate. Since the 
implementation of this requirement depends primarily on the design of the electoral districts, the structure 
of the electoral area was a key issue in the commission’s report. Although the FSC had already ruled that the 
electoral system for the Grand Council in Valais was in conformity with the federal provisions (see above) 
and that there was therefore no need for an electoral reform (at least from a legal point of view), the com￾mission was nevertheless dissatisfied with the current district structure. The commission argued that the 
will of the electorate is not adequately reflected by the current system due to the relative smallness of many 
districts: 
“The R21 Commission is guided by [the] considerations and the case law of the Federal Supreme Court. From 
a political point of view, it is not correct to simply nip in the bud the will of the voters expressed in a small 
constituency by means of a high natural quorum, without the possibility of asserting this will to vote in a 
larger constituency.”46
In the section on electoral districts and the electoral system, the commission presented three different mod￾els to remedy the undesired allocation effects arising from small districts: 
1. Formation of six electoral districts with a minimum size of nine seats each. 
2. Retention of the current electoral districts and simultaneous introduction of (three) superordinate 
electoral regions (biproportional model). 
3. Formation of six electoral districts, each comprising two or more sub-districts (alternative bipro￾portional model). 
46 Source: Report of the “R21” commission of October 3, 2012, p. 75. Translated from German. 

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
60 
The first model was excluded from the outset because, in the opinion of the majority of the commission, it 
pays too little attention to the peripheral areas. Two-thirds of the commission were in favor of the third 
model, as this option takes better account of regional units. The remaining third supported the second 
model. The commission suggested to uphold the district-based statutory quorum of 8%. The Government 
Council welcomed the changes proposed by the majority of the commission and requested the Grand Coun￾cil to approve the proposed reform so the changes could be subjected to a mandatory constitutional refer￾endum. However, before the Grand Council could launch the debate on the planned constitutional reform, 
the FSC issued a new ruling in electoral law matters. 
In its decision of February 12, 2014, the FSC ruled that the current electoral system for the Grand Council 
does not comply with the Federal Constitution, as it does not meet its requirements (BGE 140 I 107). Some 
of the electoral districts are too small and as a consequence, proportional representation cannot be realized 
in the districts concerned. The FSC has thus ordered the cantonal authorities to introduce a different elec￾toral system for the Grand Council which complies with the Federal Constitution. This is quite astonishing, 
since the Court had ruled that the election procedure was permissible ten years earlier. In its ruling, the FSC 
also pointed out this contradiction with its previous case law and justified the deviation with the circum￾stances that have changed since then. On the one hand, it referred to the new possibilities of allowing the 
inadequacies of small districts to be compensated for with measures that do not require a change in the 
constituency structure.47 On the other hand, the Court referred to the cantonal efforts for territorial reor￾ganization, which relativize the importance of the political districts (as historical relics) as a basis for the 
electoral districts.48 Although this Court decision did not fundamentally change the state of affairs, it did 
give urgency to the cantonal government’s aspiration to modernize the cantonal institutions. 
The Government Council supported the changes as proposed by the R21 commission and applied to the 
Grand Council to partially change the cantonal constitution in accordance with the propositions. The 
Grand Council accepted the partial amendment to the constitution in two separate readings on September 
8, 2014, and March 12, 2015, with some substantive changes. 
During the parliamentary deliberations, a variety of reform variants were proposed and discussed. In addi￾tion, other elements were added to the reform, which were neither foreseen in the Commission’s draft nor 
proposed by the government: For example, the attempt to reduce the council to 100 members or the intro￾duction of a guarantee of 35 Grand Council seats for the Upper Valais as a means of minority protection for 
the German-speaking part of the canton, as proposed by the CSPO and CVPO. Although the latter element 
of linguistic minority protection had no substantive connection with the reform of the allocation method 
itself, it still found a parliamentary majority and was thus included in the reform bill. This addition sparked 
a controversial discussion in the Grand Council and was also to play a decisive role in the ensuing popular 
vote. 
The centerpiece of the electoral reform was Article 42 of the cantonal constitution, which is to determine 
the method of seat-allocation as well as the future district structure. There was a broad consensus among all 
political factions that an electoral reform was inevitable. In light of the upcoming elections in 2017, it was 
47 Here the Court mentioned the two possibilities of either creating constituency associations or introducing the bi￾proportional apportionment method. 
48 Here the Court referred to the reform project R21. 

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
61 
therefore considered a high priority issue on the political agenda to lay the groundwork for legitimate par￾liamentary elections and to prevent the reform from failing at all costs. Accordingly, all factions signaled a 
high willingness to compromise. The CVPO and CSPO tabled two identical amendments which, in addition 
to guaranteeing seats for Upper Valais, also provided for the division of the electoral area into districts and 
sub-districts. The existing districts would continue in the form of sub-districts and be grouped into new 
districts on the upper-tier level. Additionally, the seats should be allocated based on “inversed biproportion￾ality”.49 However, the idea of an inversed biproportional method was discarded during the second reading. 
Besides the CVPO and CSPO, also the UDC, PDCB and PDCC supported the idea of dividing the electoral 
area into districts and sub-districts. They emphasized the importance of maintaining the existing and his￾torically deeply anchored district structure. In their view, only the biproportional method could sustain this 
continuity since one-dimensional PR would require a change in the number and size of the electoral dis￾tricts. The other factions, SVPO, PLR, AdG/LA, Les Verts, voted for a model that proposed the creation of 
six new districts around the urban centers in the canton and the retention of the one-dimensional PR ap￾portionment formula (i.e., district-based elections).50 They asserted that this model is easier for the electorate 
to understand and that it is more in line with the concept of proportional representation (since one-dimen￾sional PR does not allow for the occurrence of locally disproportionate results, they argued). 
By a narrow majority, the Grand Council followed the CVPO/CSPO proposal to establish a guarantee of 35 
seats for the Upper Valais with 66 votes in favor, 58 against and 4 abstentions. With regard to the district 
structure, the Grand Council followed the proposal of the Commission, which explicitly defined the six 
upper-tier districts and the 14 sub-districts. In addition, the biproportional procedure should be explicitly 
mentioned in the constitution (instead of implementing it “solely” as law). 
Figure 23 shows the positions of the parliamentary groups on the final reform bill: 71 supported the partial 
constitutional revision, 53 opposed it and 4 abstained from the vote. 
Figure 23: Positions on the final reform bill, which, among other things, provided for the introduction of the bipro￾portional apportionment method for parliamentary elections in the Canton of Valais. 
However, the figure illustrates the final vote on the overall reform conducted on March 12, 2015, rather than 
specifically on the allocation procedure. The reform itself was highly contentious within the Grand Council, 
as it encompassed a range of elements, each of which was controversial in its own right. Additionally, these 
elements highlighted shifting fault lines among the factions. The complexity and multidimensionality of the

reform made it challenging to discern the factions’ stances on each component individually and to fully 
grasp the considerations and arguments that influenced their final positions in the vote. 
Nevertheless, the controversial bill found a parliamentary majority and, as a constitutional bill, was subject 
to a popular referendum. To avoid any extraneous influence with regard to the sensitive and controversial 
issue, the parliamentarians decided that the composition and electoral system of the Grand Council should 
form the subject of a separate issue, independent from other reform elements concerning the organization 
of the Valais authorities. 
The popular vote was held on June 14, 2015. 45% of the voters voted in favor of the amendment, 46% voted 
against, and 9% cast either a blank or invalid ballot. The constitutional amendment thus failed to win the 
required absolute majority (and also fell short of a relative majority).51 In a later message to the Grand Coun￾cil, the Government Council commented on the voting result with the following words: “It is not unreason￾able to assume that the guarantee clause of 35 seats in favor of the two Upper Valais constituencies [Brig and 
Visp, S.L.] played a role in the result that occurred.”52 Given the scope and urgency of the reform, the failure 
of the reform bill constituted a significant political setback. Additionally, with the popular initiative “Every 
Vote Counts” failing to secure a majority in the vote held on November 29, 2015, the canton found itself 
without a legally valid framework for conducting cantonal parliamentary elections. 
In response to the deadlock, the Government Council enacted a temporary decree to establish a legal frame￾work for the 2017 Grand Council elections in compliance with federal law. If the Grand Council had rejected 
the decree or if a subsequent referendum had annulled it, the only recourse would have been to adopt a 
government resolution as a last resort. However, the Grand Council unanimously approved the decree on 
March 9, 2016. The decree, which mirrored the R21 proposal but excluded the seat guarantee for Upper 
Valais, was intended for the 2017 elections and had a provisional validity of four years. 
To ensure the electoral system’s compliance with federal law beyond the 2017 elections, the provisions of 
the decree were incorporated into a revision of the Cantonal Law on Political Rights. The Grand Council 
approved this revision on December 14, 2017, with a vote of 83 in favor, 42 against, and one abstention. 
51 According to the Cantonal Law on Political Rights Art. 4(2) and the Cantonal Constitution Art. 106, the absolute 
majority of the citizens participating in the vote decides on constitutional revisions. This peculiar regulation means 
that both blank and invalid (!) ballots are added to the no-votes, resulting in a strong braking effect. 
52 Source: Message of the Government Council to the Grand Council on the issuance of a decree regulating the can￾tonal parliamentary elections, December 17, 2015, p. 4. Translated from German.

2.4.8 Canton of Uri 
Uri, a rural-alpine canton in central Switzerland, 
features a fragmented electoral landscape due to its 
mountainous geography. The canton’s 64-member 
cantonal council elections are conducted across 20 
municipalities, which also serve as electoral districts. 
The electoral system is mixed: proportional 
representation (PR) applies to districts with five or 
more seats, while plurality voting is used in districts 
with fewer than five seats. As of the 2020 elections, 16 
districts utilized plurality voting and four districts 
employed PR. Following the 2019 electoral reform, PR 
districts now use the biproportional apportionment 
method. 
Politically, Uri is conservative, with approximately three-quarters of electoral votes going to the SVP, FDP, 
and CVP. However, party performance in majority/plurality voting districts – comprising 27 of the 64 
parliamentary seats – can be misleading, as party alignments are less significant in these districts. Party vote 
shares have been officially recorded by the FSO since the 2008 cantonal elections, although PR has been in 
place in certain districts since 1992.

In 1989, Uri’s electorate approved a par￾tial introduction of PR for cantonal par￾liamentary elections. The new system was 
implemented in the 1992 elections, ap￾plying PR to districts with three or more 
seats while maintaining MR in districts 
with one or two seats. Uri remains one of 
the few cantons with a mixed electoral 
system, alongside Basel Stadt and Appen￾zell Ausserrhoden. 
In 2011, the CVP youth section proposed 
a cantonal popular initiative titled “Elec￾tion of Heads Instead of Parties”, aiming to reduce party influence by advocating for a return to majoritari￾anism. The initiative was decisively rejected in a popular vote held on September 23, 2012, with 57.5% of 
voters opposing the change. This outcome reaffirmed the electorate’s preference for retaining the PR system 
in larger municipalities. 
In preparation for the 2016 cantonal parliamentary elections, the Government Council issued a resolution 
on September 29, 2015, specifying the number of seats allocated to each municipality. This decision was 
subsequently challenged by a coalition of left-wing politicians from the GPS and SP, who filed an appeal 
with the FSC (BGE 143 I 92). The appellants criticized the electoral district division, arguing that the small 
size of some districts created unacceptable distortions in electoral equity, contrary to FSC case law. They 
contended that the seat guarantee for municipalities lacking an arithmetical entitlement further exacerbated 
these distortions. Additionally, they argued that party affiliation plays a significant role even in majoritarian 
districts, countering the Government Council’s claim that party influence was negligible in personality￾based elections. 
On October 12, 2016, the FSC upheld the appeal. While acknowledging that a mixed electoral system is 
permissible, the Court found that the district-induced quorum in PR districts with fewer than nine seats was 
excessively high. The Court noted that deviations from proportional representation are only opportune in 
“objectively justifiable cases” and did not find any such justification in this instance, thus rejecting the sig￾nificant intrusions into proportional representation (BGE 143 I 92, p. 100). 
A few weeks after the Court ruling, the SP and GPS proposed a parliamentary motion for a comprehensive 
electoral district reform. They advocated for either creating a single constituency or establishing new dis￾tricts that would align with future population trends and federal constitutional principles. This motion, 
however, failed to secure a parliamentary majority and was therefore rejected. Consequently, the responsi￾bility to draft a revised legal framework for cantonal parliamentary elections fell to the Government Council. 
The Government Council submitted a draft reform to the Cantonal Council to implement the FSC’s deci￾sion, which proposed introducing the biproportional seat allocation method in PR electoral districts. How￾ever, discontent with the FSC’s practice led the Cantonal Council of Uri, mirroring earlier actions by the 
Canton of Zug, to submit an initiative to the Federal Council on July 7, 2014. This initiative aimed to safe￾guard cantonal sovereignty over electoral law by amending the Federal Constitution to grant cantons the 
authority to determine their electoral systems and district boundaries. As the issue remained unresolved, 
Cornerstones during the reform process in the Canton of Uri September 23, 2012 Vote on a popular initiative calling for the (re)introduction of

the Cantonal Council of Uri instructed the Government Council to suspend the revision bill pending clarity 
at the federal level.53 On December 14, 2018, the National Council rejected the constitutional amendment 
bill by 90 votes to 103 (with 3 abstentions). Consequently, with the upcoming 2020 cantonal elections, new 
legal foundations needed to be established promptly. 
In response to the FSC ruling, the Government Council conducted a consultation process on the electoral 
law, engaging political parties and municipalities. All respondents supported the introduction of bipropor￾tional apportionment in municipalities using PR. However, the CVP, FDP, parts of the SVP, and the Mu￾nicipal Councils of Attinghausen, Flüelen, Schattdorf, and Seedorf advocated extending the majoritar￾ian/plurality voting system to municipalities with three and four seats (Attinghausen, Flüelen, Seedorf, and 
Silenen). They argued that, due to the small population size and the minimal influence of party affiliation, 
the party dimension was less significant, and local parties had a low presence. They also highlighted that the 
small number of seats posed a high risk of distorting results due to seat reversals necessary for maintaining 
overall proportionality. Thus, they proposed that plurality/majority voting should be used for municipali￾ties with three or four seats, while biproportional apportionment would apply to those with five or more 
seats. Despite these recommendations, the Government Council, in its January 8, 2019, statement, rejected 
extending majoritarianism to municipalities with three and four seats, citing both substantive reasons and 
the increased risk of litigation. 
In contrast to the Government Council, the Judicial Commission supported extending majority/plurality 
voting to municipalities with three and four seats, incorporating this into its draft bill. Consequently, there 
were two reform proposals: one from the Government Council, advocating for biproportional apportion￾ment across all PR municipalities, and another from the Judicial Commission, which also endorsed bipro￾portional apportionment but limited it to municipalities with five or more seats. The primary difference 
between the proposals was the scope of the new seat-allocation method. 
On February 13, 2019, parliament debated the future apportionment method in a single reading.54 The FDP 
and CVP supported the Judicial Commission’s draft, arguing that it better addressed the interests of Uri’s 
municipalities and had a lower litigation risk, as the FSC had previously upheld the mixed system. However, 
not all members of these parties were fully supportive. An FDP deputy expressed concern that the bipro￾portional method could lead to a situation where the party with the most votes in a PR district might not 
secure a seat, thereby lacking parliamentary representation. Another FDP member advocated for the Gov￾ernment Council’s proposal, which was seen as legally safer. Similarly, within the CVP, there was criticism 
of the biproportional method, with a representative acknowledging its imperfections but deeming it the best 
available option. 
On February 13, 2019, the debate on the apportionment method highlighted stark contrasts in parliamen￾tary positions. The SP and Greens supported the Government Council’s draft, criticizing the Judicial Com￾mission’s proposal to extend majoritarianism to additional municipalities. They argued that this extension 
was an unjustified attempt by the bourgeois parties to consolidate their power, undermining electoral equity

and political efficacy. Christoph Schillig, MP from the SP, emphasized the importance of maintaining pro￾portional representation to ensure that every vote is meaningful, and that the cantonal parliament accurately 
reflects political forces: 
“This agenda item is by far the most important since I am part of the Council. The right to vote is an enor￾mously important topic. (…) With the proposal of the Judicial Commission, I would in fact be deprived of

In the debate on February 13, 2019, concerning electoral reform in the Canton of Uri, the SP and Greens 
expressed strong support for the Government Council’s proposal, which advocated for the implementation 
of the biproportional seat allocation method in municipalities with five or more seats. This method was 
viewed as essential for ensuring fair representation and maintaining electoral equity. The SP and Greens 
also shared the Government Council’s concerns about the potential for legal challenges. They feared that 
adopting the Judicial Commission’s draft, which extended majoritarian voting to municipalities with three 
and four seats, might not withstand scrutiny from the FA. They argued that such a reform could leave the 
canton without a constitutionally compliant electoral system and require a new reform process. 
The SVP, on the other hand, was divided on which draft should be prioritized. A narrow majority within 
the SVP favored the Government Council’s draft. They referenced the previous popular vote against a return 
to canton-wide majoritarianism, stressing that this decision should be respected. Concerns about the possi￾bility of legal challenges and the risk of the reform being overturned were also significant for the SVP. One 
SVP representative warned that extending majoritarianism could jeopardize the diversity of the party land￾scape, potentially benefiting established parties at the expense of smaller or new ones. While the arguments 
of the minority within the SVP supporting the Commission’s draft were not detailed, it is likely that their 
reasoning aligned with that of the CVP and FDP, which had advocated for extending majoritarianism to 
smaller municipalities. 
Despite these internal differences, all parties agreed on the need for electoral reform and supported the in￾troduction of the biproportional method for proportional representation municipalities. The consensus was 
that the Government Council’s draft, despite its limitations, offered a more stable and legally secure path 
forward compared to the Judicial Commission’s proposal, which risked further legal complications and po￾tential invalidation of the electoral system. 
In the detailed consultation process, a decisive vote determined the priority between the two reform drafts. 
The Commission’s draft, which proposed extending majoritarian voting to municipalities with three and 
four seats while applying biproportional apportionment to larger municipalities, emerged victorious. It was 
favored by 45 votes to 16 for the electoral law and 47 votes to 14 for the constitutional amendment. Subse￾quent to this decision, both drafts were subject to final votes, which saw broad approval from the deputies, 
as illustrated in Figure 25 and in Figure 26.

2.4.9 Canton of Graubünden 
The cantonal legislature of Graubünden, which con￾sists of 120 members, transitioned to PR in 2022. Prior 
to this, Graubünden, along with Appenzell Inner￾rhoden, was among the last cantons in Switzerland to 
use majority voting for parliamentary elections. The 
electoral system in Graubünden is highly fragmented, 
with 39 constituencies, making it the most divided 
canton for cantonal parliamentary elections. Seats are 
now allocated using the biproportional method, which 
includes a majority clause applicable to the 20 single￾member constituencies. Graubünden’s shift marks not 
only its adoption of a new PR-based electoral system 
but also its distinction as the sole canton among those 
that reformed to transition from a purely majority/plu￾rality voting system. 
Despite Graubünden’s historical adherence to majoritarianism, its party system has exhibited significant 
dynamism, as illustrated in Figure 27. The introduction of PR in 2022 did not result in the election of any 
new parties to the cantonal parliament, which is not surprising given the brief interval of only one year be￾tween the adoption of PR and its first implementation. This period is generally too short to observe substan￾tial changes in party system dynamics. 
Figure 27: Parties’ seat-shares in parliamentary elections in the Canton of Graubünden, 1977-2022.

August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
69 
Nevertheless, the electoral reform has 
had an immediate effect on the distribu￾tion of seats, reflecting broader electoral 
trends beyond the reform itself. Notably, 
the decline in the SVP’s seat-share be￾tween the 2006 and 2010 elections can be 
attributed to its rebranding and subse￾quent split in federal politics in 2008. The 
SVP’s offshoot, the BDP, eventually 
merged with the CVP to form the centrist “Die Mitte” in 2021. 
Before delving into the reform process, it is essential to understand the context in which the electoral reform 
in Graubünden took place. Unlike other Swiss cantons that have undergone electoral reforms, Graubün￾den’s reform represented the most significant departure from the existing electoral system. Located in the 
Alps, Graubünden is Switzerland’s largest canton by area. Its rugged alpine terrain has led to a dispersed 
settlement pattern, contributing to the development of distinct language cultures in its remote valleys. As a 
result, political identities in Graubünden are deeply rooted in territorial affiliations, making the territorial 
dimension a critical aspect of the canton’s politics. 
This emphasis on territorial representation is pervasive in Graubünden’s political sphere. There is a strong 
belief among both the political elite and the general population that ensuring territorial representation is 
crucial for maintaining the delicate political balance between the various regions of the canton. This belief 
is reflected in the structure of Graubünden’s electoral system, which includes 20 single-member constitu￾encies. Many of these constituencies, particularly those in more peripheral regions, benefit significantly 
from the legal guarantee of a parliamentary seat, even though they would not be entitled to one based purely 
on population numbers. 
This seat guarantee is a political concession to the more remote regions of the canton, ensuring their political 
inclusion despite their smaller population sizes. In this context, the guarantee of parliamentary seats in even 
the smallest constituencies is considered more important than strict adherence to principles of electoral 
equity. 
The reluctance to depart from majoritarian rule in Graubünden, coupled with concerns about altering the 
constituency structure, stemmed from fears that such changes could weaken the entrenched protection of 
local minorities. As a result, all eight previous attempts to introduce a PR-based system for cantonal parlia￾mentary elections failed (Rathgeb & Lanz 2023: 60-62). However, these numerous reform attempts also in￾dicate that there had been a prolonged and advanced public debate on electoral law and districting issues in 
the canton. 
Despite the continuing importance of constituencies as guarantors of local representation, the majoritarian 
system began to be seen as outdated. Over time, there was a growing public desire for a “fairer” electoral 
system. This shift was driven by increasing disparities between party strengths in PR-based federal elections 
to the National Council and the majoritarian system used for cantonal elections to the Grand Council (ibid. 
2023: 64-66). These discrepancies were interpreted as signs of the distorting effects of the majoritarian sys￾tem, leading to doubts about whether the composition of the Grand Council accurately reflected the politi￾cal preferences of the electorate (Ehrat & Eigenmann 2018: 185). 
Cornerstones during the reform process in the Canton of Graubünden July 29, 2019 Ruling of the FSC that the cantonal parliamentary electoral sys￾tem is incompatible with the Federal Constitution. 
February 15, 2021 First parliamentary reading on the electoral reform. 
February 16, 2021 Second parliamentary reading on the electoral reform. 
June 13, 2021 Popular vote on the reform bill. A vast majority of the voters 
supported the constitutional amendment. 
Table 23: Key reform stages in the Canton of Graubünden.

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
70 
Following the last unsuccessful attempt to reform the cantonal electoral system in 2008, the SVP launched 
a popular initiative in 2009 titled “For fairer elections to the Grand Council”. The initiative aimed to intro￾duce a unidimensional PR system for all electoral districts with more than one seat. The fact that the SVP, a 
party generally restrained about electoral reforms, spearheaded this initiative is indicative of the growing 
and widespread dissatisfaction with the majority voting system in Graubünden.56 This movement for reform 
reflected a broader sentiment among the electorate, who increasingly viewed the existing system as failing 
to ensure fair representation of their political preferences. 
In response to the government’s determination of the number of deputies to be elected in each constituency 
for the 2018-2022 legislative period on September 12, 2017, 54 individuals and five political parties active in 
Graubünden filed a joint appeal against the decision with the FSC on September 18, 2017. The complainants 
argued that the majority voting system used for the election of the Grand Council violated Articles 8 (equal 
rights) and 34 (political rights) of the Federal Constitution. They requested that the competent cantonal 
authorities be directed to establish a legal framework for an electoral system that would conform to the Fed￾eral Constitution, to be implemented in time for the 2022 parliamentary elections. 
In its decision of July 29, 2019 (BGE 145 I 259), the FSC partially upheld the appeal against the existing 
electoral system in Graubünden. However, the Court stopped short of declaring the majoritarian system 
fundamentally unconstitutional. Instead, it provided a nuanced assessment, focusing on specific districts 
and the disproportionality caused by the current system. 
Firstly, the Court identified the district of Avers as problematic due to its extremely small population of 160 
inhabitants. Avers fell far short of the required representation figure by more than eight times, a discrepancy 
that the Court found unjustifiable under the principle of electoral equality. The Court suggested that Avers 
could be merged with neighboring constituencies to remedy this distortion. 
Secondly, the Court examined the applicability of the majoritarian system in the canton’s most populous 
districts – Chur (20 seats), Fünf Dörfer (12), Oberengadin (8), Rhäzüns (7), Davos (6), and Ilanz (6). It con￾cluded that there were no valid reasons to maintain majoritarian rule in these districts due to their size and 
the significant impact on electoral fairness. Conversely, for the remaining, less populous constituencies, the 
Court deemed plurality or majority voting as justified and consistent with the constitutional requirements. 
While the Court did not provide a detailed prescription for how the Graubünden authorities should reform 
the electoral system for the Grand Council, it did highlight potential adjustments, such as the merger of very 
small districts like Avers with adjacent ones and a reconsideration of the voting system in larger constituen￾cies.57 This left the cantonal authorities with the responsibility to create a revised electoral system that would 
comply with the Federal Constitution and address the specific issues identified in the Court’s ruling. 
56 The SVP initiative was subsequently invalidated by the Grand Council in its session of April 18, 2011, by 109 votes 
to 4, as it conflicted with provisions of the Federal Constitution. Just six months after the unsuccessful reform at￾tempt, the SVP, together with the GPS, GLP, SP, EDU, and EVP, submitted a new initiative “For fair elections”, de￾manding, among other things, the adoption of PR. However, this attempt failed as well – this time at the ballot box in 
the vote of March 3, 2013. 56% of the voters opposed the initiative. 
57 The Court was extremely careful not to give the impression that it was imposing rules on the cantonal authorities 
regarding the organization of cantonal elections. It merely added that if the Canton of Graubünden wishes to retain 
the pure majoritarian method for elections to the Grand Council, this would require a revision of the constituency 

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
71 
The FSC ruling, which declared the allocation of half of the parliamentary seats unconstitutional, initiated 
a new electoral reform process in Graubünden. In response to this ruling, the Grand Council tasked the 
Government Council during the December 2019 session with developing a proposal to revise the cantonal 
electoral system. To this end, the government launched a consultation process involving all political parties 
and municipalities. The consultation presented nine different models, ranging from purely majoritarian 
systems to mixed models combining proportional and majoritarian elements, as well as a biproportional 
model. 
Although the government expressed a preference for the biproportional model, the majority of the con￾sulted municipalities and the three strongest political parties, which together held over 50% of the seats in 
the legislature, favored a mixed electoral model. In light of these responses, the Government Council de￾cided to also explore the mixed model further in the reform process, expressing readiness to support it if it 
garnered sufficient parliamentary backing. The draft law based on the mixed model proposed dividing the 
canton into 43 electoral districts, with 41 districts electing representatives through majority voting and the 
two most populous districts, Chur and Fünf Dörfer, through unidimensional PR.58
Simultaneously, the SP had a pending initiative that proposed reducing the size of the parliament from 120 
to 90 seats and restructuring the constituency boundaries. The SP strategically offered to withdraw this ini￾tiative on the condition that the biproportional model was adopted in the parliamentary deliberations.59
Given that a similar SP initiative had only narrowly missed approval a few years earlier, securing 49.1% of 
the vote, this maneuver allowed the SP to significantly influence the reform process. 
The parliamentary deliberations on the revision of the cantonal constitution began on February 15, 2021, 
and were organized into four parts: an opening debate, a detailed discussion of the mixed model, a detailed 
discussion of the biproportional model, and the final vote. The first two parts were completed on the first 
day of the session, while the discussions and final vote on the biproportional model took place on the fol￾lowing day. 
During the opening debate on the electoral reform, it became evident that apart from the CVP, all political 
factions supported the biproportional model, though this support was more of a political compromise than 
a result of strong conviction. The FDP, for example, highlighted the importance of retaining the existing 
constituency structure as a significant argument in favor of the biproportional model. They appreciated that 
the majority clause, which guarantees the strongest list in a district at least one seat, would maintain a ma￾joritarian element within the system. This viewpoint was shared by the BDP, which saw the continued par￾liamentary representation of the numerous single-member constituencies as a key criterion for approving 
the new electoral law. Similarly, the SVP backed the compromise proposal, seeing it as a way to end the 
prolonged struggle over electoral reform. The SP, despite securing many of its primary goals, was still com￾pelled to make political concessions to ensure broader support for the reform. 
structure. As conceivable solutions, the Court mentioned the division of the largest constituencies into smaller con￾stituencies. Alternatively, a mixed system in which PR applies in the larger constituencies could be introduced (cf. 
BGE 145 I 259, p. 280). 
58 Of these 43 constituencies, 34 correspond to the previous districts. The constituencies of Ilanz, Rhäzüns, Oberen￾gadin and Davos must be divided into nine new constituencies, and the district of Avers must be integrated into the 
Rheinwald constituency (cf. report of the Government Council of August 24, 2020).
