The CVP, however, remained the sole faction advocating for the mixed model. They argued that previous 
votes on the introduction of PR had been rejected by voters, and they believed that the people of Graubün￾den preferred to maintain a majoritarian system. The CVP contended that the mixed model would best 
accommodate this preference, representing the smallest possible change to the status quo that still complied 
with the FSC’s guidelines. 
The second reading, held the following day, focused on a detailed deliberation of the biproportional model 
and the final vote on both models. A significant point of contention was the proposal to introduce a quorum. 
While most MPs supported a 3% threshold to prevent excessive fragmentation of political forces and to 
ensure parliamentary effectiveness, there were dissenting voices. Critics argued that a parliament should 
reflect societal political dynamics as closely as possible, and some were concerned that a quorum would 
disadvantage independent candidates, who are becoming more numerous and lack party affiliation. This 
concern was particularly relevant in single-member constituencies, where strong candidates might be ex￾cluded despite the majority clause being designed to ensure their representation.60 Some MPs, including 
those from the CVP, expressed concerns about this potential exclusion, while others, particularly from the 
FDP, argued that the 3% threshold was too low and proposed raising it to 5% to ensure that elected parties 
could form factions in the parliament. 
Ultimately, the proposal for a canton-wide 3% quorum, as recommended by the responsible commission 
and the government, was supported by a significant majority, with 84 votes in favor, 28 against, and no 
abstentions. Another contentious issue was whether to offer voters a choice between the biproportional and 
mixed models in a variant vote. However, the majority decided against this and opted to propose only the 
biproportional model in the referendum, as illustrated in Figure 28. 
Figure 28: Positions on the ballot to propose only the biproportional model for popular referendum, Canton of 
Graubünden.
The constitutional and legislative amendments were overwhelmingly adopted in the final vote, with 85 and 
84 votes in favor, respectively, and no votes against, though there were 32 abstentions. The constitutional 
amendments were later put to a popular vote on June 13, 2021, and were approved by a large margin, with 
79% in favor. The legal amendments were not subjected to a popular vote.

2.5 Cantons with unsuccessful reform attempts 
2.5.1 Canton of Obwalden 
The canton of Obwalden, a rural area centrally located 
in Switzerland, ranks among the smallest cantons in 
terms of population. Since 1990, the number of seats 
in its cantonal parliament has been fixed at 55, in con￾trast to earlier practices where the size of the legislature 
varied, with one seat allocated per 500 inhabitants. PR 
has been used for parliamentary elections in Obwalden 
since 1986, with elections conducted within the can￾ton’s seven municipalities. According to Article 66(2) 
of the cantonal constitution, each municipality is guar￾anteed a minimum of four seats, though this provision 
has practically affected only Lungern, the smallest municipality, which received an additional seat due to 
this entitlement. 
Obwalden was among the last Swiss cantons to transition from a majoritarian to a proportional representa￾tion system. Prior to the end of the 20th century, the CVP enjoyed an absolute majority in the cantonal 
parliament. However, since the turn of the millennium, the CVP’s seat share has significantly decreased, 
with the SVP gaining a substantial number of those seats. The current parliamentary representation includes 
not only the SVP but also the SP, FDP, CSP, and more recently, the GLP, as depicted in Figure 29.

74 
Obwalden presents a unique case for electoral system analysis due to its partial non-compliance with FSC 
rulings. In the 2022 elections, four of its seven constituencies each had fewer than nine seats, exceeding the 
permissible 10% quota for effective representation as defined by the Court. This discrepancy highlights a 
potential contradiction between Obwalden’s electoral system and federal case law. 
As early as 2005, influenced by the FSC’s landmark decision of 2002 regarding the Zurich City Council, the 
Obwalden government assessed its electoral system and identified that some constituencies were too small. 
The government of Obwalden defended its small and varied constituencies by citing their historical origins 
and strong local affiliations, emphasizing that the cantonal constitution, ratified by the FA in 1990, should 
take precedence over FSC rulings. The government believed that its electoral law complied with constitu￾tional requirements and could withstand legal scrutiny. 
However, since the government’s 2005 assessment, the FSC has increasingly reprimanded similar deviations 
from PR. The Court has acknowledged that deviations may be justified by historical, federal, cultural, reli￾gious, linguistic, or ethnic factors intended to protect local minorities. Nonetheless, it has stressed that such 
deviations must be extremely well justified, with the burden of proof becoming heavier as the deviation from 
PR increases.61 The only exception granted was to the Canton of Valais in 2004 (BGE 131 I 85), which un￾derscored the Court’s restrictive approach. 
In response to this stringent interpretation, the Obwalden government re-evaluated its electoral system. In 
2012, it conducted a consultation with all cantonal political parties to gauge the political feasibility of various 
measures designed to align the electoral system with federal constitutional standards. 
The majority of political parties in Obwalden opposed both altering the constituency structure (whether by 
creating a single constituency, redesigning existing ones, or forming constituency associations) and chang￾ing the apportionment method (whether to a majoritarian system or a biproportional approach). This re￾sistance led the Government Council to conclude that the current electoral system had broad support 
among the political elite. The Council maintained its belief that the existing legislation would stand up to 
scrutiny by the FSC.62
Moreover, at the time, several neighboring cantons – Zug, Schwyz, Uri, and Nidwalden – were undergoing 
their own electoral reforms, and it was uncertain whether their proposed changes would meet federal judi￾cial standards. Given these factors, the Government Council decided that pursuing electoral reform in Ob￾walden was not advisable at that moment. 
In subsequent rulings on cantonal electoral systems, the FSC tightened its stance on permissible deviations 
from the 10% benchmark for implicit district quorums. The Court ruled in the Canton of Valais (BGE 140 
I 107) that deviations from this benchmark were no longer acceptable, even with substantial historical, fed￾eral, cultural, linguistic, or religious justifications. It emphasized that maintaining small constituencies to 
61 Cf. BGE 136 I 376 p. 384, BGE 129 I 185 p. 190, BGE 131 I 74 p. 79, BGE 131 I 85 p. 87. 
62 Source: Media release of November 7, 2012, on the decision of the Government Council of October 23, 2012. Con￾trary to the Government Council’s assessment, however, it is more likely that the FSC would rule that the current 
electoral system is unconstitutional upon appeal (cf. Fuhrer 2018: 72-73).

protect local minorities could still align with 
accurate representation of party strengths 
through measures such as combining small 
districts for seat allocation or adopting the 
biproportional apportionment method, 
which had been effectively implemented in 
various cantons.63
This evolving legal context prompted Max 
Rötheli, a member of the SP, along with five 
co-signatories, to submit a query to the Gov￾ernment Council on May 28, 2015, seeking 
an evaluation of the need for electoral reform. 
In its response on August 18, 2015, the Government Council cited pending cantonal initiatives from Uri 
and Zug, which sought greater cantonal autonomy in electoral law and amendments to the federal consti￾tution. The government referred to the FSC’s decision regarding the Canton of Appenzell Ausserrhoden 
(BGE 140 I 394) to highlight that the legal clarity on majoritarian elections was still evolving. The govern￾ment argued that the current electoral system was politically uncontroversial and not comparable to the 
more extreme cases in Schwyz and Nidwalden. It concluded that the “residual risk” of potential legal chal￾lenges was acceptable.64
However, on October 22, 2020, Rötheli and seven co-signatories introduced a parliamentary motion advo￾cating for the adoption of the biproportional apportionment method. This followed the rejection of can￾tonal initiatives from Uri and Zug by the National Council in 2018, which had sought increased cantonal 
autonomy in electoral law. The motioners argued that the FSC’s rulings and the National Council’s deci￾sions reinforced the view that Obwalden’s electoral system violated federal law. They emphasized that the 
biproportional method, which preserves the existing district structure while ensuring proportional repre￾sentation, had been successfully implemented in other cantons. 
Acknowledging the updated legal framework and the legislative confirmation of the FSC’s case law, the 
Government Council, albeit reluctantly, recognized the necessity for reform. It recommended to the Can￾tonal Council the adoption of the biproportional method as proposed.65
The Cantonal Council considered the motion for biproportional representation on January 28, 2021. Only 
the SP faction supported the motion, arguing that the biproportional method aligns with the FSC’s rulings 
and represents only a minor adjustment to the electoral system. The SP also deemed a mixed electoral sys￾tem unsuitable due to potential legal uncertainties. Opposition came from the SVP, which found the bipro￾portional procedure overly complex and viewed the Court’s rulings as an infringement on cantonal auton￾omy. The CVP argued that proportional representation distorts voter preferences in Obwalden, where per￾sonal candidate attributes are significant. potentially increasing its chances of passing in the Cantonal Council. Nonetheless, with the FDP and CVP 
holding a combined majority, they could block this latest reform effort. The ongoing risk of a FSC appeal,

2.5.2 Canton of Bern 
In 2002, the Grand Council of Bern was reduced from 
200 to 160 members through a popular vote. This re￾duction was accompanied by a revision of the electoral 
district structure, consolidating the previous 27 con￾stituencies into eight larger ones. A second reform in 
2010 increased the number of constituencies to nine. 
Elections are conducted using unidimensional pro￾portional representation (PR) within these nine dis￾tricts. The 2022 elections in Bern were highly compet￾itive, with nearly 14 candidates per seat. 
Bern is a bilingual canton with German and French as official languages. The cantonal constitution ensures 
protection for the French-speaking minority. The Bernese Jura constituency is allocated twelve seats, and 
proportional representation is guaranteed for the French-speaking population of the Biel-Seeland constitu￾ency (Art. 73(3) of the cantonal constitution). Additionally, the Bernese Jura is assured one seat in the seven￾member government (Art. 84(2)).66
Figure 31: Parties’ seat-shares in parliamentary elections in the Canton of Bern, 1978-2022. The seat-share of “Die 
Mitte” (merger of CVP and BDP in 2021) is subsumed under “CVP” for the year 2022.

overrepresented in parliament and government, has been called into question. It is likely that the special constitu￾tional status of the Bernese Jura will be renegotiated.

The Bernese Jura, a region with significant 
linguistic and cultural diversity, has its own 
regional parliament, the Bern Jurassic 
Council, established in 2006. This body, alt￾hough possessing limited decision-making 
authority, focuses on promoting local cul￾ture and aligning political interests within 
the region. It helps the Bernese Jura to man￾age its unique political dynamics more ef￾fectively, despite the region’s diverse politi￾cal landscape and low electoral volatility, where no single party or political camp dominates the Grand 
Council. 
Unlike Obwalden, the electoral regulations in Bern have consistently adhered to federal law, and there have 
been no judicial challenges. Reforms in 2002 and 2010 aligned the electoral districts with the principles of 
PR as mandated by the FSC, ensuring that all districts meet the nine-seat requirement. 
Despite the legal conformity of the Bernese electoral system, there have been internal pressures for reform. 
Since 2007, four parliamentary attempts to implement the biproportional apportionment method have been 
made. The most recent attempt, initiated on December 10, 2019, was supported by a coalition of seven MPs 
and 45 co-signatories. The proposal emphasized that the biproportional method would make the electoral 
process fairer and more representative. However, on May 13, 2020, the Government Council recommended 
rejecting the motion. It cited that both the biproportional and unidimensional methods have their own sets 
of advantages and disadvantages. The Government Council argued that the current unidimensional PR sys￾tem, with its large electoral districts, aligns with FSC jurisprudence and is widely accepted both nationally 
and within the canton. Therefore, the Council maintained its position against adopting the biproportional 
method. 
The Grand Council debated the motion on August 31, 2020. Supporters included the EVP, GLP, EDU, GPS, 
and BDP, who argued that the biproportional method would more accurately reflect the voters’ will and 
promote a more inclusive political environment. They suggested that the reform could stimulate the partic￾ipation of smaller parties and enhance the overall representativeness of the legislative process. 
Opposition came from larger parties, including the FDP, SP, SVP, and smaller groups like the Alternative 
Left (AL) and the Autonomous Socialist Party of Southern Jura (PSA), which are part of the SP parliamen￾tary group. These opponents contended that the current system was already compliant with FSC case law 
and that the proposed changes were neither legally necessary nor urgent.67 A representative of the FDP ar￾gued that the reform was not compelling and did not address any immediate or material issues. 
The SP’s opposition is particularly notable. Despite generally supporting such reforms in other cantons, SP 
representative Urs Graf criticized the biproportional method as potentially exacerbating existing electoral 
distortions. He argued that allocating seats to fringe parties could lead to inefficiencies and that consolidat￾ing political interests at the electoral level might be preferable. 
67 Source: Minutes of the Grand Council meeting of August 31, 2020, p. 4. Translated from German.

apparentements, could threaten their viability. 
Ultimately, the motion was defeated, with 47 councilors in favor, 98 against, and four abstentions. The fail￾ure of the motion reflects a clear divide between the smaller parties advocating for reform and the larger,

2.5.3 Canton of Thurgau 
The Canton of Thurgau, located in the Swiss midlands 
and bordering Germany to the north, has a Grand 
Council consisting of 130 members elected from five 
constituencies under unidimensional PR. This 
structure emerged from a 2009 reform that 
consolidated the previously eight electoral districts 
into five. This restructuring aimed to modernize the 
constituency framework and align it with federal law 
to avoid potential legal challenges. The political 
landscape in Thurgau is diverse, with the SVP being the dominant party, holding over 30% of the seats since 
the 2000 elections (see Figure 33). 
In parallel with the district reform, there were two notable attempts to introduce biproportional 
apportionment in Thurgau. The first effort occurred on March 12, 2008, when councilors Anita Dähler 
(CVP) and Matthias Müller (EVP) proposed a motion to adopt a “fairer seat allocation procedure” in PR￾based elections. This motion was driven by a desire to improve the fairness of seat distribution within the 
Grand Council.

A subsequent reform effort to amend the apportionment method emerged through a general popular initi￾ative dated March 1, 2010.69 This initiative, titled “Fair Electoral System for the Elections to the Grand Coun￾cil,” was endorsed by the BDP, EDU, EVP, and GPS, as evidenced by their party logos on the signature sheet. 
To maintain clarity, a detailed chronological account of the reform processes is omitted. Instead, the discus￾sion begins with the reform process initiated by the parliamentary motion, followed by an examination of 
the reform process instigated by the popular initiative. 
At the time the motion was submitted, the issue of reforming political and electoral districts was already on 
the agenda but had not yet been resolved. Prior to the reform, the electoral area was divided into eight une￾venly sized districts (see Table 28). The proponents of the motion cited the district of Diessenhofen, which, 
with only four seats, fell significantly short of the 10% threshold. They argued that the existing system re￾sulted in unequal voter influence and numerous “lost” votes in smaller constituencies due to the relatively 
high implicit quorum, which adversely affected the electoral success of smaller parties. To address these 
issues and ensure the constitutionality of the electoral system, they proposed either adjusting the number 
and size of constituencies or adopting the biproportional apportionment formula. 
 
In its statement regarding the motion submitted to the Grand Council on February 24, 2009, the Govern￾ment Council recommended rejecting the proposal. While acknowledging the biproportional method’s 
benefits, such as improved proportionality and enhanced electoral opportunities for smaller parties, the 
government also highlighted significant drawbacks. These included limited guarantees for regional repre￾sentation and the potential for increased political fragmentation. Additionally, the government pointed to 
the concurrent redistricting process, which was already well advanced, as a means to achieve a fairer seat 
allocation. From the Government Council’s perspective, the proposed reform of the seat allocation proce￾dure was therefore deemed unnecessary.

entail a partial revision of the constitution, the bill is put to a vote. A vote is also held if a majority of the deputies do 
not support the initiative (cf. § 26(3) of the cantonal constitution and § 81-83 of the electoral law).

The motion received support from the EVP/EDU and GPS factions, which argued that the biproportional

in convincing other parliamentary members.

2.6 Reform attempts in other cantons 
This subsection provides an overview of biproportional reform attempts in various Swiss cantons that either 
did not come to fruition or are still in progress. Unlike the previously discussed unsuccessful reforms, the 
cases examined here were identified as unfeasible proposals early in the process, each for distinct reasons. 
These include the cases of Lucerne, Fribourg, Basel Stadt, Solothurn, and Vaud. In the case of Basel Land￾schaft, the decision-making process is still ongoing. The following sections offer a concise presentation of 
these individual cases, focusing particularly on the elements related to the apportionment method. The sec￾tion begins with the similar cases of Lucerne and Fribourg, followed by the cases of Basel Stadt, Basel Land￾schaft, Solothurn, and, finally, Vaud. 
2.6.1 Canton of Lucerne 
In Lucerne, a reduction in the size of the cantonal parliament sparked a significant debate over the future 
structure of electoral law. Specifically, in 1998, the legislature was reduced from 170 to 120 seats without 
making corresponding adjustments to the constituencies. This reduction, combined with the 2007 total re￾vision of the cantonal constitution and the FSC’s ruling on the city and Canton of Zurich, heightened the 
urgency of restructuring the electoral territory (Fuhrer 2018: 65). 
During the preliminary discussions on constitutional revision, various models were considered. These in￾cluded proposals for a single cantonal constituency, constituencies with five, eight, or ten members, the use 
of biproportional apportionment, and territorial mergers. Given the political sensitivity surrounding elec￾toral districting, the decision was made to avoid explicitly defining constituencies within the constitution. 
Instead, a minimum of five constituencies and adequate regional representation were specified, leaving the 
detailed division of electoral districts to be addressed later at the legislative level. Lucerne voters approved 
the draft constitution on June 17, 2007. 
Following this, Michael Töngi (GPS), a member of the cantonal parliament, submitted a motion on January 
21, 2008, advocating for the adoption of biproportional apportionment. In response, the Government 
Council suggested converting the motion into a postulate, signaling its intent to consider biproportionality 
during the upcoming legislative process for amending the electoral law. However, the Cantonal Council 
rejected the proposal in both forms. 
On January 25, 2010, the Cantonal Council approved a draft revision related to the territorial organization 
of cantonal parliamentary elections. This revision involved minor adjustments to the electoral law, most 
notably the formation of a constituency association between the neighboring districts of Entlebuch (7 seats) 
and Willisau (16 seats), effectively simulating a joint single constituency. The methodology for determining 
election results mirrored the biproportional process, with super-apportionment at the simulated single con￾stituency level and sub-apportionment at the district level. 
In response to the Council’s decision, the SVP launched a referendum, arguing that the small district of 
Entlebuch, with its long-standing tradition as an autonomous valley, should remain an independent con￾stituency. The SVP also criticized the new seat allocation procedure, claiming it unfairly favored smaller 
parties by lowering electoral thresholds. However, in the referendum held on September 26, 2010, over 70% 
of voters supported the proposed changes to the electoral law. The first elections under the new law took 
place in 2011, and the revised electoral system has remained legally unchallenged since then. 

=== PDF PAGE 96 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
85 
2.6.2 Canton of Fribourg 
In an amendment to the cantonal constitution dated May 16, 2004, several significant changes to the elec￾toral law in Fribourg were introduced, including a reduction in the number of seats in the Grand Council 
from 130 to 110. This downsizing occurred without a corresponding reform of the electoral districts, leading 
to an implicit quorum exceeding 10% in the Glâne constituency (8 seats) and the Vevey constituency (6 
seats). On September 4, 2012, the Cantonal Administrative Court ruled that the legal framework for Grand 
Council elections was inconsistent with the Federal Constitution (Fuhrer 2018: 73f.). 
In response, three different models were thoroughly examined: 
1. biproportional apportionment method 
2. municipal merger of the Glâne and Vevey constituencies 
3. creation of a constituency association between Glâne and Vevey (forming sub-constituencies with 
seat allocation based on the biproportional method) 
The biproportional model faced criticism for its complexity. Major political parties, including the FDP, SP, 
SVP, and CVP, either rejected the biproportional model outright or demanded the introduction of statutory 
quorums up to 7.5% at the cantonal level as a condition for their support. In contrast, smaller parties like 
the CSP, BDP, GLP, and GPS supported biproportionality without statutory quorums. 
The second proposal, which involved politically merging the Glâne and Vevey municipalities, was almost 
unanimously rejected by both the affected municipalities and the broader political landscape. 
After two rounds of consultation, a comprehensive legal analysis, and two extensive parliamentary deliber￾ations, the third model emerged as the most viable alternative. This model was ultimately approved by the 
Grand Council on October 7, 2014, with 85 council members voting in favor, none opposing, one abstain￾ing, and 24 absent. The decision mirrored a similar approach previously adopted in Lucerne to address the 
issue of undersized constituencies. These changes were first implemented in the 2016 cantonal parliamen￾tary elections. 
2.6.3 Canton of Basel Stadt 
In contrast to the prevalent electoral practices in several other Swiss cantons that use a one-dimensional PR 
system, specifically the d’Hondt/Hagenbach-Bischoff method, which rounds down non-integer seat alloca￾tions, the Canton of Basel-Stadt employs the Sainte-Laguë method, which uses standard rounding. Notably, 
the smallest electoral district, Bettingen, benefits from a constitutional seat guarantee and uses a plurality 
voting system, making Basel-Stadt one of the few Swiss cantons with a mixed electoral system. 
In 2005, the reduction in the size of the Grand Council from 130 to 100 seats prompted discussions about 
electoral reform in Basel-Stadt. This reduction did not alter the constituency structure, leading to an in￾creased implicit electoral threshold within constituencies. However, except for the single-member district 
of Bettingen, the resizing did not result in any of the other four constituencies exceeding the 10% threshold 
(Beriger 2018: 148-152). 
In 2009, Remo Gallacchi, a CVP member of the Grand Council, and co-signatories proposed a parliamen￾tary motion to introduce the biproportional apportionment method for cantonal parliamentary elections. 
A Special Commission was appointed to examine this proposal. In 2011, the Commission presented its re￾port, which, while recognizing the advantages of biproportionality in accurately converting votes into seats, 

=== PDF PAGE 97 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
86 
ultimately rejected its adoption due to the complexity and potential paradoxes in seat distribution. Instead, 
the Commission recommended the following reforms: 
- Maintaining the one-dimensional PR system with standard rounding of seat quotients. 
- Retaining the existing constituency structure. 
- Reducing the district-based quorum from 5% to 4%, applicable individually to each constituency. 
- Abolishing both horizontal (between party lists) and vertical (within party lists) apparentements. 
On June 8, 2011, the Grand Council approved all these proposed changes, with the statutory quorum being 
a particularly contentious issue. The new electoral procedure was first implemented in the 2012 Grand 
Council elections. In 2016, the Grand Council decided to abolish the statutory quorum, a move that was 
approved by voters the following year (Beriger 2018: 150) 
2.6.4 Canton of Basel Landschaft 
The electoral system for cantonal parliamentary elections in Basel Landschaft is organized into twelve con￾stituencies, each allocated between six and ten seats, with a minimum guarantee of six seats per constituency. 
These twelve constituencies are grouped into four electoral regions, each with 17 to 30 seats, ensuring that 
the implicit quorum stays below the 10% threshold across all regions, thus complying with federal legal re￾quirements. 
In 2015, Klaus Kirchmayr, a GPS councilor, initiated an interpellation requesting the cantonal government 
to examine the comparative effects of the current electoral system versus a biproportional model. The fol￾lowing year, the Government Council responded without identifying any need for reform. 
Kirchmayr later introduced a procedural postulate advocating for the formation of a special commission to 
thoroughly review the electoral law. This commission would develop various reform proposals aimed at 
improving the proportional representation of party strengths within the Cantonal Council, while preserving 
the constituencies as local representation guarantors. Although the Cantonal Council rejected this proposal 
on October 17, 2019, the concern it raised gained traction. Parliament instructed the parliamentary execu￾tive board to collaborate with external experts in devising alternatives to the current electoral system, focus￾ing on achieving greater proportionality while maintaining the local connection of elected representatives. 
On July 1, 2022, the parliamentary executive board submitted a report recommending a comprehensive 
electoral system reform, favoring a canton-wide biproportional apportionment system while retaining con￾stituencies. The proposal included replacing the six-seat guarantee with a one-seat guarantee. On September 
15, 2022, the parliament approved this proposal with a vote of 45 to 32, instructing the government to draft 
a revision of the electoral law in line with the proposed biproportional method. Apart from the SVP and 
FDP, all other factions supported the electoral reform. The matter is currently pending with the cantonal 
government. 
2.6.5 Canton of Solothurn 
In the Canton of Solothurn, the reduction of the cantonal parliament from 144 to 100 seats led to significant 
deliberation on the design of electoral legislation. A popular initiative, aimed at reducing the size of the 
parliament and proposing a maximum of seven constituencies of comparable magnitude, was approved by 
voters on March 3, 2002. However, the Cantonal Council’s counterproposal, which retained the five existing 

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
87 
administrative districts as constituencies despite their varying sizes (ranging from 13 to 29 seats), was ulti￾mately endorsed by the voters. 
In 2011, Markus Knellwolf from the GLP initiated an investigation into the potential adoption of the bipro￾portional apportionment method for cantonal parliamentary elections. The following year, a coalition of 
the CVP, EVP, and GLP requested the Government Council to assess the biproportional method for both 
municipal and cantonal elections through a parliamentary motion. This proposal was partly driven by a 
concurrent motion from the FDP, SP, and SVP seeking to prohibit cross-party apparentements, which could 
significantly impact smaller parties. The proponents viewed biproportionality as a compensatory measure. 
The Government Council, after extensive deliberation, acknowledged the advantages of the biproportional 
method but also pointed out its complexities and potential risks, such as discordant seat allocations and 
party fragmentation. It found the existing constituency structure to be compliant with federal law and rec￾ommended rejecting the motion. After a contentious debate, the Grand Council rejected the motion, with 
support only from the CVP, EVP, GLP, and GPS. Since this rejection, there have been no further attempts 
to reform the electoral system in Solothurn. 
2.6.6 Canton of Vaud 
In the Canton of Vaud, an attempt was made to introduce biproportional apportionment in response to 
concerns about high implicit electoral quorums in smaller constituencies. The Canton of Vaud, along with 
Ticino, is unique in Switzerland for using the Hare-Niemeyer method for seat allocation. As part of a con￾stitutional revision, the number of seats in the Grand Council was reduced from 180 to 150, with the elec￾toral districts reorganized into ten constituencies, three of which include two sub-constituencies. Despite 
some constituencies having only eight seats, which falls below the FSC’s nine-seat minimum guideline, the 
electoral system is considered likely to remain compliant due to district mergers and the accurate represen￾tation achieved by the Hare-Niemeyer method (Beriger 2018: 160-161). 
On June 23, 2009, Bernard Borel of the PST-POP party (“Parti Ouvrier et Populaire”) proposed a parlia￾mentary initiative to introduce biproportional apportionment to address the disproportionately high elec￾toral thresholds in smaller constituencies. However, Borel later withdrew the initiative in favor of a motion 
submitted on November 1, 2009, which also supported maintaining a 5% electoral threshold, which needed 
to be surpassed in at least one constituency. 
This motion was eventually turned into a postulate and debated in the Grand Council on February 8, 2011. 
Despite support from some members, the postulate was rejected. Opponents cited concerns about the com￾plexity and transparency of the proposed system, potential disruptions to the constituency structure, and 
the risk of inconsistent seat allocations across constituencies. Since then, there have been no further attempts 
to implement biproportional apportionment in Vaud.

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
88 
2.7 Parties between strategical maneuvering and idealism? 
In the context of electoral reform, political parties are uniquely positioned as both architects and primary 
stakeholders of electoral laws. This dual role creates strong incentives for strategic behavior, as parties aim 
to maximize their electoral advantages. But are they exclusively guided by strategic considerations or are 
there other factors that moderate their reform positioning? 
This chapter starts with addressing the question whether parties that previously benefited from electoral 
design tend to resist biproportional reform. Conversely, it explores whether parties previously “penalized” 
by electoral regulations are more inclined to strive for comprehensive biproportional reform adoption. If 
parties seek to maximize their share of seats and use their political leverage to twist the “rules of the game” 
in their favor, their actions in decision-making during electoral reform processes will largely hinge on the 
expected improvement or worsening of their position relative to the status quo. According to the first hy￾pothesis, parties advantaged by electoral regulations in force will resist any electoral reforms or elements 
thereof that endanger their advantageous position. Conversely, if the current electoral law adversely affects 
their legislative representation, it is reasonable to expect these parties to push for potentially radical reforms 
to the electoral system. 
Various factors influence the extent of a party’s misrepresentation relative to its vote share, thereby affecting 
the overall proportionality of an electoral system. Key factors include the specific formula used to translate 
votes into seats, the total number of legislative seats, and constituency characteristics such as number, size, 
and shape. Consequently, changes to the seat allocation method and other mechanical features of the elec￾toral system are likely to produce varying distributional effects on different parties, both in direction and 
magnitude (Kedar et al. 2016). 
Understanding a party’s position and behavior during the electoral reform process requires an analysis of 
its electoral performance prior to the reform. Parties that have historically experienced significant misrep￾resentation, with a substantial portion of their votes failing to convert into seats, are likely to support reforms 
that promise to improve their parliamentary representation. In contrast, parties benefiting from the existing 
electoral system are expected to oppose changes that could undermine their current advantages. 
In cantons that underwent biproportional reform, most previously used unidimensional PR (ZH, AG, SH, 
NW, ZG, VS). However, Uri and Schwyz adopted a mixed electoral system (MR in smaller districts and PR 
in larger multi-member districts), while Graubünden exclusively used MR in its former cantonal parliamen￾tary elections. For Graubünden, party representation estimates are not indicated due to the electoral system, 
and for the cantons of Schwyz and Uri, estimates should be viewed with caution. Cantons rejecting bipro￾portional reform (OW, BE, TG) all persist in electing their parliaments using unidimensional PR. Table 30 
forms the basis for estimating the parties’ stances on biproportional reform. It shows the degree and direc￾tion of the competing parties’ legislative misrepresentation for all elections between 1976 and 2023, ex￾pressed in vote-seat disproportionality. To ensure comparability across cantons, these values were stand￾ardized using the total number of parliamentary seats per canton. Further specifics are available in the table’s 
notes. 
The table illustrates the anticipated effect where the big, established parties (cf. columns 1-4) tend to expe￾rience higher levels of overrepresentation, while smaller fringe parties consistently face underrepresenta￾tion. It’s notable that these disproportionalities are considerable, particularly during specific (pre-reform) 
periods and in certain cantons. In the cases of Uri and Schwyz, the electoral systems prior to the reform 

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
89 
featured pronounced majoritarian elements, as MR was (and in the case of Uri still is) used in small constit￾uencies/municipalities, resulting in greater disproportionalities. 
One source of systemic distortions besides the district structures and the electoral system/formula lies in the 
legal provision that allows electoral alliances between different lists, which is provided in most cantons that 
elect their parliaments based on unidimensional PR. Although smaller parties can also benefit from these 
alliances, empirical evidence indicates that they primarily favor larger parties or the dominant partner 
within the alliance (Bochsler 2010b), thereby further perpetuating systemically induced disproportionali￾ties. 
Certain anomalies can be observed, such as the 2006 elections in Zug, where the Alternative Green Party 
(classified as FGA in the table) as part of an apparentement doubled its seats from five to ten. However, this 
was partly at the expense of its coalition partner, the SP. This demonstrates that parties in a marginalized 
position can not only minimize systemic disadvantages through strategic electoral alliances but also leverage 
them to their advantage. Nevertheless, a broader analysis of the data suggests that such anomalies are rela￾tively rare occurrences. 
The successfully reformed cantons generally exhibit a clear “proportional effect”, where the disparity be￾tween votes and seats – after the implementation of the reforms – typically remains within the threshold of 
+/- one parliamentary seat, with few exceptions. The case of Uri, however, warrants discussion, as significant 
distortions persist even after the reform. This can be attributed to the mixed electoral system in place, where, 
as part of the reform, the majoritarian system was extended to four additional municipalities that previously 
used PR. As the results are aggregated across the entire area, including municipalities voting according to 
MR, the overall disproportionality has actually slightly increased following the reform.

Notes: 
˗ Cell values: Each cell represents the difference between a party’s share of seats and its share of votes expressed in 
parliamentary seats. This metric ensures inter-cantonal comparability by considering varying parliament sizes. 
For instance, a 5.0 percentage point difference between seat and vote shares translates to 3 seats in the Nidwalden 
cantonal parliament (with 60 seats), whereas the same variance corresponds to 9 seats in the Zurich cantonal par￾liament (with 180 seats), demonstrating a more substantial effect. Hence, a standardized metric is used that con￾siders varying parliament sizes. Changes in parliament size within certain cantons over time are also considered.

Table 31 provides an overview over the assumed party positions in all cantons concerned.70 In line with their 
parliamentary over-representation, it can be expected that, in many cases, the SP, CVP and SVP and to a 
lesser extent the FDP as established and dominant parties will reject a reform or reform elements aimed at 
a more proportionate representation of the parties. However, there are exceptions observed in certain can￾tons, particularly in central Switzerland, where the trend is not distinctly clear to estimate their stance. Par￾ticularly, the case of the FDP is noteworthy. Despite being a prominent beneficiary of the electoral law for 
an extended period and, in some cantons, being the strongest party at times, it has been experiencing a con￾sistent decline in vote share. This downward trajectory is observable on a national scale as well. In line with 
this electoral decline and the consequential loss of its status as a dominant party, the vote-seat-disparities in 
various cantons exhibit significant volatility, rendering it difficult to estimate the parties’ reform stances. 
The smaller parties including the GPS and GLP, on the other hand, which face a legislative underrepresen￾tation in many cantons, are presumably mostly in favor of reform.

In the next step, the anticipated positioning is juxtaposed with the observed positioning. The assessment of 
the actual positioning draws from the case studies delineated in the previous subchapters. Instances where 
a deviation occurs between the expected and observed positioning are highlighted in the table (cf. table 
notes). As shown in Table 32, there are deviations in seven cases out of a total of 71, with estimations not 
possible due to unclear trends in legislative misrepresentation in 31 cases. Astonishingly, in all seven deviant 
cases, the parties expressed support contrary to expectations – there is no case where a party unexpectedly 
opposed reform. Broadly, the parties’ observed and their expected positions coincide in most cases. How￾ever, drawing the conclusion that expected improvement or deterioration in the parties’ post-reform status 
governs the parties’ positioning would be unwarranted. Looking at the parties’ positioning reveals discern￾ible cross-cantonal patterns that suggest an additional dimension alongside the “winner-loser” antagonism. 
As anticipated, the GPS consistently supported the reform across all cantons under scrutiny, while the SP 
showed higher support than expected. In three cases, the majority favored the implementation of bipropor￾tional reform despite potential seat losses. The SP took a negative stance only in two cases, where the reform 
attempt failed (BE, TG).

94 
In these cantons, it is important to recognize that despite the potential political feasibility of electoral reform, 
there was or is no legal imperative for such changes, as the existing electoral laws already comply with federal 
regulations. Notably, support for reform was not limited to the usual proponents like the GPS and SP; other 
left-leaning parties, including the FGA, also advocated for electoral reform. This highlights that beyond the 
typical “winner-loser” antagonism, reform positions have an ideological dimension, with left-wing parties 
generally pushing for cantonal reforms toward biproportionality. 
The minutes from legislative discussions support this assumption. Representatives from the SP frequently 
underscored their commitment to establishing a more equitable electoral system, even if it might result in 
potential seat losses for their party. Furthermore, smaller parties such as the EVP, BDP, SD, EDU, and the 
“others” group in Uri also demonstrated support for the reform, aligning with the anticipated coalition of 
proponents. 
The SVP generally resisted biproportional reform across most cantons, with notable exceptions in Zurich 
and Uri, where it supported the reform. In Zurich, this support, though unexpected, was partially driven by 
legal pressures that necessitated adjustments to the electoral system to comply with constitutional require￾ments. In Uri, the FSC similarly compelled the canton to reform its electoral law. However, in Uri, the SVP’s 
support for biproportional apportionment was accompanied by successful advocacy for the extension of the 
plurality voting system to municipalities with three and four parliamentary seats. 
Given that the SVP had only recently established itself within Uri’s party landscape and thus held a “fringe 
party” profile during the reform process, their support for the reform is less surprising. This context under￾scores how the party’s relatively weaker position at that time may have influenced its strategic alignment 
with the broader reform efforts. 
The positions of the FDP and CVP exhibit less consistency in their stance on biproportional reform com￾pared to other parties. The expected alignment is largely confirmed for the FDP across all cantons, with the 
notable exception of Aargau, where the party opposed reform despite its eventual adoption. In other cantons 
that successfully implemented biproportional reform, the FDP generally supported it. 
Similarly, the CVP mostly favored reform across the successfully reformed cantons, with Zug being the only 
exception. However, in Aargau, Nidwalden, and Uri, the CVP’s support deviates from expectations. In Aar￾gau, the surprise lies not only in the CVP’s unexpected support for reform but also in the enthusiasm with 
which the party championed biproportional reform. In Nidwalden, the CVP was internally divided: while 
the majority supported the reform, a vocal minority criticized it for its perceived impracticality and com￾plexity, aligning with the SVP in opposing the reform. 
In Uri, the CVP’s support was part of a broader cross-party agreement. After the FSC invalidated key ele￾ments of the cantonal electoral law, reform became a pressing political issue. The biproportional method 
was identified as the preferred solution, though debates arose over its implementation across different mu￾nicipalities. A compromise was reached, proposing a mixed model where smaller municipalities would use 
plurality voting, and larger ones would adopt the biproportional method. This compromise, supported by 
both the FDP and CVP, gained broad parliamentary approval. 
The FDP and, to a greater extent, the CVP are considered key actors in the “consensus-oriented political 
center”, a role that enables them to significantly influence policy and decision-making processes (Schwarz 
2009: 7). Their support or opposition is crucial, particularly in cantons where these parties traditionally hold 

=== PDF PAGE 106 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
95 
a substantial share of parliamentary seats. When they withhold consent, as seen in failed reform attempts, 
securing majority support for electoral reforms becomes challenging, if not impossible. This underscores 
their pivotal role in shaping the outcomes of reform initiatives, particularly in regions where they have tra￾ditionally wielded significant power. 
While left-leaning parties and groups generally exhibit a strong inclination towards electoral reform, the 
SVP, in contrast, typically opposes biproportional reform. However, the motivations behind the SVP’s op￾position may not always be as genuine as they appear. Their arguments might serve as strategic justifications 
aimed at preserving the status quo, which often works to their advantage. Similarly, fringe parties that ad￾vocate for radical reforms may also be driven primarily by utility considerations, with their arguments serv￾ing as a mere facade to justify their stance. 
Given this, it is challenging to definitively ascertain the sincerity of the arguments presented by any party, 
regardless of affiliation. It is plausible that both ideological beliefs and strategic, utility-driven considerations 
influence party positions, with the emphasis on each varying depending on the specific circumstances. This 
observation suggests a broader hypothesis: across the political spectrum, parties weigh ideological and util￾ity-centered considerations differently, depending on the situation and potential conflicts between these 
factors. This dynamic is illustrated in Figure 36, which demonstrates how these mechanisms interplay in 
shaping party positions on electoral reform. 
 Ideology Consonance 
 Utility Dissonance Primacy of Ideology 
 Primacy of Utility 
Supporting or opposing the final reform policy is the most direct and visible way for parties to shape the 
reform process, yet it is not the only avenue. Biproportional reforms often consist of various components, 
allowing parties to exert their political influence on each element individually. Statutory quorums, in par￾ticular, became a focal point of intense political contestation. These quorums were extensively debated in 
the parliaments of nearly every canton. Consequently, the following examination of the more nuanced at￾tempts by parties to influence the reform process will concentrate on statutory quorums, as they provide a 
clearer insight into how parties sought to subtly shape the outcomes of electoral reforms. 
In most cases, decisions regarding statutory quorums aligned with the broader reform process, as evidenced 
in Table 33. However, Aargau stands out as an exception, where no statutory quorums were initially in￾cluded in the original electoral design but were introduced only years later through a subsequent decision. 
In contrast, Schaffhausen, Nidwalden, and Uri have never seen sufficient support for establishing statutory 
quorums. 
The SVP consistently advocated for the introduction of quorums across nearly all cantons. Similarly, the 
FDP and CVP, as established forces in Swiss politics, largely supported the implementation of quorums. 
Conversely, left-wing and smaller parties consistently opposed quorums across the board. Even the SP, 
which might have benefited from statutory quorums in certain contexts, rejected them on ideological 
Figure 36: Theoretical interaction between ideological and utilitarian considerations of reform positioning.

=== PDF PAGE 107 ===

96 
grounds, arguing that such measures conflicted with the core principle of biproportional apportionment – 
namely, to enhance vote-seat proportionality and reduce ineffective votes. An exception to this trend oc￾curred in Zurich, where the SP agreed to a quorum as part of a broader cross-party agreement. 
The CVP’s stance toward statutory quorums, however, was more ambiguous, as it supported them in some 
instances while rejecting them in others. This variability highlights the CVP’s role as a key majority provider: 
whenever a quorum was successfully introduced, it was with the CVP’s support. Conversely, in cases where 
CVP backing was absent, no majority could be secured for the implementation of quorums.

From an inter-cantonal perspective, the emerging patterns align with the assumption that larger, established 
parties tended to support the concurrent implementation of statutory quorums. In some cases, the intro￾duction of a quorum was a prerequisite for their endorsement of the overall reform, effectively serving as a 
political bargaining chip. Conversely, left-wing and fringe parties only reluctantly agreed to quorums as a 
necessary concession to secure broader support for the overall reform. 
Consistent with the findings on overall reforms, left-wing parties exhibited fundamental reservations about 
quorums. Generally, it was these left-wing parties, alongside smaller fringe groups, that championed the 
least distorted reforms possible. This stance aligns with a “natural inclination” for self-preservation among 
smaller parties, as the decision to implement quorums or not can have critical implications for their survival. 
However, left-wing parties sometimes faced the prospect of a potential decline in their electoral standing 
due to the introduction of electoral hurdles. Despite this risk, they consistently opposed quorums, invoking 
principles such as fairness, equality, and integrity, which they prioritized over maintaining electoral ad￾vantages. While other parties were not entirely uninfluenced by such egalitarian concepts, their impact was 
generally more moderate and less frequent. In contrast, right-wing parties, particularly the SVP, and to some 

=== PDF PAGE 108 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
97 
extent the FDP and CVP, prioritized stable governance and operational efficiency over concerns about elec￾toral fairness and party diversity, arguing from a more pragmatic standpoint. 
These findings support the emphasis by earlier authors on the significant role of ideas, ideologies, and values 
in shaping electoral institutions (Blais & Massicotte 1997, Katz 2005, Bowler et al. 2006, Blais & Shugart 
2008, Bowler & Donovan 2013). In their comparative study on the dissemination and underlying patterns 
of electoral formulas across the world, Blais and Massicotte (1997: 117) concluded that “ideas count as much 
as interests” and that “politicians sometimes make choices primarily on the basis of their views about what 
is good, just, or efficient”, rather than solely based on strategic considerations. The adoption of bipropor￾tional apportionment in several Swiss cantons corroborates this perspective. While parties generally advo￾cate for rules that they perceive as advantageous, there is substantial evidence that political idealism also 
significantly influenced the positions taken during the reform processes, albeit with varying levels of em￾phasis among the involved parties. 
2.8 Policy-level implications of process-related conflict structures 
Political parties employ a range of tools and strategies to shape electoral reform designs in line with their 
interests and principles. Consequently, the final design of such reforms often reflects the conflict dynamics 
inherent in the reform process. As discussed in the previous sections, there are situations where parties may 
opt to align with electoral reforms despite holding fundamental criticisms. Rather than outright opposing 
the reform, they may choose to influence it more subtly at the policy level. This approach is particularly 
relevant when parties are either judicially compelled to undertake electoral reform or when they perceive 
that the proponents of the reform are gaining momentum, making the passage of the reform likely despite 
their opposition. In such scenarios, parties may strategically shift from their original position, abandon out￾right opposition, and engage in political bargaining. This proactive approach allows them to address poten￾tial electoral threats strategically rather than passively accept an unfavorable outcome (cf. Walter & Em￾menegger 2019: 66). 
With a nuanced understanding of their electorate and the subtle but powerful effects that electoral regula￾tions can produce, political parties are well-positioned to strategically shape institutional rules during elec￾toral reforms to their advantage, potentially at the expense of rival parties. The more powerful the party and 
the stronger its parliamentary alliances, the greater its capacity to influence policy-making. Considering the 
multiple ways in which parties can influence the reform process, it is essential to closely examine the process 
itself to understand the resulting reform design. 
In this context, this section examines reform elements that emerged from political negotiations, highlighting 
instances where the reform was diluted, leading to distorted designs. Broad cross-party support for a reform 
can often indicate such distortions, while reforms achieved without the support of advantaged parties are 
presumably less prone to distortion. Within the framework of biproportional reforms, institutionally in￾duced distortions of PR can empirically occur through various mechanisms, including: 
- statutory quorums 
- subdivision of the electoral area instead of a single constituency for the super-apportionment 
- retention of elements of the electoral system that distort PR (i.e. mixed electoral system) 

=== PDF PAGE 109 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
98 
These institutional features distort PR by interfering with the optimal conversion of votes into seats.71 Since 
the resulting disproportionalities typically favor larger, more established parties at the expense of smaller 
ones, it is plausible that such parties are motivated to incorporate elements into the reform design that in￾herently distort proportionality (second hypothesis). 
Six of the nine reformed cantons have implemented statutory quorums (ZH, AG, ZG, SZ, VS, GR), with 
most introducing these quorums alongside the biproportional reform. In the remaining three cantons, no 
quorums have been introduced to date (SH, NW, UR). These quorums can be categorized into three groups: 
1. Exclusively global quorums (SZ, GR), 
2. Exclusively local quorums (VS), 
3. Hybrid approach combining global and local quorums (ZH, AG, ZG). 
The electoral impact of quorums depends not only on their size but also on post-election factors, such as 
the geographic distribution of party electorates. Global quorums disadvantage parties that are locally strong 
but weak overall, while local quorums have the opposite effect. The hybrid approach, which employs an 
either/or formulation, helps mitigate the biases associated with the interplay between global and local quor￾ums and the geographic distribution of votes. However, determining the extent of distortion caused by stat￾utory quorums is challenging. The resulting vote-seat disproportionalities are influenced by several factors, 
including the quorum magnitude, vote shares, geographic distribution of party votes (concentration vs. dis￾persion), and the size of the legislature. For simplicity, the following analysis distinguishes between the ab￾sence of statutory quorums, quorums of 5% or less, and quorums exceeding 5%. 
An additional legal deviation from “pure” biproportional apportionment is the subdivision of the electoral 
area into electoral regions for the super-apportionment. Instead of treating the entire canton as a single 
constituency, the electoral area is divided into regions where the super-apportionment is conducted inde￾pendently. Since the implicit quorum is inversely proportional to the total number of seats, any artificial 
division of the electoral area represents a deviation from optimal proportionality. Empirically, Valais is the 
only reformed canton that divides the electoral area into six independent electoral regions. In all other can￾tons, the entire canton serves as the relevant level for seat allocation to parties.72
The retention of majority or plurality voting elements, representing only a partial implementation of bipro￾portional apportionment, is another method through which reform designs can be distorted. Uri is the sole 
canton with a mixed electoral system that combines biproportional and majority/plurality voting elements. 
As part of its reform, plurality/majority voting was even extended to four additional municipalities that had 
previously used PR. 
The “winner-take-one modification”, also known as the majority clause, guarantees the strongest party in a 
district at least one seat (Pukelsheim 2017: 265). This can be seen as a majoritarian tool that distorts optimal 
vote-seat proportionality. However, in all instances where it is applied (SH, ZG, GR), the clause is legally 
designed to prioritize optimal vote-seat proportionality over any seat claims arising from the majority 
clause, thereby overriding such claims in case of conflict. Consequently, empirical evidence indicates that 
71 The “optimal” conversion of votes into seats implies that the overall proportion of seats corresponds perfectly to 
the overall proportion of votes, apart from mathematically unavoidable inaccuracies. 
72 One exception is the Canton of Uri, where all constituencies applying PR form the upper-tier level of seat alloca￾tion.

=== PDF PAGE 110 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
99 
optimal vote-seat proportionality remains unaffected by majority clauses, and they are therefore not con￾sidered a significant source of distortion in PR. 
Table 34 provides a detailed assessment of the distortive elements present in the electoral systems of all suc￾cessfully reformed cantons, evaluating the overall degree of distortion in their reform designs. Two cantons 
(SH, NW) exhibit no distortion in their reform designs. Notably, these two have the smallest cantonal leg￾islatures among the reformed cantons, each comprising 60 seats. The limited size of their legislatures ren￾dered additional statutory quorums unnecessary. 
Most reformed cantons (ZH, AG, ZG, SZ, GR) incorporate a statutory quorum of 5% or lower, which results 
in a slight degree of distortion. Uri’s reform design is moderately distorted due to the retention and expan￾sion of majority/plurality elements within its electoral system. Valais, in contrast, displays the highest level 
of distortion among the reformed cantons. This is due to its implementation of a local statutory quorum of 
8% and the subdivision of its electoral area into six separate regions, with each region independently allo￾cating seats regardless of the results in other regions.

The next step is to assess whether the extent of distortion in biproportional reforms can be linked to the 
conflict structures within the decision-making process, as proposed in Hypothesis 2. Specifically, the hy￾pothesis suggests that more conflictual reform processes are likely to produce less distorted reform policies, 
while consensual processes may lead to greater distortions. 
The data shows that most reform designs exhibit minimal distortion, achieving near-optimal proportional￾ity. This outcome is attributable to the FSC’s strong disciplinary role, which includes intervening in some 
cases already during policy formulation (e.g., in the Canton of Zug) to uphold proportionality standards. 
While the Court has allowed moderate quorums, substantial deviations from proportionality are permitted 
only in exceptional circumstances, and its stance has become stricter over time. In Uri and Valais, where 
distortions are more pronounced, the Court justified these deviations by the unique territorial characteris￾tics of these cantons. Therefore, the legal framework largely accounts for the minor degrees of distortion 
observed. 

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
100 
However, legal constraints are not the only explanatory factor. It is hypothesized that the dynamics of con￾flict during the reform process also influence the degree of distortion. Conflict-ridden processes, where re￾forms face significant opposition from dominant political forces, are expected to result in less distorted re￾forms. Conversely, consensual processes are more likely to produce reforms with distorting elements. 
Referring to Table 35, six out of nine reform processes were marked by significant conflict and were adopted 
despite political resistance. The Canton of Schwyz implemented reform through a popular initiative, show￾casing a direct democratic approach. In Zurich and Uri, reforms were achieved through consensus, with all 
parties supporting the reform bills. Valais presents a complex case. Despite political resistance, the opposi￾tion was mainly directed at comprehensive territorial changes rather than the biproportional reform itself. 
This resistance often crossed party lines and was not spearheaded by dominant political forces. Instead, the 
reform was supported by the main political forces, including the CVP/CSP and the majority of the SVP. 
Given that the need for reform was widely recognized and the dominant forces were proponents, Valais is 
categorized as having a “consensual” reform path.

Table 35: The link between conflict structures during reform processes and the distortedness of reform designs. 
The relationship between conflict structures during the reform process and the distortedness of the reform 
design reveals that more contentious reform paths tend to result in less distorted designs. Table 35 shows 
that in cantons where the reform faced significant resistance from dominant political forces, the resulting 
designs are generally less distorted compared to those in cantons where the reform was supported by key 
political actors. 
An exception is the Canton of Zurich, where cross-party consensus led to only marginally distorted reforms. 
This is partly because Zurich was the first canton to implement biproportional apportionment, and critiques 
of an electoral reform subsided following a leading FSC decision. Additionally, besides statutory quorums, 
no other policies restricting biproportional representation were ever considered in Zurich. 
Empirically, reforms backed by dominant parties often resulted in more distorted designs, featuring high 
statutory quorums, artificial subdivisions of the electoral area, and/or the retention of majoritarian ele￾ments. Conversely, reforms imposed against the will of dominant parties generally exhibited lower levels of 
distortion, challenging the established advantages of larger parties. This pattern suggests that, in addition to 
the FSC’s influence, the conflict structures in political decision-making processes significantly impacted the 
extent of distortion in reform designs. 

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
101 
Larger parties, even when consenting to reform, often used their political influence to introduce elements 
that reduced their potential losses, effectively “watering down” the reforms. In contrast, smaller parties, 
which were negatively affected by systemic distortions, were more motivated to advocate for reforms that 
would enhance proportionality. 
2.9 Conclusion 
Drawing on a comprehensive documentation of biproportional reform trajectories in the Swiss cantons, 
this chapter, structured around two central hypotheses, shows how parties operate within a highly complex 
and sensitive policy field, where they simultaneously act as both lawmakers and principal recipients of the 
outcomes of these laws. 
The analysis confirms that parties that have traditionally benefited from existing electoral laws exhibit a 
tendency to oppose or dilute reform drafts, seeking to preserve their advantageous position. This behavior 
is evident in various cantons where established parties have assisted in implementing statutory quorums, or 
other immanently distortive elements, such as a subdivision of the electoral area, or the retention of majori￾tarian elements. These maneuvers align with H1, showing that parties have a vested interest in maintaining 
their electoral advantages and will resist reforms that threaten their status. On the other hand, parties dis￾advantaged by these laws generally push for more comprehensive reforms to create a more equitable system. 
This strategic calculus underscores the balance of power within electoral politics, where parties strategically 
navigate reform processes to either maximize their relative electoral advantage or to minimize potentially 
adverse effects (Kedar et al. 2016). However, deviations from this pattern also emerge. In some cantons, 
certain parties, notably the Social Democrats, unexpectedly supported reforms despite potential bonus 
losses. This finding challenges a notion that views electoral systems primarily as tools of partisan politics, 
where “the election of a particular voting system is dictated more by partisan interests and political oppor￾tunism than by the principles governing electoral equity” (Cincea 2013: 182). Instead, the findings suggest 
that beyond maximizing strategies, legal requirements, political idealism, and democratic principles also 
impact party positions (cf. Blais & Massicotte 1997). 
H2 posits that reforms imposed against the interests of advantaged parties tend to result in less distorted 
reform designs compared to those achieved through cross-party consensus. The results support this hypoth￾esis by demonstrating that contentious reform processes, marked by significant parliamentary opposition 
or direct democratic initiatives, often lead to less distorted reform outcomes. In contrast, reform processes 
marked by cross-party consensus are more prone to including distortive elements such as higher statutory 
quorums, an artificial subdivision of the electoral area or the retention of majoritarian elements. The main 
explanation for this phenomenon is that cross-party consensus often comes at a political cost (cf. Walter & 
Emmenegger 2019: 71). In the context of electoral reforms, this means that larger, traditionally advantaged 
parties are more likely to support a reform if it includes elements that secure and stabilize their power posi￾tion. In contrast, dissent signals a contentious reform process, where smaller fringe parties may successfully 
unite to advance a reform that aligns with their interests through parliament. In other words, the support of 
major parties carries a political price, and depending on the political configuration, it may be necessary to 
“purchase” their agreement to the overall reform at a significant political cost. 
In conclusion, the study affirms that advantaged parties typically resist reforms to protect their positions, 
while reforms imposed against these parties tend to result in fairer electoral systems. These findings high￾light the intricate nature of electoral reforms, where strategic interests intersect with institutional constraints 

=== PDF PAGE 113 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
102 
and legal mandates. Further research is needed to disentangle the complex dynamics at play and refine the 
understanding of electoral reform processes and their underlying drivers. Comparative studies across di￾verse political contexts can help increase the generalizability of the findings. Moreover, investigating public 
opinion formation in the realm of democratic norms, the interplay between these norms and jurisdiction, 
and their implications in electoral reform processes also provide potential avenues for future research.
