Part III 
*** 
3. Reforms and Their Effects: 
A Comparative View on Different Reform Outcomes

=== PDF PAGE 115 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
104 
3.1 Introduction 
While the second part of the thesis focused on the dynamics of cantonal reform processes towards bipro￾portionality and their effects on reform designs, this part is dedicated to the consequences of the reforms on 
the structures of cantonal political systems. Here, the electoral system is treated as an explanatory variable, 
while various macro-level variables, such as characteristics of the party system, are regarded as dependent 
variables. The research tradition devoted to this “effect-side” of electoral institutions traces its origins to 
Duverger’s seminal work “Les Partis Politiques” (1951) and has since become the dominant approach in the 
study of electoral systems (Charvát 2023: 15-18). Against this background, the primary objective of this third 
part is to assess the extent and magnitude of effects that can be causally attributed to biproportional reforms. 
Despite extensive research on the mathematical properties of the biproportional divisor method (cf. Bach￾arach 1970, Balinski & Demange 1989a, Balinski & Demange 1989b, Gassner 1991, Balinski & Young 2001, 
Pukelsheim 2004, Maier et al. 2010, Demange 2011, Pukelsheim 2017, Pukelsheim 2019), as well as a series 
of simulations (cf. Balinski 2002, Bochsler 2005, Pennisi et al. 2006, Zachariassen & Zachariasen 2006, Ramí￾rez et al. 2008, Oelbermann & Pukelsheim 2011, Hartmann 2023), there is yet no research that looks at the 
effects of biproportional apportionment beyond the distributional dimension. Consequently, the potential 
polity-level ramifications of the new method in the nine Swiss cantons where it currently applies are still 
largely obscure. This represents a surprising oversight given the practical relevance and the public interest 
in this new seat allocation procedure. Therefore, and in view of growing advocacy for the adoption of bi￾proportionality for Swiss National Council elections, this section aims to offer initial insights into the impact 
of this new apportionment method on various characteristics of the cantonal democracies concerned.73 The 
corresponding research question is: To what extent have biproportional reforms affected changes in can￾tonal political landscapes? 
Building on existing research on party system evolution, I argue that the introduction of biproportional 
apportionment has increased party system fragmentation, manifesting in a larger number of effective legis￾lative parties. Furthermore, I expect voter turnout and electoral competition to intensify, which is reflected 
in higher and an increased number of electoral lists and candidacies. However, concerning the expected 
impact on the number of lists, an alternative mechanism is also conceivable. Strategic considerations may 
drive parties to shift away from district-specific voter targeting strategies and opt to compete in all districts 
instead. This shift could potentially lead to a reduction in the overall number of submitted lists and ulti￾mately contribute to a “de-regionalization” or “cantonalization” of the party system. 
The chapter is structured as follows: It begins with an introduction to the core concept of proportionality in 
electoral systems, setting the stage for understanding how biproportional reforms impact vote-seat dispro￾portionality. The chapter then moves to examine the varying levels of proportionality resulting from these 
reforms and their subsequent political effects, including party system fragmentation, electoral turnout, and 
electoral competition. Following a discussion of data sources, variables, and analytical strategies, the chapter 
73 Beyond academic discourse, calls for electoral reform of elections to the National Council are a recurring phenom￾enon in the aftermath of national elections. These calls are typically voiced by parties that stand to benefit from an 
electoral reform. Local and national media outlets frequently cover the topic, publishing articles on the alleged neces￾sity of reform and exploring the potential distributional effects of various reform proposals (e.g., Tagesanzeiger 2023 
or Neue Zürcher Zeitung 2024). The successful implementation of biproportionality at the cantonal level often serves 
as justification for advocating its introduction at the national level.

=== PDF PAGE 116 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
105 
presents results on how biproportional reforms influence these outcomes. It concludes by summarizing key 
findings and reflecting on the broader implications of biproportional reforms for political systems. 
3.2 Proportionality as the fundamental concept in electoral system analysis 
Vote-seat proportionality and the number of parties are among the most frequently employed metrics for 
evaluating electoral systems and democratic competition (Shugart & Taagepera 2017). Despite being widely 
established in the field of electoral research, vote-seat proportionality is not as straightforward a concept as 
it might initially appear. As a metric based on the actual voting results, vote-seat proportionality is not only 
tied to the institutional rules in place but also depends on a set of other factors such as the actual distribution 
of votes between contesting parties (Cincea 2013: 179). It thus combines both endogenous (pre-election) 
and exogenous (post-election) information about an electoral system. The proportional properties of an 
electoral system have been identified as relevant variables in comparative studies across political science 
disciplines, including political economy, civil conflict literature, and comparative politics. For example, they 
have been found to affect the stability of government coalitions, the responsiveness of politics to shifts in 
public opinion, and political behavior (Bochsler 2024: 1). Proportionality thus captures more than certain 
effects of an electoral system. It serves as an important link in a longer causal chain, directly or indirectly 
affecting a wide array of electoral outcomes, thus assuming the role of an intermediate variable. 
The goal of the subsequent subchapter is to show how proportionality intersects with electoral systems. I 
start with a working definition of “proportionality” and then delve into its connection to electoral rules. I 
then introduce the key indices and measurements devised to capture the distortive nature of an electoral 
system. These indices fall into two categories: those that gauge the proportionality of an electoral system 
irrespective of election results and strategic (re)actions of voters and parties, and empirical metrics that 
measure disproportionalities based on the actual election results. 
3.2.1 Proportional representation and its determinants 
The concept of proportionality is rooted in a normative notion of political representation according to 
which voters should be represented in rough proportion to their share of votes, as succinctly articulated by 
John Stuart Mill (1861: 85): “In a really equal democracy, every or any section would be represented, not 
disproportionately, but proportionately.” The normative connotation of the concept also surfaces in the 
work of Arend Lijphart (1984: 140), who viewed proportionality as “virtually synonymous with electoral 
justice”. Hence, the more precisely an electoral system maps the party preferences of the electorate in terms 
of seat shares, the more proportional and, consequently, the fairer it is. However, due to the “indivisible 
nature of seats” (Charvát 2023: 44), achieving perfect proportional representation, where seat shares pre￾cisely match vote shares, remains an empirically unattainable ideal. Therefore, proportionality can only be 
achieved in an optimal sense by approximating the “perfect” or “ideal” seat allocation. 
Yet, the inevitability of leaving certain votes unrepresented due to mathematical constraints is only one as￾pect of how electoral systems inherently conflict with “perfect” proportional representation. Other, more 
decisive parameters of electoral systems found to affect proportionality include the district magnitude, de￾fined as “the number of representatives elected in a district” (Lijphart 1994: 10), the electoral system (ma￾jority, plurality, or proportional representation), the mathematical formula used for vote-seat conversion, 
the assembly size, and legal thresholds (Charvát 2023: 46-50). In the following review, I will focus on these 
explanatory factors identified as central in the relevant literature. The review extends to “apparentements”, 
a feature exclusively relevant in PR systems, that enables parties to “formally (…) link or connect their lists” 

=== PDF PAGE 117 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
106 
(Lijphart, 1994: 15).74 This often “neglected feature of electoral systems” (Bochsler 2010b: 617) will be in￾cluded in the discussion, given its relevance in the context of this thesis. While there are indeed numerous 
other factors at play, the goal here is not to provide an exhaustive examination of all possible determinants 
(an impossible undertaking anyway). Rather, the aim is to point out the inherent link between seat-vote 
proportionality and various characteristics of electoral systems. As previous research has demonstrated, the 
“mapping accuracy” of an electoral system holds significant implications for various political outcomes. 
Among all parameters, the district magnitude has been identified as “the decisive factor” in affecting elec￾toral proportionality (Taagepera & Shugart 1989: 112, Grofman & Lijphart 2003: 53). James Hogan (1945: 
13), one of the pioneering scholars in the field and among the first to recognize the significant role of district 
magnitude in shaping electoral system effects, noted that “the decisive point in P.R. is the size of the constit￾uencies: the larger the constituency, that is, the greater the number of members which it elects, the more 
closely will the result approximate to proportionality”. Subsequent empirical studies have supported his 
finding, demonstrating that the proportionality of seat allocation correlates significantly and positively with 
larger district magnitudes. However, research suggests that this relationship is not linear but rather curvi￾linear or degressively proportional, meaning that the proportionality of seat allocation increases at a de￾creasing rate with larger district magnitudes (Rae 1971, Gallagher 1991, Lijphart 1994, Powell 2000, Gal￾lagher & Mitchell 2005, Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017). Within typical multiparty systems, district magnitudes 
of five or six have been identified as a lower boundary “to provide relatively proportional outcomes” (Taa￾gepera & Shugart 1989: 114). Notably, various studies have pointed to the inconsistency of the effect across 
different electoral system types. In fact, the effect may reverse under plurality/majority rules, where propor￾tionality tends to decrease with increasing district magnitudes (Blais & Carty 1987, Taagepera & Shugart 
1989, Lijphart 1999, Benoit 2001, Farrell 2001). This indicates a more complex and less straightforward re￾lationship between district magnitudes and vote-seat proportionality when electoral rules are factored into 
the equation. 
Introducing an upper tier of seat allocation within a broader region or across the entire electoral territory 
can further enhance the overall vote-seat proportionality as corresponding district magnitudes and, conse￾quentially, the mapping accuracy increases. With higher district magnitudes, the implicit threshold as an 
“entry barrier” for parties is lower and votes can be converted into seats more accurately since the vote quota 
required to win a seat decreases (Farrell 2001, Blais & Massicotte 2002, Charvát 2023). Additionally, higher 
district magnitudes can also reduce the number of “wasted votes” for parties whose share of votes falls below 
the implicit threshold needed to gain parliamentary representation, thereby fostering proportionality (Du￾verger 1964: 216-228, Gallego et al. 2012: 165). The same logic extends to the proportionality effect of as￾sembly sizes: larger legislative bodies with more available seats tend to exhibit higher levels of proportional￾ity (Lijphart 1999, Farrell 2001, Gallagher & Mitchell: 611). However, this effect is tempered by the number 
of electoral districts, as a greater number of electoral districts implies smaller districts and thus negatively 
affects the relationship between assembly size and proportionality (in PR systems). 
Previous studies have consistently found that vote-seat proportionality is significantly higher under PR elec￾toral rules compared to non-PR rules (Mackie & Rose 1991, Lijphart 1994, Gallagher et al. 1995, Norris 
1997). In his seminal work, Rae (1971: 124) concluded that alongside district magnitude, electoral formulae 
74 According to Nohlen (2014: 113), “apparentement” is a term borrowed from French as there is no equivalent Eng￾lish term. However, the alternative spelling “apparentment” can also be found in the relevant literature (e.g. Bochsler 
2010b or Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017). In acknowledgment of its French origin, I will adhere to the former spelling. 

=== PDF PAGE 118 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
107 
are “among the most important aspects of electoral laws” influencing the proportionality of seat allocation.75
Even if this nexus appears rather trivial since PR systems are quite literally devised to represent voters pro￾portionally, there can be considerable differences when comparing the different apportionment formulae 
used in PR elections. Within the PR family, three different formulae are typically distinguished: the highest 
averages or divisor methods (like d’Hondt or Sainte-Laguë), the largest remainders or quota methods (like 
Hare, Droop, or the two Imperiali quotas), and the single transferable vote (STV) (Lijphart 1994: 23, Riera 
2013: 54-55). In his empirical analysis, Lijphart (1994: 159) finds that “LR-Hare and pure Sainte-Laguë (...) 
are the most proportional formulas; the d’Hondt and the two LR-Imperiali formulas are the least propor￾tional; and LR-Droop, STV (...), and modified Sainte-Laguë are in an intermediate category.”76
Other important devices of PR-based electoral systems that directly affect the vote-seat proportionality are 
legal thresholds. Legal thresholds can broadly be defined as a minimum level of electoral support a party 
needs to gain parliamentary representation, or inversely, as a “cut-off point below which parties contesting 
the elections are not awarded any seats” (Charvát 2023: 49).77 Such an additional criterion can either apply 
at the national, regional, or at the district level. Legal thresholds can be specified in terms of absolute votes, 
as a percentage of votes or as some other criterion (Lijphart 1994: 11-12). It is important to distinguish be￾tween these formal thresholds and electoral thresholds implied by the territorial structure, i.e. the district 
magnitude, as they former are explicitly stipulated in the electoral law (unlike implicit thresholds) and ex￾hibit slightly different effect mechanisms.78 Given the presence of implicit thresholds, explicit formal thresh￾olds are only effective if they significantly exceed the former. Although Lijphart (1994: 12) initially found 
that most electoral systems lack such formal thresholds, subsequent research reached a different conclusion, 
suggesting that they are relatively common (Charvát 2023: 49). Gallagher and Mitchell (2005: 13) asserted 
that “[v]irtually every PR system employs some kind of threshold that prevents the smallest parties getting 
the ‘fair’ share of the seats.” A conclusion supported by Riera (2013: 58), who noted that “[a]lmost every PR 
system employs some kind of threshold.” 
The possibility to conduct inter-party a/o intra-party apparentements is another aspect pertinent to the 
overall proportionality and closely intertwined with the issue of electoral thresholds. They are a peculiarity 
of PR systems and enable parties to “pool their votes for seat allocation purposes” (Gallagher & Mitchell 
2005: 631). Apparentements can involve two or sometimes more electoral lists and serve as a potent tool, 
especially for smaller parties whose voter share often hovers around or falls below the “threshold of repre￾sentation”. Empirical studies indicate that apparentements are used by parties of varying sizes and types (see 
e.g. Pukelsheim & Leutgäb 2013). While their specific effects are among the most cryptic aspects of electoral 
75 By “electoral formula”, Rae means the main rationale of vote-seat conversion and distinguishes between majority, 
plurality, and proportional representation types of electoral formulas. However, other authors refer to “electoral for￾mulae” as “the mathematical mechanisms governing the transformation of votes into seats” (Benoit 2000: 381). 
Given this definitional ambiguity, I adhere to the distinction proposed by Charvát (2023: 48), who defines “electoral 
formula” as the “way (default logic) in which votes are to be counted in order to allocate seats, and the mathematical 
formula as the method by which votes are translated into seats within electoral districts under PR. 
76 For a comprehensive discussion on the ranking of the different formulas in terms of their estimated effect on vote￾seat proportionality, see Gallagher 1991: 33-51, Lijphart 2003: 170-179, or Gallagher & Mitchell 2005: 584-589.

extent on the mathematical formula used for vote-seat conversion (cf. Gallagher & Mitchell 2005: 607-608).

=== PDF PAGE 119 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
108 
systems, the mechanism behind them is rather straightforward: parties involved in the apparentement are 
considered as a single list in the super-apportionment of the seats to parties. Then, in the subsequent step, 
these seats are sub-apportioned to the parties involved in the apparentement based on their respective share 
of votes. While there remains much ambiguity, partly due to limited research on the topic and the depend￾ency of apparentements’ effects on specific electoral contexts, the few existing studies offer some valuable 
insights (i.e., Lijphart 1994, Bochsler 2010b, Pukelsheim & Leutgäb 2013). 
Firstly, parties involved in an apparentement cannot fare worse than if they competed independently with 
a single list.79 This is because participating in an electoral alliance allows for the aggregation of “remainder 
votes”, which would otherwise be ineffective, potentially leading to additional seat gains. Or, as Pukelsheim 
and Leutgäb (2013: 128) put it: “Lists who prefer to maintain their independence and do not join an appar￾entement must, on average, endure a seat deficit so that their competitors may be served with a seat surplus.” 
However, it would be erroneous to conclude that the participation in an apparentement guarantees seat 
gains. The potential benefits depend on various factors, including the apportionment formula, the actual 
vote shares of the competing lists, the respective position within the apparentement in terms of votes, the 
presence of other apparentements between different parties, and numerous other aspects. 
Secondly, apparentements prove to be particularly effective when used within highly fractionalized political 
blocs comprising numerous small parties (Bochsler 2010b: 625). However, as Bochsler (2010b) highlights, 
this does not necessarily mean that fractionalized parties reap the primary benefits. In his empirical analysis 
of Swiss parliamentary elections spanning from 1995 to 2007, the findings suggest quite the opposite. He 
argues that “[e]ven if alliances of small parties are among the main beneficiaries of apparentments, the gains 
of these apparentments are more likely to be accorded to large parties within these apparentments” 
(Bochsler 2010b: 625). However, it is important to note that in Swiss National Council elections, the 
D’Hondt method is employed. This method is known to have mathematical properties that disadvantage 
small parties (e.g. Benoit 2000). Since the D’Hondt method is also used for the sub-apportionment of seats 
within the apparentements to individual list partners, the adverse effect on the “junior partner” is exacer￾bated. When apparentement partners are unequally sized in terms of vote shares, Pukelsheim and Leutgäb 
(2013: 128) denote the resulting advantage for the larger partner within the apparentement thus as a “double 
bonus”. 
Lastly and most importantly, while the initial belief was that apparentements had a consistently positive 
effect on overall vote-seat proportionality (Lijphart 1994, Nohlen 2014), more recent studies paint a more 
nuanced picture. Although Pukelsheim and Leutgäb (2013) find that in many cases, apparentements indeed 
lead to a reduction in the overall bias in most of the cases, they also found the bias to increase “in more than 
one third of all cases” (2013: 130) and conclude that “[i]t is a widespread belief that apparentements always
dampen the total bias. This belief is erroneous (...)” (ibid: 2013: 130). Similarly, Bochsler (2010b: 625) finds 
that “apparentments, especially when they unite parties of different sizes, might rather increase than de￾crease the seat bias under D’Hondt”. In summary, apparentements have the potential to enhance propor￾tionality by providing smaller parties with a chance to have their votes represented in parliament. However, 
under certain conditions, they can also have the opposite effect, benefiting larger parties and contributing 
to a decrease in the overall proportionality. 
79 At least not under the assumption that a list does not incur any “electoral damage” from forming an alliance with a 
partner considered unsuitable by its own electoral base. 

=== PDF PAGE 120 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
109 
To summarize, the (in)capacity of an electoral system to allocate the seats proportionately to the parties’ 
vote shares is conditioned by various institutional features. These technical aspects of electoral systems col￾lectively set the limits of how (in)accurately votes are translated to seats. The less restrictive specific regula￾tions are, i.e. no legal hurdles, low implicit thresholds, and a politically unbiased apportionment formula, 
the higher the inherent propensity for a proportional translation from votes into seats. Apparentements can 
also contribute to an improvement of vote-seat proportionality. However, this effect is contingent upon 
other aspects of the electoral system as well as the specific electoral circumstances. 
The resulting effects on proportionality can be straightforward and direct, but they can also be a product of 
more complex, cumulatively reinforcing, weakening, or neutralizing interactions. In small electoral dis￾tricts, e.g., the choice of mathematical formula can significantly impact the proportionality of seat allocation, 
whereas in larger electoral districts, the influence of different formulas may be relatively small (Charvát 
2023: 48). As Gallagher (1991: 50) put it: “When district magnitude is large, either because constituencies 
are large or because of the use of higher tier seat allocation, the proportionality of election outcomes is high, 
regardless of the formula or the index of measurement employed.” Or, to provide another example: In a 
multi-district electoral context without a superordinate allocation tier, the factors that hinder proportional 
vote-seat transmission at the district level can exacerbate across the entire electoral area, culminating in a 
substantial impairment of the overall vote-seat proportionality. 
Deviations from perfect vote-seat proportionality, known as “disproportionalities” or “distortions”, indicate 
systematic biases in an electoral system that favor certain parties (Grofman 1983, Gallagher 1991, Lijphart 
1995b, Calvo 2009, Nohlen 2014, Cox 2006). Lijphart (1994: 58) described proportionality and dispropor￾tionality as “two sides of exactly the same coin”. Evaluating electoral systems often involves examining their 
inherent tendencies to obstruct optimal proportionality. Maurice Duverger (1954) was among the first to 
formalize the disproportional effects of electoral systems, conceptualizing these effects as “mechanical ef￾fects” that distort vote-to-seat conversion due to electoral rules in combination with election outcomes (Be￾noit 2006, Riera 2013, Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017). The next section discusses the key indices and concepts 
developed to quantify these distortions and the extent to which electoral systems skew proportionality. 
3.2.2 Measurements of mechanically induced disproportionalities 
A variety of measures have been developed to assess the extent to which electoral rules interfere with the 
proportional conversion of votes into seats. The quest to measure the degree of distortion caused by electoral 
rules has diverged into two distinct methodical approaches. One set of measures, categorizable as “a priori” 
indices, is theory-based and attempts to predict the extent to which electoral rules inherently affect propor￾tionality, regardless of actual election outcomes. In contrast, “a posteriori” measurements are empirically 
grounded and assess the actual disparities between votes and seats, thus also conveying information about 
the actual election results (Bochsler 2024: 2). 
3.2.2.1 “A priori” measurements 
The a priori measurements that I will briefly outline have the common goal of predicting how electoral rules 
inherently influence an electoral system’s (dis)proportionality. They do so by approximating a theoretical 
value referred to as the “effective threshold”, which delineates the percentage of votes a party needs to secure 

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
110 
a seat.80 The corresponding formula is based on a single predictor: the district magnitude. This should not 
come as a surprise in light of the vast body of research showing that district magnitude is the primary insti￾tutional feature influencing an electoral system’s (dis)proportionality (cf. Taagepera and Shugart 1989, Li￾jphart 1994, Boix 1999, Lublin 2015, Flick-Witzig and Vatter 2017). As Lijphart (1994: 12) highlighted, “low 
magnitudes have the same effect as high thresholds: both limit proportionality and the opportunities of 
small parties to win seats; as magnitudes increase and thresholds decrease, proportionality and the chances 
for small parties improve.” The concept of the effective threshold thus helps us to understand and quantify 
the “degree of squeeze [electoral systems, S. L.] put on representation of small parties” (Taagepera 1998: 
393). 
However, the effective threshold implied by district magnitude does not reflect a fixed, objectively assessable 
percentage. Rather, the estimate encapsulates a spectrum of potential values between the “threshold of rep￾resentation” and the “threshold of exclusion”, which set the lower and upper theoretical boundaries (Li￾jphart 1994: 25, see also Gallagher & Mitchell: 607-611). While the threshold of representation (or inclusion) 
indicates the vote share enabling a party to win a seat under the most favorable conditions, the threshold of 
exclusion reflects the scenario where a party garners a large vote share and yet fails to win a seat due to 
extremely unfavorable circumstances (Rokkan 1968, Rae et al. 1971, Laakso 1979, Gallagher 1992, Lijphart 
1994, Taagepera 1998). An exception arises when a formal national threshold applies. In such cases, the 
effective threshold equals the formal threshold, resulting in a precise value rather than a rough estimate 
(Lijphart 1994: 29). 
Figuratively speaking, if a party’s vote share falls below the “lower” boundary, it cannot win a seat. If it ex￾ceeds this threshold, winning a seat becomes possible (if other parameters align). And if it surpasses the 
“upper” boundary, it will certainly secure a seat. The effective threshold lies between these boundaries and 
therefore provides a more practical univariate estimation of the exclusionary or reductive nature of electoral 
systems (Taagepera 1998: 395). The relationship is depicted in Figure 37 on the next page. 
Lijphart (1994) went a step further and looked at the correlation between the effective threshold and vote￾seat disproportionality. He regressed the percentage of disproportionality (using the least-squares index) on 
the effective threshold for two different groups: a pool of PR electoral systems with formal thresholds (1) 
and a group without formal thresholds where the effective thresholds were inferred directly or partly from 
the district magnitudes (2). He repeated the regression for the second group, adding the mathematical for￾mula and the (logged) assembly size to the equation (3). He found slopes of 0.42 for equation (1), 0.40 for 
equation (2) and 0.42 for equation (3). The results indicate that for every percentage increase in the effective 
electoral threshold, disproportionality increases on average by 0.42 (Lijphart 1994: 27-28).81
Building on the seminal contributions of Rokkan (1968), Rae et al. (1971), Taagepera & Shugart (1989), and 
Lijphart (1994), recent research has made significant strides in accommodating the complexities of contem￾porary electoral systems. For the a priori estimation of the institutional “permissiveness” or “openness” of 
electoral systems for (small) parties, more recent studies include factors such as a multi-tier structures of the 
80 An alternative and essentially equivalent concept is the “effective magnitude”. According to Taagepera and Shugart 
(1998: 398) the effective threshold and the effective magnitude “are the reciprocals of one another”. However, in my 
brief outline, I focus solely on Lijphart’s perspective, which puts emphasis on the effective threshold and, in my view, 
offers a more intuitive approach. 
81 Unfortunately, he did not report any numbers on the robustness of his findings. 

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
111 
electoral system, formal thresholds, and bonus seats (Neto & Cox 1997, Clark & Golder 2006, Lublin 2015, 
Renwick & Pilet 2016, Shugart & Taagepera 2017, Bochsler 2024).

3.2.2.2 “A posteriori” measurements 
While a priori measurements exclusively take institutional parameters into account and are only indirectly 
linked to disproportionality, a posteriori measurements also take actual electoral results into consideration 
and are therefore designed to measure the actual disproportionality of an electoral system. The higher the 
summed deviations between votes and seats for all parties in a particular election, the greater the value of 
disproportionality, indicating a more substantial departure from “ideal proportionality”. While dispropor￾tionality as a measurement of partisan misrepresentation in terms of votes and seats appears to be a rather 
simple and straightforward concept, the methodology for its measurement is more complex (Lijphart 1994: 
57-59). The different approaches led to the development of various indices. The one devised by Gallagher 
(1991) emerged as the most widely applied measure in relevant literature. The index squares the vote-seat 
share differences for each party, totals these differences, divides the total by two and takes the square root of 
the resulting value (Lijphart 1994: 60-61). In his evaluation of four measures of disproportionality, Lijphart 
(1994: 62) describes the Gallagher index as “the most sensitive and faithful reflection of the disproportion￾ality of election results”. 
In their evaluation of nineteen different indices established in the field, Taagepera and Grofman (2003: 659) 
arrived at the same conclusion as Lijphart (1994) and stated that the Gallagher index “offers the most desir￾able combination of features”. Gallagher’s least-squares index offers the advantage of not just measuring 
vote-seat disparity, but also taking into account how the competing parties “contribute” to this disparity. It 
is designed to be sensitive to (a small number of) large seat-vote disparities while being relatively insensitive 
to (a large number of) small disparities. Taagepera and Grofman (2003: 668-669) argue that the problem 
with (over)sensitivity to smaller deviations is that this sensitivity also applies to the “lumping of residuals”, 

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
112 
which arise randomly rather than systematically, resulting in an overestimation of the overall dispropor￾tionality (see also Lijphart 1994: 60-61 and Gallagher & Mitchell 2005: 602-606).82
3.3 Moving beyond (dis)proportionality: Varying levels of proportionality and their political effects 
Understanding the political ramifications of electoral systems requires more than the examination of their 
institutional features. Duverger (1954) introduced the concept of “psychological effects” to complement the 
mechanical effects. These psychological effects include the responses of parties and voters to the mechanical 
constraints imposed by the electoral system. Voters interpret the electoral rules and adjust their behavior in 
anticipation of their expected effects. In a formal sense, strategically minded voters seeking to influence 
electoral outcomes are likely to deviate from their preference order if voting for their preferred options car￾ries a high risk of ineffectiveness due to slim chances of success. Instead, they opt for alternative choices that 
are close to their ideal point but offer better electoral prospects. This strategic behavior is well-captured by 
the rational choice model (Downs 1957, Grofman and Lijphart 2003, Aldrich et al. 2018, Harfst et al. 2018). 
Similarly, parties may choose to participate in elections only if they expect intact prospects of electoral suc￾cess. Otherwise, if they deem their chances futile and when the projected costs outweigh potential benefits, 
they may refrain from candidacy (Bochsler 2005). Duverger (1954) suggested that the interaction between 
the static mechanical effects and the dynamic psychological effects of electoral rules significantly influence 
the character of a party system. In essence, he argued that plurality/majority systems tend to favor bipartism 
(or “party dualism” in Duverger’s own words, ibid. 1954: 247) due to both mechanical and psychological 
factors. In contrast, PR-based systems tend to foster the emergence of multipartism. The regularities de￾scribed by Duverger have shown high empirical validity, although the precise mechanisms behind these 
effects remain the subject of ongoing debate.83 Subsequent studies have built upon Duverger’s rich theoret￾ical legacy and aimed to disentangle and formalize the relationship between electoral rules and the structures 
of political systems. 
Following this line of research, this section of the thesis delves into the potential macro-level effects induced 
by cantonal biproportional reforms. The central theoretical premise is that while biproportional reforms 
primarily alter the mechanics of the electoral system, they also engender dynamic responses from both vot￾ers and partisan elites (Sartori 1997: 32). These actors interpret the institutional changes, anticipate their 
potential ramifications, and adapt their behavior accordingly. The behavioral adaptations of voters and par￾ties are expected to materialize in observable and quantifiable changes in the political landscape. 
The illustrated mechanism (Figure 38) suggests that modifications to the electoral system’s mechanics may 
alter the perceived electoral permeability. Voters are anticipated to respond to these changes by adjusting 
their voting behavior, while political parties may reassess their electoral strategies in light of the new system. 
It is expected that the combined and mutually influencing reactions of voters and partisan actors contribute 
82 For these reasons, I will also apply Gallagher’s least-squares index for the measurement of vote-seat disproportion￾ality in the further course of the thesis. 
83 The question about nature of the relationship between electoral systems and characteristics of party systems in Du￾verger’s postulates has sparked controversy and has been the subject of numerous inquiries (e.g. Sartori 2003: 43-68). 
I prefer to cautiously describe it as a strong relationship, given its high empirical plausibility, without delving into the 
specifics of whether it adheres to a strict law or lawlike pattern (or neither). 

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
113 
to a change in the structure of a political system. However, the evolution of the political landscape is influ￾enced by various factors beyond electoral rules and reactions to them, most notably by socio-structural fac￾tors. 
Moreover, changes in political structures may provoke calls for further electoral reforms, suggesting a re￾cursive relationship between electoral rules and political structures. This model highlights the dynamic na￾ture of this relationship. It illustrates how alterations in electoral rules can trigger a cascade of responses 
from voters, political actors and institutions and ultimately shape the trajectory of the political system over 
time. 
Feedback Loop

In the following subchapters, I will discuss various political parameters that are presumably influenced by 
the transition to biproportional apportionment and derive corresponding hypotheses. These parameters 
include the number of parties, voter turnout rates, and the number of lists/candidacies. However, before 
delving into the assessment of these outcomes, as a preliminary step, it is essential to determine the extent 
to which the accuracy of the electoral system in translating votes to seats has been affected. This is important 
because electoral rules do not directly affect political outcomes. Rather, their impact hinges on the degree to 
which they facilitate or constrain proportional representation. Therefore, if the mapping accuracy would 
remain untouched by the electoral reforms, hypotheses about changes in the political landscape would be 
unfounded and implausible. 
3.3.1 Reform effects on disproportionality 
Vote-seat disproportionality is both an outcome influenced by multiple factors and a driver of various con￾sequences. Exclusive electoral rules inevitably lead to high vote-seat disproportionality. Conversely, inclu￾sive rules are a precondition for low disproportionality. However, low disproportionality does not neces￾sarily imply a mechanically inclusive system. Even in highly constraining systems that impose significant 
electoral hurdles, disproportionality can be low if voters are aware of the systemic effects induced by the 
electoral rules. By strategically assessing their options, voters may preemptively discard choices that are un￾likely to become effective. This behavior reflects an adaptive response to the mechanical constraints imposed 
by the electoral system. Similarly, parties or less institutionalized electoral groups may be dissuaded from 

=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
114 
electoral participation by high electoral barriers and abstain from candidacy. The proactive alignment with 
mechanical constraints ultimately leads to a reduction in vote-seat disproportionality, despite the exclusivity 
of the electoral rules. Bipartism can be viewed as another epitome of the same effect. 
Although biproportional reforms in Switzerland vary in their implementation pathways and designs, the 
applied formula is consistent over all cantons. In a first step, seats are allocated between the districts propor￾tionally to population figures and between parties proportionally to their cross-district, canton-wide vote 
count. In the following step, seats are distributed simultaneously by district and party (Pukelsheim 2017: 
259). The first step is pivotal, particularly in its impact on disproportionality. By super-apportioning seats 
across districts in the first step, parties secure a seat share reflective of their canton-wide vote share. This 
approach effectively counters the constraining effect of electoral districts, creating an artificial, canton-wide 
unitary district. In cases where the formula applies to the entire canton, the magnitude of this artificial dis￾trict matches the size of the cantonal assembly. In instances where the formula is limited to a subset of elec￾toral districts, the size of this new fictional district corresponds to the sum of seats allocated to these districts 
(as is the case in the Canton of Uri). 
Expanding the electoral area mitigates the mechanical effect arising from the division of the electoral area 
into districts. If implemented purely, i.e. without additional provisions that adversely affect vote-seat con￾version, disproportionality should take a value close to ideal proportionality. In addition, the possibility to 
create apparentements was not provided in any of the reform designs. Neither “horizontal” apparentements 
between parties or list groups, nor “vertical”, within-list apparentements between different subgroups of 
parties (“sub-apparentements”). As such alliances offer smaller parties, particularly those with a vote share 
below or around the threshold of representation the opportunity to circumvent the adversity of mechanical 
effects by collaborating with other parties, there was long the erroneous belief that the possibility to form 
such alliances immanently reduces overall disproportionality. The main argument is that the participants 
of such alliances, or at least the junior partners, would not manage to enter parliament on their own, i.e., as 
a single list, without this electoral tool, contributing to an increase in disproportionality. However, empirical 
research casts doubt on this view. In his study of apparentements in Swiss National Council elections, 
Bochsler (2010b) showed that seat gains through apparentements tend to work to the benefit of large parties 
already favored by the d’Hondt formula. This amplifies disproportionality rather than curbing it. In fact, 
according to Bochsler, apparentements would only reduce disproportionality under specific conditions. He 
concluded (ibid. 2010b: 618): “The model established in this paper suggests that the frequent believe that 
apparentments correct the disproportionalities of the D’Hondt rule cannot be maintained.” Since bipropor￾tional apportionment empirically does not include the provision of apparentements, this potential source 
of disproportionality is eliminated. 
Altogether, the implementation of biproportional apportionment is thus expected to result in a significant 
reduction of vote-seat disproportionality, irrespective of the specific reform design. Despite the resulting 
effect being purely mechanical, a reassessment of the systemic incentives might prompt changes in the voter 
and partisan behavior that potentially mitigate this mechanical effect. The prospect of a more accurate vote￾seat translation may encourage voters to vote more “sincerely”, i.e. in accordance with their “true prefer￾ences”. This could lead to increased voter support for fringe parties that ultimately still fall below the thresh￾old of representation. While votes for such parties may not suffice for a parliamentary seat, they contribute 
to an increase in disproportionality, albeit marginally, as the LSq-index is relatively insensitive to small vote￾seat differences. Nonetheless, significant reductions in vote-seat disproportionality can be expected in the 

=== PDF PAGE 126 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
115 
wake of biproportional reforms, given that this is an essential goal of reform. The first hypothesis thus sug￾gests:84
H3: The adoption of biproportional apportionment corresponds with a significant decrease in vote-seat 
disproportionality. 
Setting aside any case-specific peculiarities, a common feature among the cantons transitioning to bipro￾portional apportionment is the presence of electoral districts failing to meet the FSC’s stipulation of at least 
nine seats for PR-based elections. As per the Court’s interpretation, proportional representation is not ef￾fectively realized in districts with magnitudes lower than nine seats. These districts imply a hidden but sub￾stantial effective threshold, which poses obstacles to fair representation for smaller parties and their elec￾torates. The Court concluded that the greater the deviation from the nine-seat requirement, the more pro￾nounced the departure from the principle of proportional representation. Table 36 illustrates the count of 
districts within the group of reformed cantons that did not conform to the judicature before the transition 
(t-1). The numbers indicate large variances, both in absolute and relative terms. These differences can be 
largely explained by the different territorial structures of the cantonal electoral systems.

Building upon H3, which suggests a substantial decrease in disproportionality following the reform and 
considering that district magnitude is recognized as an important institutional driver of disproportionality, 
it can be assumed that the resulting declines in disproportionality will be more pronounced in cantons with 
a large proportion of small electoral districts before the reform. This leads to the following hypothesis: 
H4: The decrease in disproportionality following a reform is more significant in cantons with lower 
district magnitudes prior to the reform. 
3.3.2 Party system fragmentation 
Assessing party system fragmentation or concentration through the number of parties has become the gold 
standard of electoral system analysis. It is generally accepted that party system fragmentation is shaped by 
the interplay of institutional and socio-structural factors (e.g. Ordeshook & Shvetsova 1994, Neto & Cox 
84 The numbering of the hypotheses is not specific to individual sections but applies to the entire thesis. Therefore, 
the first hypothesis is continued at number three. 

=== PDF PAGE 127 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
116 
1997, Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017, Taagepera & Shugart 1989). In my short outline, I will first focus on the 
institutional factors and then discuss the socio-structural determinants. 
Duverger’s distinction between static mechanical and dynamic psychological effects (1954), akin to Rae’s 
concept of “proximal” and “distal” effects (1967), has emerged as the dominant institutional framework for 
understanding how electoral rules affect the number of parties.85 Mechanical or proximal effects highlight 
the inherent tendency of an electoral system to grant bonus seats to larger parties, thereby overrepresenting 
them in parliament at the expense of smaller parties (Taagepera & Shugart 1989: 61-66). Because these ef￾fects contribute to a reduction in the number of parties, they are often labelled as “reductive” or “constrain￾ing” in relevant literature. Their magnitude varies depending on the specific design of the electoral system 
and the degree to which it facilitates (or restricts) a proportional translation from votes to seats. In contrast, 
the psychological or distal effects refer to strategical considerations of voters, parties, and other stakeholders. 
Anticipating this mechanically induced discrimination against smaller parties and seeking to avoid the risk 
of wasting their votes (on them), they may strategically opt for larger, more promising parties instead, even 
if this implies a deviation from the first preference (Lijphart 1994: 71-72). This realism-driven “pre-filtering” 
amplifies preexisting biases in favor of larger, established parties and further reduces the number of legisla￾tive parties. Duverger famously posited that these effects accentuate under plurality rule and tend to either 
establish or solidify a two-party system, while proportional representation allows for multipartism (see also 
Riker 1982). 
The socio-structural approach to understanding party fragmentation originates from Lipset and Rokkan’s 
cleavage theory, which identifies socio-structural divisions as the primary determinants of party system 
characteristics. Lipset and Rokkan (1967) identified four dominant cleavages, two arising from historical 
national revolutions and two from the industrial revolution. Essentially, they argue that these four cleavages 
structure political conflict in a political entity. Political parties are seen as mechanisms to regulate these con￾flicts. The more salient these cleavages are within a country, the higher the number of legislative parties, 
resulting in a higher fragmentation of the party system. Initially, the cleavage concept was operationalized 
using the raw number of issue dimensions within a society (Taagepera & Grofman 1985, Lijphart 1994). 
However, this approach was later supplanted by indicators of social heterogeneity, which were identified as 
the true causal factors driving social divides (Ordeshook & Shvetsova 1994, Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017). 
In their study, Neto and Cox (1997) explored the relationship between electoral institutions and cleavage 
structures as two distinct approaches to understanding the determinants of the number of effective parties. 
Their findings suggest an interactive rather than a summative effect of socio-structural and institutional 
factors. In their own words, “the effective number of parties appears to depend on the product of social 
heterogeneity and electoral permissiveness, rather than being an additive function of these two” (ibid. 1997: 
166). Put differently, bipartism, or low party fragmentation, can arise due to either a “strong electoral system 
or because it has few cleavages” (ibid. 1997: 167). This indicates that neither an open electoral system nor 
the presence of numerous potentially exploitable cleavages alone is enough to guarantee multipartism. In￾stead, multipartism emerges (only) when these two factors align. Table 37 shows the theoretical relationship 
85 There are various ways in which parties can be counted (see e.g. Lijphart 1994: 77-91). The most common distinc￾tion lies between “elective” or “electoral” parties and “effective”, “parliamentary”, or “legislative” parties. In my dis￾cussion, I will exclusively focus on the latter category, as it aligns with the relevant dimension of my empirical meas￾urement. I will denote this construct as the “effective number of legislative parties”. 

=== PDF PAGE 128 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
117 
between the mechanical inclusivity of electoral rules, socio-structural heterogeneity, and the expected de￾gree of party system fragmentation. By integrating both institutional and sociological perspectives, we can 
expect an increase in the number of parties in settings characterized by high socio-structural heterogeneity, 
provided that the institutional conditions allow for the mechanical translation of this diversity.

Switzerland, when viewed through a macroscopic lens, reveals a landscape rich in socio-structural contrasts: 
four national languages and a history marked by enduring political tensions between anticlerical liberals 
and conservative catholics during the early years of the Swiss federal state. Additionally, Switzerland is rec￾ognized as having “one of the countries with the most salient rural-urban divide” (Zumbrunn 2024: 28). 
Despite this, the class divisions appear comparatively subdued (Lijphart 1979). In an exploration of party 
fragmentation within Swiss cantonal parliamentary elections spanning from 1986 to 1997, Vatter (2003: 
454) discerned a pattern where fewer legislative parties tended to emerge in cantons characterized by rural￾agrarian settings, lower population density, religious uniformity, and smaller district magnitudes. Con￾versely, urban areas, greater religious diversity, and lower electoral thresholds correlated with a higher num￾ber of parties in cantonal parliaments. Similarly, Flick-Witzig and Vatter (2017) concluded in their research 
that the exclusion magnitude, indicative of the permissiveness of an electoral system, emerges as the primary 
determinant in explaining party fragmentation in Swiss cantons, superseding socio-structural factors. 
Socio-structural divisions generally hold less sway at subnational levels, with their impact dwindling from 
national to cantonal and district levels. This trend is attributed to reduced prevalence a/o politicization of 
these divisions as one moves down the administrative hierarchy (cf. Flick-Witzig & Vatter 2017: 12). More￾over, in the context of Swiss biproportional reforms, socio-structural factors are expected to have marginal 
to negligible impact, not only due to their reduced salience on the cantonal level and due to their relative 
stability over time, but also because these parameters are not targeted by reform. Consequently, the socio￾structural dimensions of cantonal party systems will not be considered. Given that biproportional reforms 
primarily pertain to the institutional dimension of electoral systems, significant changes to electoral rules 
are likely to entail alterations in the structure of the cantonal party systems. 
Biproportional apportionment entails various consequences, with the most significant and decisive being 
the simulation of a single constituency in the upper-tier allocation of seats. This is presumed to result in a 
significant reduction of the threshold implied by the district structure. This mechanical effect, coupled with 
the anticipation of a more accurate vote-seat conversion by both voters and parties, is likely to encourage 
party entrance and an increase in votes for small parties that were previously penalized by the psychological 
effect. These effects could contribute to a rise in the effective number of legislative parties. However, it is 
possible that this effect may not manifest immediately, as voters and political stakeholders may respond to 
the new institutional stimuli with a certain delay (Klöti & Widmer 1997: 207-210). This leads to the follow￾ing two hypotheses: 

=== PDF PAGE 129 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
118 
H5a: The introduction of the biproportionality corresponds with an increase in the effective number of 
legislative parties in cantonal parliaments. 
H5b: The introduction of the biproportionality corresponds with a delayed and gradual increase in the 
effective number of legislative parties in cantonal parliaments. 
3.3.3 Electoral turnout 
Turnout rates vary widely not only across countries but to some extent also sub-nationally as well as over 
time. Scholars have devoted considerable attention to understanding how features of electoral systems con￾tribute to differences in turnout levels. Empirical studies generally suggest a positive correlation between 
the vote-seat proportionality and voter turnout (see, e.g., Jackman & Miller 1995, Blais & Dobrzynska 1998, 
Blais 2006, Geys 2006, Selb 2009). In his examination of the role of strategic mobilization in the relationship 
between electoral rules and turnout, Cox (2015: 50) noted that “the most abrupt, durable, and significant 
changes in turnout coincide with changes in electoral rules”, citing the Swiss case where the adoption of PR 
for the election of the National Council in 1919 resulted in an average increase of 20% in voter turnout 
across cantons (Gosnell 1930: 126). Similarly, Selb (2009), who conducted a cross-country analysis using 
district-level data from 31 national elections in 20 different developed democracies, stated that “[e]vidence 
that turnout is higher under PR than in majoritarian elections is overwhelming.” A common framework for 
explaining this relationship is the concept of pivotality, rooted in the economic model of voting proposed 
by Downs (1957) and further elaborated upon by Riker and Ordeshook (1968). It suggests that the decision 
to participate in elections largely hinges on the perceived ability of a voter to sway the outcome (Eggers 2013: 
1, Sanz 2016: 8). The higher the expected effectiveness of one’s vote, the greater the likelihood of turnout. In 
plurality elections, where the chance of being decisive is slim and typically only plausible in closely contested 
races among the top candidates, voters in multi-member districts in PR-based elections have better pro￾spects of effectively affecting the outcome. Thus, the electoral system shapes voters’ perceptions of efficacy, 
which in turn influences the propensity to vote. Given that the likelihood of a vote being effective is greater 
under PR than under MR, this positive stimulus may translate into higher turnout rates. 
In another study, Gallego et al. (2012) found that systemic stimuli do not immediately alter electoral behav￾ior but rather exhibit a certain delay. While disproportionality appears to be unrelated to voter participation 
in the early elections following democratization, it has a negative effect in established democracies. Their 
findings suggest that small parties adapt to the new structures of incentives and adjust their mobilization 
strategies to maximize their parliamentary representation. Citizens who feel close to small parties respond 
similarly to these new stimuli and are additionally influenced by the parties’ new mobilization strategies. 
The combination of these two effects explains why disproportionality does not have an immediate effect on 
turnout directly after democratization but becomes apparent just after several elections. This “lagged re￾sponse” also indicates that parties and voters are not able to fully anticipate the consequences of an electoral 
system ex ante but require some time to familiarize themselves with its new incentives and effects. 
Despite the strong empirical and theoretical support suggesting that electoral systems play a significant role 
in stimulating electoral participation, the exact causal mechanism underlying this relationship remains 
largely a puzzle. This concern is echoed by Blais (2006: 116), who summarized the literature on electoral 
systems and turnout concluding that “[m]ost of the literature supports the view that PR fosters turnout, but 
there is no compelling explanation of how and why”. A challenge arises from the fact that electoral systems, 
also tend to vary significantly along other dimensions such as district sizes, coalition types, party systems, 

=== PDF PAGE 130 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
119 
etc. This complicates the isolation of specific factors that explain differences in turnout (Eggers 2013: 2). In 
other words, higher turnout rates under PR may also, at least partially, be influenced by other variables, and 
the impact of these variables may change from one setting to another.86
In the context of biproportional reforms in Switzerland, one of the primary immediate effects is presumably 
a significant reduction of vote-seat disproportionality. Consequently, voters can generally be expected to 
have better prospects of effectively impacting the electoral result. This is particularly true for voters or sym￾pathizers of small parties who may have previously been discouraged from casting their vote for their pre￾ferred party or from voting at all due to a high improbability of their vote being effective. The new system 
provides a set of incentives that enhance the effectiveness of their vote, which may fundamentally alter their 
cost-benefit calculus. Citizens for whom the costs of turnout previously outweighed its expected benefits 
may find their calculus changed due to the reform, potentially motivating them to participate in the electoral 
process. In contrast, voters of larger established parties, who had a higher propensity to impact the election 
results with their vote than their counterparts supporting smaller parties, may lose their previous bonus. 
However, there is no reason to believe that this loss of bonus would push such voters into abstention. There￾fore, it can be assumed that the introduction of biproportional apportionment will increase turnout rates. 
However, again it is possible that this effect does not manifest immediately in the first post-reform election 
but rather occurs with a certain delay, as voter expectations are shaped by past experiences and institutional 
knowledge (Taagepera & Shugart 1989, Klöti & Widmer 1997, Riera 2013). 
H6a: The introduction of biproportionality corresponds with an increase in voter turnout for cantonal 
parliamentary elections. 
H6b: The introduction of biproportionality corresponds with a delayed and gradual increase in voter 
turnout for cantonal parliamentary elections. 
3.3.4 Electoral competition 
Competitive elections stand as important hallmarks of modern-day democracy (Lindberg 2004: 67). High 
levels of political contestation are indicative of a vital democratic culture and inclusive electoral rules, as the 
incentives for competition are evidently structured in a way to mobilize a high number of political contend￾ers. The “pivotal elites model” (Selb 2009) suggests that parties mobilize their potential electorates “if the 
benefits gained through winning parliamentary seats, weighted by the probability that the mobilization ef￾fort will be decisive, exceed the mobilization costs” (Selb 2009: 531, see also Cox & Munger 1989, Cox 1999). 
Mobilization efforts are, in turn, influenced by institutional mechanisms governing the translation of votes 
into seats as these mechanisms “are commonly assumed to condition the chances of electoral success of 
competing parties” (Charvát 2023: 19). Hence, it is rational for parties to allocate their limited mobilization 
resources in districts where they have a higher chance of gaining seats, rather than in districts where their 
prospects are low or where such efforts are in vain (Selb 2009: 531, Charvát 2023: 1). Considering that dis￾trict magnitude largely determines the threshold, electoral competition tends to be higher in larger electoral 
districts, holding other factors constant (Bochsler & Wasserfallen 2013: 252). Higher local competitiveness 
also correlates with increased electoral turnout. Relevant literature suggests that competitiveness is the key 
86 However, this should not bother us here, as we are not interested on the precise theoretical mechanism behind the 
proportionality-turnout nexus, but rather on whether this relationship also applies in the context of biproportional 
reform in Switzerland. With this short paragraph I simply want to highlight the fact that previous research suggests 
that this relationship may not be as straightforward as it might appear prima facie. 

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
120 
variable driving the proportionality-turnout nexus (e.g., Selb 2009). Thus, factors fostering competition 
likely overlap with those stimulating voter turnout, and high local competition further amplifies turnout. 
Biproportional apportionment, by reducing the mechanical factors that impede the approximation of ideal 
vote-seat proportionality, is likely to enhance electoral competition, leading to an increase in the number of 
candidacies and electoral lists. With regard to the electoral lists, however, it must be noted that none of the 
reforming cantons provided for the possibility of apparentements, neither interparty nor intraparty appar￾entements. Under the d’Hondt method, apparentements were widely used as a strategic means to maximize 
votes and, consequently, parliamentary seats. The inflationary use of these apparentements and the resulting 
lack of transparency in electoral competition gave rise to voter criticism. Voters specifically criticized the 
challenge in determining which party ultimately benefited from votes cast for parties allied in sub-apparen￾tements. This skepticism towards apparentements was also prevalent among parties themselves, albeit re￾luctant to forgo such alliances voluntarily. The redundancy of apparentements under biproportionality pro￾vided an opportunity to eliminate this widely unpopular but highly effective electoral tool. Analysis of policy 
processes in the second part of the thesis revealed that the abolition of apparentements was generally met 
with unanimous approval from political parties. The removal of this option is likely to negatively affect the 
total number of electoral lists. 
Another characteristic of biproportionality is the mandate for list groups to have a consistent label across 
the entire electoral area. For instance, in the electoral law of the Canton of Aargau, the respective paragraph 
from the electoral law paradigmatically stipulates that “lists with the same label form a list group in the can￾ton” (Grossratswahlgesetz §13(1)). This requirement assumingly reduces the inter-district variance of lists, 
particularly as list labels previously sometimes explicitly referenced the constituency. In summary, it is likely 
that the absolute number of electoral lists will show a significant decline immediately following the reform. 
The stimulating effect of biproportionality is anticipated to manifest gradually in terms of the number of 
electoral lists, leading to a gradual increase over time. 
H7a: The introduction of biproportionality corresponds with an increase in the number of candidacies. 
H7b: The introduction of biproportionality corresponds with a short-term decrease followed by a longer￾term increase in the number of electoral lists. 
3.4 Data, variables, and analytical strategy 
This section delineates the structure of the data and the data sources. It further provides a description of the 
key variables, and outlines the analytical approach undertaken in this third part of the thesis addressing 
hypotheses 3-7. 
3.4.1 Data and data sources 
The units of analysis in this chapter are Swiss cantons, with each observation corresponding to a canton in 
a specific year between 1976 and 2023. The electoral data set comprises 1209 observations and 31 variables. 
Researchers working on Swiss cantons are well aware that Swiss federalism is also reflected at the data level. 
Typically, centralized, and systematized historical data at the cantonal level, extending beyond de￾mographics and election results, are scarce. As a primary data reference, I rely on two publicly available data 
sets: the data set “Quality of Democracy in the Swiss Cantons 1979-2006” by Schaub & Dlabac (2012), and 
the data set “Patterns of Democracy in the Swiss Cantons 1979-2018” by Vatter et al. (2020). This data is 
cross-checked randomly and adjusted as necessary. For missing years, data is supplemented following the 

=== PDF PAGE 132 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
121 
procedures outlined in the respective codebooks. While these two data sets are referenced, most of the data 
in the main data set created for this study (Lüscher 2024b) is self-collected, primarily through archival re￾search or official statistics provided by the FSO. Notably, there is no existing data on electoral competition 
in cantonal parliamentary elections (number of electoral lists and number of candidacies), requiring data 
collection through research in cantonal archives. Additionally, all district-related data is sourced from an 
additional data set (Lüscher 2024c) I separately created as detailed data on electoral districts for cantonal 
parliamentary elections does not exist – at least not systematically and for the time frame of interest. This 
district-related information is also gathered through a combination of archival research, data research, and 
historical news articles accessed through online archives, as well as outreach to libraries and cantonal chan￾celleries. A codebook for the main data set is provided in Table 42 the appendix. 
3.4.2 Variables 
As explained in the previous section, some variables are adopted from preexisting data sets, and their oper￾ationalizations are applied identically in this study for data completion. Other variables are self-collected 
and, in some cases, coded in a specific way to suit the analysis. This section aims to provide transparency 
regarding how the variables in the data set 2 (Lüscher 2024b) are operationalized and the limitations asso￾ciated with these operational definitions. 
Number of lists 
The variable “number of lists” indicates the total number of distinct electoral lists submitted across the entire 
electoral area. This can be understood as the raw number of “elective parties”, although not all lists are for￾mally constituted as parties. Alternatively, one could total the submitted lists from each district. In fact, 
many cantonal statistical offices use this straightforward indicator in their statistical publications. For intra￾cantonal comparisons, such a self-referential measure is suitable. However, for inter-cantonal, longitudinal 
comparisons, this approach has two shortcomings: 
˗ The number of lists submitted in the districts contains redundant information, as most contesting 
parties submit identical (or nearly identical) lists in more than one district. Thus, the number of 
lists is highly contingent on the number of electoral districts, which varies greatly between cantons, 
thus distorting inter-cantonal comparisons. 
˗ Several cantons have transitioned to biproportionality where the main tier of allocation is the can￾ton. Consequently, the number of lists reflects the count at the cantonal level and does not represent 
an aggregate of district-level list numbers, as is typical in unidimensional PR elections. A straight￾forward before-and-after comparison without accounting for these direct reform effects would be 
inappropriate. 
To conduct valid cross-sectional and longitudinal comparisons, a standardized measure is needed. There￾fore, I use the number of electoral lists submitted across the entire electoral area. As previously mentioned, 
cantonal statistical offices vary in how they report electoral lists. Some sum the lists across all districts, others 
report lists in the manner suggested here, and some do not report them at all or do so minimally. Conse￾quently, data is collected for the vast majority of cantons by consulting cantonal statistical offices, chancel￾leries, and archives to obtain official statistical results for all parliamentary elections in the period of interest. 
I manually counted the number of “distinct” electoral lists submitted in the electoral districts for almost all 

=== PDF PAGE 133 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
122 
cantons. The operationalization of “distinctiveness” of electoral lists is empirically by no means trivial. Be￾low, I explain the criteria used to determine whether electoral lists are subsumed under a single list or treated 
as distinct lists. 
Different lists are subsumed under a single list when: 
˗ If list labels clearly refer to the same party, even if they carry district-specific attributes, are treated 
as one list. For example, if the CVP contests with a district-specific attribute in every district, e.g. 
“CVP Oberrheintal” and “CVP Werdenberg”, etc., these lists are counted as one list. The situation 
differs if the CVP launches multiple nominally distinct lists within a district (as discussed below). 
˗ Lists combining members of different parties or groups on a single list in an electoral unit are 
treated as single lists. This is distinct from apparentements, where parties compete with separate 
lists and electorally merge them. For example, if the CVP, BDP, and EVP contest elections with a 
common list (not apparentement) in a certain district, it is counted as one list. 
Electoral lists were treated as distinct when: 
˗ If there is no obvious nominal commonality between lists, they are counted individually. 
˗ They are also counted separately if the same party contests with multiple distinct lists within a dis￾trict. For instance, “CVP West” and “CVP Ost” within the same district are counted as two separate 
lists (subject to the restriction outlined in the following point). 
˗ If a party uses different district-specific targeting strategies (e.g., geography, gender, age), these dis￾tinctions are considered only once. For example, if the CVP contests with lists labeled “CVP Men” 
and “CVP Women” in different districts, these distinctions are not treated as additional lists when 
such a differentiation is already present in another district. 
This operational approach strikes a balance between completely ignoring strategic list differentiations and 
overvaluing them due to district-specific targeting strategies. Consequently, the raw number of electoral lists 
may not always match the officially reported numbers, which can be viewed as a drawback. However, by 
consistently applying the above criteria, longitudinal and cross-sectional comparisons of electoral competi￾tion, as measured by electoral lists, are more valid. 
Number of candidacies for cantonal elections 
Collecting the number of candidacies running for office for a given election required less effort than collect￾ing data on electoral lists, as it is information of public interest and is therefore more often included in offi￾cial statistical documents. However, in some cases, it was necessary to consult official statistics (official ga￾zettes) in the cantonal archives to determine the number of candidacies. In the French-speaking part of 
Switzerland, relevant media reports are also taken into consideration. The number of candidacies surveyed 
relates exclusively to PR elections. In the case of mixed electoral systems (e.g., in the case of Appenzell 
Ausserrhoden), only the data for constituencies where proportional representation applies is collected and 
reported. In PR elections with pre-cumulated lists, cumulated candidate names are considered by subtract￾ing them from the total number of candidate names indicated (= filled list lines). 
Number of candidacies for National Council elections 
This variable shows the raw number of candidacies for National Council elections. Single-seat and majori￾tarian cantons (UR, OW, NW, GL, AI, GR) are excluded, following the practice of the FSO. 

=== PDF PAGE 134 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
123 
Number of candidacies per seat for cantonal elections 
This variable measures electoral competition for a political mandate. A higher number of candidacies per 
seat indicates greater electoral competition. The calculation involves dividing the total number of candida￾cies by the number of available mandates. For mixed electoral systems, the number of candidacies per seat 
pertains only to constituencies where PR applies. Originally, this variable was intended as the primary meas￾ure of electoral competition. However, its sensitivity to changes in parliament sizes in many cantons 
prompted me to exclude it from the analysis. Nevertheless, it will remain in the data set for reference. The 
same applies to the next variable. 
Number of candidacies per seat for National Council elections 
This variable represents the number of candidacies per seat and canton for National Council elections. The 
data is sourced from the official data set of the FSO on national elections, specifically the number of seats 
attributed to the cantons (FSO 2023d) and the number of candidacies per canton (FSO 2023c). The ratios 
are subsequently calculated. Single-seat and majoritarian cantons (UR, OW, NW, GL, AI, GR) are excluded, 
following the practice of the FSO. Until 2011, the data for calculating the distribution of seats among the 
cantons is based on the census conducted every 10 years under the old system. From 2015 onwards, the 
distribution of National Council seats among the cantons is based on the new annual census. 
Least Squares Index 
The LSq, also known as the “Gallagher index” (Gallagher 1991), is a widely used measure in electoral re￾search. It assesses the disproportionality of an electoral system by quantifying the deviation between the vote 
shares v and seat shares s of contesting parties i. Specifically, it calculates the squared differences between 
the vote shares and seat shares for each party, sums these squared differences, and takes the square root of 
half of the sum. The value of 0 indicates minimal disproportionality, while 100 indicates maximal dispro￾portionality. Mathematically, the LSq formula is expressed as: 
LSq=√ 1
2෍(si-vi)
2
or alternatively: 
LSq=√ 1
2෍(vi-si)
2
Effective number of parties 
The concept of the “effective number of parties” (ENP) is a common measure used to quantify the fragmen￾tation of a party system. Unlike a simple count of parties, the ENP accounts for the relative strength of each 
party based on its vote or seat share. This concept was introduced by Laakso and Taagepera in 1979 and 
remains widely used in political science research. The effective number of parties is calculated by squaring 
the seat share of each party, summing up these squared values, and then dividing 1 by that sum. The formula 
for calculating the ENP is: 
ENP=
