∑ (si
2
)

=== PDF PAGE 135 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
124 
Data for calculating the ENP is sourced from the “Patterns of Democracy” data set, with manual supple￾mentation for missing years (2019-2023). Similar to the approach of previous authors, the residual group of 
“others” is excluded, leading to a slight overestimation of the “true” number of effective parties based on the 
Laakso-Taagepera Index. 
Number and size of districts and other district-related data 
District-related data, such as the number and size of electoral districts, are gathered from public statistics, 
official election results, and archival research. These data are used to determine various district statistics, 
including the maximum and minimum district sizes, as well as the standard deviations from the mean. 
These district features are calculated separately in an Excel sheet and then imported into the main database. 
The data set includes information on the number of districts, the mean and median district sizes, the mini￾mum and maximum district sizes, the range between these extreme values, and the standard deviation as a 
measure of data dispersion. In cases where the electoral area consists of multiple tiers, the number of districts 
refers to the upper-tier electoral districts, consistent with the practice of the FSC, which bases its assessment 
of the (dis)proportionality of electoral systems on the highest allocation level within the electoral territory. 
Absolute and relative number of districts with less than 9 seats (applies PR cantons/districts only) 
This variable represents both the absolute and relative count of districts within a canton that have fewer 
than 9 parliamentary seats, relative to the total number of districts in that canton. Its aim is to indicate the 
number of PR districts that are deemed unconstitutional according to the current jurisdiction of the FSC. 
Cantons or districts applying plurality voting or biproportional apportionment are left blank. If the seat 
allocation follows a two-step method, where constituencies are grouped into electoral regions, the counts 
provided relate to the upper-tier level of these electoral regions. 
Absolute and relative number of seats from districts with less than 9 seats (applies PR cantons/districts only) 
This variable, similar to the previously mentioned one, measures the total number and share of seats stem￾ming from “non-conforming” districts within a canton. Its aim is to illustrate the sum and share of seats 
originating from districts deemed unconstitutional according to the jurisdiction of the FSC. Cantons or 
districts applying plurality voting or biproportional apportionment are left blank again. If the seat allocation 
follows a two-step method, where constituencies are grouped into electoral regions, the counts provided 
relate to the upper-tier level of these electoral regions. 
Turnout in cantonal elections 
Voter turnout data for cantonal parliaments is extracted from the “Patterns of Democracy” data set and then 
manually supplemented to include the missing years (2019-2023). 
Turnout in National Council elections 
The turnout for elections to the National Council serves as an auxiliary variable for estimating turnout in 
cantonal parliamentary elections. However, a limitation arises due to the asynchrony between cantonal and 
national parliamentary elections. While National Council elections occur in the same year across all can￾tons, cantonal parliaments are elected at different times and sometimes in different rhythms. To address this 
issue, one could theoretically use the National Council election closest to the cantonal parliamentary elec￾tion as a reference point. However, this approach would distort the date as well as the temporal reference to 

=== PDF PAGE 136 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
125 
the number of cantonal candidacies per National Council seat. Since the statistical models employed can 
accommodate complex data structures including staggered data, I decided to accept the asynchrony be￾tween elections of different tiers. For cantons that elect their National Council representatives by major￾ity/plurality vote, electoral turnout is not considered (AR, AI, GL, NW, OW, UR). 
Turnout in cantonal votes (referenda, popular initiatives, etc.) 
This variable represents the annually averaged turnout in cantonal votes, distinct from the turnout for na￾tional votes held at the cantonal level. To ascertain the cantonal voter turnout, the average of all votes on 
cantonal matters is computed for each year. Multiple votes occurring on the same date are considered indi￾vidually. Each voting issue carries equal weight in the calculation process (cf. codebook “Patterns of Democ￾racy”). Data from 1976 to 2018 is sourced from the “Patterns of Democracy” data set, while values for the 
years 2019 to 2023 are supplemented using official data from cantonal authorities. 
Number of seats per canton for National Council elections 
This variable represents the absolute number of mandates per canton. It is important to note that starting 
from the 2015 federal elections, there was a change in the data basis for seat apportionment. Refer to the 
remarks on the variable “number of candidacies per seat for National Council elections” for further details. 
Effective electoral threshold 
This variable captures the effective threshold implied by the electoral rules. Given that these rules vary be￾tween cantons and sometimes within cantons over time, different formulas are applied to account for this 
heterogeneity. Four rules apply to differentiate whether the election occurred under biproportionality or 
not, whether there are several constituencies or only one constituency, and whether a statutory quorum 
applies or not. Rule (1) relates to Lijphart’s (1994: 11) proposition of using the average district size for mul￾timember districts to approximate the effective threshold. Hence, M refers to the mean district size for the 
for the election i. Rule (2) pertains to single-district elections and follows a formula suggested by relevant 
literature (e.g. Pukelsheim 2004: 518). Here, M equals the assembly size. Rule (3) applies to all biproportional 
elections. M denotes the assembly size and L denotes the number of elective parties, which equals the num￾ber of electoral lists. The formula also originates in Pukelsheim’s work (ibid. 513-518), which references 
earlier work by Balinski & Young (2001: 154) and Lijphart & Gibberd (1977: 223). Rule (4) overrides all 
other rules, as it can be assumed that such a threshold only exists if it exceeds the effective threshold. For 
biproportional elections with two quorums, the global/canton-wide quorum is taken as reference.

3.4.3 Analytical strategy 
The analysis focuses on the twenty-six Swiss cantons, nine of which have adopted the biproportional appor￾tionment method. The R-package used for the statistical analysis, discussed in more detail below, automat￾ically excludes treated cases with fewer than five pre-treatment periods. Since the Canton of Graubünden 
lacks data for many key variables before treatment/reform, owing to its previous use of majoritarianism for 
parliamentary elections, this case is excluded from the analysis. The primary analytical goal of this section 
of the thesis is to determine the dynamic causal effect of the electoral reform on various aggregate-level 
variables by isolating this effect from other potential confounders, both observed and unobserved. 

=== PDF PAGE 138 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
127 
The method applied in this third part of the thesis is based on the framework of two-way fixed effects coun￾terfactual estimation for causal inference (fect) with time-series cross-sectional (TSCS) data. This frame￾work advances and outperforms traditional, more demanding models, such as those in the synthetic control 
method class, in several respects (Liu et al. 2024). Traditionally, the estimation of treatment effects in longi￾tudinal studies has relied heavily on fixed effects models, which often make strong assumptions about the 
temporal stability of treatment effects and the absence of time-varying confounders. However, these as￾sumptions can be unrealistic in many real-world scenarios and can lead to biased estimates and erroneous 
conclusions. The proposed framework addresses these limitations and offers three different (but similar) 
estimators: 
˗ FEct (fixed effects counterfactual) : This estimator extends the traditional fixed effects model by 
incorporating counterfactuals to estimate treatment effects. It accounts for individual-specific ef￾fects and controls for unobserved heterogeneity, ensuring robust treatment effect estimation by 
leveraging within-unit variation over time. 
Yit (0) = Xit
' β + αi + ξ
t + εit, for all (i,t)
87
 
˗ IFEct (interactive fixed effects counterfactual) : Building on the FEct model, the IFEct estimator 
integrates interactive fixed effects to handle unobserved time-varying confounders. This model 
captures complex temporal patterns by including factors that interact with individual fixed effects. 
It thus addresses potential biases from unobserved time-varying factors. 
Yit (0) = Xit
' β + αi + ξ
t + λi
'
ft + εit, for all (i,t)
88
 
˗ MC (matrix completion) : The MC estimator employs matrix completion techniques to fill in miss￾ing data and estimate treatment effects. This method is particularly useful in contexts with substan￾tial missing data or irregular treatment timings, as it reconstructs the missing entries by leveraging 
the low-rank structure of the data matrix, ensuring a robust analysis even with incomplete data sets.

˗ β is the vector of coefficients associated with the covariates Xit. Each element of β represents the effect of one of 
the covariates on the outcome. Essentially, β quantifies how changes in each covariate Xit are expected to influ￾ence the outcome Yit(0).

each unit 𝑖 but may vary across different units.

but may vary over time.

These models offer more reliable causal estimates compared to conventional two-way fixed effects models, 
especially when treatment effects are heterogeneous or unobserved time-varying confounders are likely to 
exist. 
In this framework, estimators treat the observations from the treated group as missing data and impute 
counterfactuals for each treated observation within a time-series cross-sectional (TSCS) data set. This im￾putation is achieved by fitting one of the previously discussed models using the data from untreated cases 
and a set of covariates associated with the outcome variable. Subsequently, the individual treatment effect 
for each treated observation is estimated by subtracting the predicted counterfactual outcome from its ob￾served outcome. Finally, the average treatment effect on the treated (ATT) or, alternatively, period-specific 
ATTs are calculated (Liu et al. 2024). 
A key innovation of this class of estimators is their independence from the observed outcomes of the treated 
units at the modelling stage, which allows for more flexible and robust estimates of treatment effects over 
time. Central to this approach is an iterative algorithm trained to identify the optimal model based on mean 
squared prediction errors (MSPE) via a k-fold cross-validation scheme with 2000 bootstraps. Furthermore, 
the estimation models based on the “fect” framework are equipped to effectively handle missing data, a com￾mon issue in panel data analysis. The framework also provides a set of inbuilt diagnostic tests to verify the 
validity of the identifying assumptions underpinning the estimation process. These tests include placebo 
tests, which check for spurious treatment effects by simulating treatment effects at placebo time points, and 
tests for (no) pretrend.90 The staggered adoption of reforms renders the tests for carryover effects inapplica￾ble. By subjecting the estimated treatment effects to these diagnostic tests, the framework helps to detect 
potential model misspecifications and enhances the credibility of the causal claims. 
Overall, the proposed framework represents a significant advancement in causal inference methodology. It 
offers a powerful toolkit for analyzing complex panel data and enhances the credibility of causal claims. By 
combining flexible estimation techniques with rigorous diagnostic tests, the method allows for more accu￾rate and reliable estimates of treatment effects, ultimately leading to a deeper understanding of causal rela￾tionships in panel data analysis (cf. Gobillon & Magnac 2016, Xu 2017, Athey et al. 2018, Liu et al. 2024). 
Both the data analysis and the data visualization are carried out with the statistics software “R” version 4.4.0 
and “R-Studio” version 2024.04.1-748. For counterfactual ATT estimation, I use the “fect” package written 
by Liu et al. (2022). The package effectively circumvents the problems addressed above, such as missing data, 
complex data structures, and staggered reform adoption. However, a drawback of the package is that it only 
generates data matrices for the relative ATTs and directly computes the delta between observed and esti￾mated data. There is no matrix that stores the estimated outcome in absolute numbers. Although the ATT

effect. To circumvent this limitation, I reconstruct the estimated averages for the outcome variables of the 
reformed cantons over the entire available period (i.e., before and after the reform). I do so by isolating the 
ATTs and subtracting them from the observed averages. This subtraction provides the estimated outcomes 
in absolute numbers. In simpler terms, I calculate the estimated outcome by subtracting the ATTs from the 
observed outcome.91 Although this process is rather inconvenient because of the complex data structures 
and thus requires a lot of coding work, it is the only way to quantify the substantivity of the ATT. In the next 
step, I plot the ATTs to better visualize the dynamic of the effect. Another impediment arises from the stag￾gered implementation of the reforms between 2007 and 2022. The inbuilt algorithm addresses this issue by 
standardizing the time variable (year) relative to the time of reform (t = 0). However, this leads to a decreas￾ing number of cases with increasing years into the post-reform period, implying higher statistical uncer￾tainty. 
3.5 Results 
3.5.1 How biproportional reforms affect vote-seat disproportionality 
One of the key goals for implementing biproportional apportionment in parliamentary elections is to min￾imize the discrepancy between a party’s share of votes and its share of seats. These discrepancies, however, 
cannot be entirely explained by the mechanical features of the electoral system itself but by a combination 
of these features and actual voting figures (i.e., number and geographical dispersion of votes, number of 
competing lists, etc.). Thus, the least squares index as a measure of vote-seat disproportionality reflects not 
only mechanical effects but is also subject to electoral volatilities. To quantitatively assess the influence of 
the mechanical features of an electoral system on observed disproportionality, I conduct a regression anal￾ysis of the LSq-index against the effective electoral threshold, while controlling for unit (i.e., canton) fixed 
effects. The model yields an R
2
 of 0.34 and a slope for the effective threshold of ≈ 0.191. An increase of five 
percentage points in the effective threshold thus corresponds to approximately a one percentage point in￾crease in the LSq-index (p < 0.001) when controlling for canton fixed-effects.92 This finding indicates a sig￾nificant relationship between the effective threshold, used as an indirect measure of an electoral system’s 
mechanical effect, and disproportionality, as anticipated. Moreover, the effective threshold accounts for ap￾proximately one third of the observed variance in disproportionality. However, it is worth noting that there 
are other factors, collectively more influential, that also impact the disproportionality level of an election. 
91 The reason for subtraction can be exemplified as follows: If the ATT is 3.5 and the mean observed outcome of the 
treated at time t is 10.0, the absolute estimated (counterfactual) outcome at time t would be 6.5. Thus, the difference 
between the observed and the estimated outcome corresponds to a positive ATT of 3.5. 
92 The following formula applies: lsq_indexit = β0 + β1 × eff_thresholdit + ∑iαi

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
130 
Figure 40: General time trend for vote-seat disproportionality for all PR-based cantonal parliamentary elections. The 
graph shows the locally weighted polynomial regression curve with 95% confidence intervals. 
In the Swiss cantons, vote-seat disproportionality is generally moderate and aligns with international aver￾ages (cf. Lijphart 1999: 163, Vatter 2008: 17-18). Figure 40 illustrates an upward trend from the mid-1970s, 
peaking in 1990, followed by a continuous decline. This decline, both in relative and absolute terms, is no￾table. Particularly noteworthy is the steeper decline observed since 2005, possibly indicating the impact of 
biproportional reforms. During the period depicted, several electoral system reforms occurred. Notably, 
since 2005, eleven cantonal parliaments have undergone reductions in size, some quite substantial (e.g., SG 
from 180 to 120 seats per 2007). These parliamentary reductions, in turn, required adjustments at the level 
of electoral districts. While revisions of cantonal electoral law are not rare, the period since the turn of the 
millennium has seen notably high levels of cantonal reform activity, with biproportionality being one man￾ifestation of this trend. 
An examination of cantonal trends in disproportionality levels reveals significant inter- and, in some cases, 
intra-cantonal variations (Figure 41). This variance can mainly be attributed to differences in parliamentary 
sizes, constituency structures, seat allocation methods, and statutory quorums. For instance, the canton of 
Geneva, voting as a single electoral district, imposes a canton-wide quorum of 7%, creating a substantial 
electoral hurdle that impedes or even prevents the emergence of small parties and can result in high dispro￾portionality values. It is also notable that the overall trend towards lower disproportionality levels is ob￾served in most cantons. However, some cantons exhibit a contrary trend. Nevertheless, it is evident that all 
cantons adopting biproportional apportionment have witnessed a decline in disproportionality over time, 
with some experiencing a significant reduction. 
While these descriptive statistics suggest a negative impact of biproportional reforms on vote-seat dispro￾portionality, they do not conclusively prove an effect. If disproportionality was exclusively determined by 
institutional factors, it could be predicted prior to elections and would be constant if these institutional pa￾rameters remained unchanged. However, while mechanical aspects are indeed highly determinative for dis￾proportionality and set the boundaries within which disproportionality empirically fluctuates, it is exoge￾nous factors that ultimately determine the actual level of disproportionality of an election. 

=== PDF PAGE 142 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
131 
Figure 41: Trend in disproportionality levels in PR-based cantonal parliamentary elections. The dashed line shows 
the observed LSq values; the solid line shows the locally weighted linear regression line with 95% confidence inter￾vals.
To estimate the causal effect of biproportional reforms on disproportionality levels, i.e. the ATT, I incorpo￾rate the following variables: the reform dummy as the binary treatment indicator; the number of effective 
legislative parties, the range between the largest and smallest district in terms of seats and the number of 
candidacies in cantonal elections as the covariates. The MSPE-based cross-validation procedure suggests 
using the matrix completion method (MC).93

Figure 42 shows the observed and counterfactual trends relative to the time of reform adoption, depicted as 
absolute averages (left panel) and residual averages (right panel). The left panel reveals that during the pre￾treatment period, the treated cases’ averages range between LSq values of 3 and 4.5, showing a strong corre￾spondence until a sudden decline in observed averages at the time of intervention, followed by a minor 
increase in the final post-treatment years. It is important to note that these averages are based on only three 
treated cases (ZH, SH, AG). After treatment, the counterfactual values fluctuate between 2.5 and 3.5. 
In the right panel we see the ATT. Residual averages in the pretreatment period remain close to zero, with a 
pronounced negative treatment effect on LSq values emerging immediately after treatment. The overall 
ATT is approximately -2.048 (p < 0.001) and remains negative throughout the post-treatment period. 
The regression results (Table 43 in the appendix) indicate that all covariates are positively associated with 
the outcome variable. Noteworthily, no significant relationship appears between the effective number of 
parties and the LSq-index. This implies that a fragmented party system does not necessarily translate to 
higher disproportionality rates, nor does a lower level of fragmentation inherently correspond with lower 
disproportionality rates. 
The placebo test (Figure 54 in the appendix) indicates that we cannot reject the null hypothesis of zero pla￾cebo effect (p ≈ 0.507), and we cannot reject the null hypothesis of inequivalence (p ≈ 0.116). Hence, the 
placebo test passed but not the test for equivalence. To test for the absence of a pretrend, the F test does not 
reject the null hypothesis of no pretrend (p ≈ 0.904). Meanwhile, the TOST rejects the null hypothesis of 
inequivalence (p ≈ 0.015), indicating a good pre-treatment fit. 
The findings confirm the third hypothesis according to which the implementation of biproportional appor￾tionment leads to notable reductions in disproportionality averages. Post-reform, average disproportional￾ity levels are approximately half of the pre-reform values. Thus, despite the diverse sources contributing to 
vote-seat disproportionality and the variations in reform designs, the adoption of these reforms notably 
enhanced the electoral systems’ ability to accurately reflect voter preferences. 
However, does the increase in proportionality after reform show a stronger effect in cantons with lower 
average district sizes before the reform, given that district size is a key factor in affecting disproportionality 
(H4)? To address this question, the assessment includes all treated cantons except Graubünden due to miss￾ing LSq-index data prior to reform. As Uri is somewhat of an outlier, providing only one post-reform ob￾servation and due to its unique mixed electoral system with PR in larger districts (≥ 5 seats) and majority 
representation in smaller ones, I conduct the assessment with and without Uri. 
Exploring the relationship between mean district sizes and disproportionality levels based on the treatment 
status, Figure 43 presents the marginal effects. While a perfect parallel trend would imply the absence of an 
effect, a very small effect emerges when Uri is included. However, when Uri is excluded, the effect is more 
substantial, albeit not overwhelming: The decline in disproportionality, indicated by a relatively large delta, 
is more pronounced in elections where the mean district sizes were smaller compared to elections with 
larger district averages. It is also noteworthy that including Uri yields a slightly negative relationship be￾tween disproportionality and mean district sizes after the reform. However, this weak relationship vanishes 
when Uri is excluded. The zero slope and low intercept indicate that after reform, disproportionality is low 
irrespective of mean district sizes. This empirical finding is in line with theoretical expectations. Bipropor￾tional apportionment mitigates the adverse impact of electoral district sizes on vote-seat proportionality by 

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
133 
employing an allocation mechanism that distributes seats to parties based on their overall electoral perfor￾mance. Therefore, these results lend support to H4. 
Figure 43: Marginal effects of reform on LSq-index by mean district size with (left panel) and without the Canton of 
Uri (right panel). The graphs show the locally weighted linear regression lines with 95% confidence intervals. 
3.5.2 Proportionality as a catalyst for party system fragmentation? 
The previous chapter demonstrated that, as anticipated, disproportionality decreased significantly following 
the implementation of the biproportional reform, albeit to varying degrees across the cantons that adopted 
it. This prompts an investigation into potential secondary effects, particularly the “effective number of leg￾islative parties”. The theoretical framework suggests a positive correlation between the proportionality of an 
electoral system and the number of effective legislative parties, leading to the hypothesis that the number of 
parties would increase post-reform (H5a). However, it is conceivable, and quite likely, that this effect may 
not manifest immediately but rather with a certain delay (H5b). 
First and foremost, a gradual increase is evident in the overall trend of the effective number of parties (see 
Figure 44). While the effective number averaged well below four parties in the 1980s, it has since increased. 
Despite a brief period of stagnation just before the turn of the millennium, the positive trend has persisted. 
Examining individual cantonal trends (see Figure 45) confirms that this upward trend is supported by most 
cantons. Aargau, Schaffhausen, and Zurich show no or only a very weak increase, while Basel Stadt shows a 
decrease over time. 
Figure 44: General time trend for the effective number of legislative parties for all cantonal parliamentary elections. 
The graph shows the locally weighted polynomial regression curve with 95% confidence intervals. AI is excluded due 
to missing data. 

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
134 
Figure 45: Trend in the effective number of legislative parties in the Swiss cantons. The dashed line shows the ob￾served ENP values; the solid line shows the locally weighted linear regression line with 95% confidence intervals. AI is 
excluded due to missing data. 
In his comprehensive assessment of the Swiss national and cantonal party systems, Ladner (2001: 127) con￾cluded that “cantonal party systems become more alike, converging towards a medium effective number of 
parties between four and five (…).” While the descriptive graphs of cantonal trends support a gradual con￾vergence in effective party numbers, Ladner’s forecast appears somewhat conservative. The “steady in￾crease” he had diagnosed (ibid. 2001: 125) did not plateau between an effective number of parties of four to 
five, as predicted, but clearly surpassed this range, and indicate a further rise towards an effective number of 
six parties. The evident question at this juncture is: Was this trend propelled by the cantonal biproportional 
reforms? Could Ladner’s prediction have possibly materialized if these reforms had not been implemented 
in the nine cantons, which he could not possibly have foreseen? 
To estimate the causal effect of biproportional reforms on the ENP, I use the following variables: the reform 
dummy as the binary treatment indicator, the least squares index, the range between the largest and smallest 
district in terms of seats, the turnout in cantonal parliamentary elections, and the number of candidacies in 

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
135 
cantonal elections as the covariates. The MSPE-based cross-validation procedure suggests using the inter￾active fixed effects counterfactual model (IFEct) and indicates three latent factors (r = 3).94
Figure 46: Observed and estimated average effective number of legislative parties (left panel) and the corresponding 
ATT for treated cases (right panel) before (t < 0) and after (t ≥ 0) treatment/reform. The bar plot in the figures dis￾playing the test diagnostics shows the number of cases treated per year.
Looking at Figure 46, the clear answer is “no”. Although there is a slightly positive ATT immediately follow￾ing the reforms, this trend is interrupted by a subsequent negative trend, which accounts for almost one 
effective party. The wide range of the confidence intervals, coupled with the fact that they never differ sig￾nificantly from the zero ATT baseline during the entire post-treatment period, indicates that the estimated 
ATTs are not statistically significant (overall ATT ≈ -0.118, p ≈ 0.763). The number of candidacies demon￾strates a substantial effect on ENP, both in terms of the coefficient and statistical significance (see Table 44 
in the appendix). In contrast, the other covariates do not exhibit strong effects.
Thus, the ongoing proliferation of parties in Swiss cantonal parliaments seems unrelated to the increased 
proportionality resulting from biproportional reforms. While this does not exclude potential reform effects 
within individual cantons, there is no evidence of a systematic cross-cantonal relationship. The lack of a 
consistent effect may be due to the quorum requirements in most cantons, which can significantly hinder 
party formation as well as the entry of small, non-partisan groups. 
Figure 55 in the appendix shows that the null hypothesis of no differences cannot be rejected (p ≈ 0.182), 
indicating no significant differences between the placebo ATTs and the estimated ATTs. The placebo equiv￾alence test is slightly above the 5% threshold (p ≈ 0.069). Similarly, as shown in the right panel of the graph, 
the test for a potential pretrend yields mixed results. The high F test p-value suggests there is no statistically 
significant evidence of a problematic pretrend (null hypothesis = no pretrend). However, the p-value of ≈ 
0.084 indicates that we cannot confidently assert that the pretrends are statistically equivalent. This ambi￾guity might be due to the relatively small sample size and/or the omission of other key covariates. Overall, 
the data indicates that there is no significant reform effect on the effective number of legislative parties. 
Thus, hypotheses 5a and 5b are rejected.

3.5.3 Higher mapping accuracy as a stimulant for electoral turnout? 
As demonstrated in the previous chapter, the significantly lower disproportionality values in cantonal bi￾proportional elections do not have a systematic effect on the number of parties represented in parliament. 
How does this relate to voter turnout? Theoretically, improved prospects for an efficient allocation of votes 
should incentivize higher voter turnout (H6a). However, this effect is expected to manifest gradually over 
time (H6b), rather than immediately. This is because the mechanism is indirect, depending on voters’ per￾ceptions of and reactions to the electoral system’s incentives. While the introduction of biproportional ap￾portionment directly results in a more accurate reflection of the voters’ will and enhances the chances of 
electoral success of smaller parties (and their voters, respectively), a broader effect on turnout is likely to 
emerge only over time as voters gain experience and adjust their perceptions of the electoral system and its 
incentives.
Figure 47: General time trend for the electoral turnout in cantonal parliamentary elections. The graph shows the lo￾cally weighted polynomial regression curve with 95% confidence intervals. AI and GR are excluded due to missing 
data. 
The estimation of the causal effect of biproportional reforms on the electoral turnout includes following 
variables: the reform dummy as the binary treatment indicator, turnout in National Council elections, the 
number of candidacies in cantonal elections, the effective number of legislative parties, and the effective 
electoral threshold as the covariates. The MSPE-based cross-validation procedure suggests using the matrix 
completion method and indicates the presence of one latent factor (r = 1).95
As depicted in Figure 47, voter turnout has steadily declined across cantons over time. Although the rate of 
decline has slowed somewhat since the turn of the millennium, the trend persists. Before 1980, the average 
turnout was over 55%, but it has now fallen to around 40%. This widespread decline in cantonal election 
turnout has prompted authorities to introduce measures such as postal voting and free postage for postal 
votes, though these efforts have not achieved lasting success.96
95 The following formula applies: turnout_ce = β0 + β1 × ref_dummy + β2 × turnout_ne + β3 × cand_num_ce + β4 × 
enp + β5 × eff_threshold + L + ε. The turnout in cantonal votes (turnout_cv) was not included as an additional varia￾ble (as originally intended), as its inclusion significantly impairs the model’s performance. 
96 Selb and Lachat (2004) demonstrated in their analysis of the Swiss national elections in 2003 that electoral turnout 
increased particularly in cantons where the option for “simplified postal voting” was provided (see also Lutz & Selb 
2006: 442). 

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
137 
Figure 48 also highlights significant variations in voter turnout levels between cantons and a consistent de￾cline in most. The decline is especially pronounced in cantons with traditionally high participation rates 
(e.g., Jura and Glarus), while cantons with generally low participation rates have experienced stagnation or 
only a slight decline. Schaffhausen presents a unique case, where voting is compulsory, and non-compliance 
incurs a symbolic fine of six Swiss francs. Despite this measure, voter turnout in Schaffhausen has also 
dropped by a substantial 20 percentage points over the past four decades. 
Figure 48: Time trend of turnout in cantonal parliamentary elections. The dashed line shows the observed turnout 
rates; the solid line shows the locally weighted linear regression line with 95% confidence intervals. AI and GR are 
excluded due to missing data. 
The macro-level causes of this downward trend are manifold. A distinction must be made between general 
dynamics of alienation from institutional politics observed in many developed democracies, such as general 
political apathy, saturation, and overburdening effects, or socio-economic changes like increasing mobility 
on the one hand – and specific causes within the polity on the other hand (Freitag 2005). In the context of 
cantonal parliamentary elections, these include a certain relativization of cantonal identity, or the demise of 
local media outlets, leading to a reduced coverage of regional events and, consequently, to a depoliticization 
(Kübler & Goodman 2018). The question now is: would the decline in voter turnout have been even more 
pronounced in the reformed cantons had the biproportional reforms not taken place? Is there a positive 
effect on electoral turnout? 
The MSPE-based cross-validation procedure suggests using the matrix completion method (MC). As Figure 
49 illustrates, there is no evidence of a positive effect of biproportional reforms on voter turnout. In fact, an 
immediate negative effect appears in the first election following the new procedure, which stabilizes over 

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
138 
time and approaches the zero-effect line. The mean ATT is -4.455 (p ≈ 0.260). However, this effect is statis￾tically not significant at any point during the post-reform period, as the 95% confidence interval consistently 
overlaps the zero-effect line. 
Figure 49: Observed and estimated average electoral turnout in cantonal parliamentary elections (left panel) and the 
corresponding ATT for treated cases (right panel) before (t < 0) and after (t ≥ 0) treatment/reform. GR, NW, and UR 
are excluded from the counterfactual estimation due to missing data on the covariates. The bar plot in the figures dis￾playing the test diagnostics shows the number of cases treated per year. 
In terms of p-values, voter turnout in National Council elections is most strongly and positively correlated 
with voter turnout in cantonal parliamentary elections as shown in Table 45 in the appendix. The number 
of candidacies and the effective number of parties show a negative correlation with cantonal voter turnout, 
which is unexpected given that electoral competition and a fragmented party system typically promote po￾litical participation according to the literature. On the other hand, the negative relationship between a 
higher effective threshold and voter turnout aligns with theoretical expectations. 
Upon examining the robustness tests (Figure 56) and comparing them with previous tests for other outcome 
variables, no placebo effect or problematic pretrend is observed. However, it is evident that the residual 
averages in the pretreatment period are not statistically equivalent to zero, as indicated by the high p-values 
from the TOST equivalence tests. Therefore, despite the absence of indications of problematic pretrends in 
the tests, the data used for estimation is not sufficient for robust effect estimation. For a more precise effect 
estimation, integrating additional covariates into the model is required. However, identifying supplemen￾tary variables for the entire period under study that effectively covary with electoral turnout beyond those 
already included poses a challenge. Overall, the model does not provide evidence of a significant positive 
reform effect on cantonal voter turnout, leading to the rejection of hypotheses 6a and 6b. However, the 
evaluation indicates a dynamic effect over time, albeit with a high degree of statistical uncertainty.
3.5.4 Biproportionality and its impact on electoral competition 
Thus far, no significant positive reform effect has been found on either the effective number of parties or 
political participation. Initially, a positive effect on political competition was also anticipated, but these pre￾liminary findings cast doubt on a possible effect. If biproportional reforms did not boost participation and 
did not substantially alter the number of parties, it would be a rather surprising finding if political competi￾tion as a variable related to the other outcome variables showed strong positive effects. Nonetheless, the 

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
139 
assumption is that the reforms positively influence the number of candidacies (H7a). Due to further tech￾nical adjustments in the course of reform, such as the abolition of the possibility of apparentements and the 
requirement for uniform list group labels, an immediate decrease in the number of lists post-reform is an￾ticipated. A gradual positive effect on the number of lists is expected over time (H7b). 
If we look at Figure 50, we can observe a continuous increase in both the number of candidacies and the 
number of electoral lists before the turn of the millennium. However, the increase in the number of candi￾dacies halts before the millennium and, on average, even slightly decreases. From the turn of the millen￾nium, the two trends decouple to some extent. While the number of candidacies shows a strong and stable 
increase, the number of lists stagnates. 
Figure 50: General time trend for the electoral competition in cantonal parliamentary elections. The graph shows the 
locally weighted polynomial regression curve with 95% confidence intervals for the number of candidacies (left 
panel) and the number of electoral lists (right panel). AI and GR are excluded due to missing data. 
Why do these two figures not correlate perfectly with each other? The reason is that parties do not have to 
fill all list places with candidates when drawing up their party lists. For instance, in a constituency with ten 
seats (i.e., a maximum of ten list places), a party can field as few as one candidate. If these figures correlated 
perfectly, the total number of candidacies divided by the number of electoral lists should roughly equal the 
number of total seats, i.e., the parliament size. Thus, if we divide the ratio between the number of candidacies 
and electoral lists by parliament size, the resulting value should theoretically be close to one. 
Figure 51 shows the actual ratio, categorized by reform status. The observed values reveal a distribution with 
a median below one and lower for non-reformed cases. This indicates that for a significant share of lists, not 
all available spots are being filled. The figure also shows a higher ratio for reformed cases, suggesting that 
electoral lists submitted post-reform have fewer empty spots. Further deviations can be explained by the 
specific operationalization of the number of lists, which adjusts the total for strategic submissions in elec￾tions with many sub-lists, providing an approximation of the “effective number” of lists. If the actual num￾ber of lists were used as a basis, the ratio would be even lower than shown. 

=== PDF PAGE 151 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
140 
Figure 51: Total number of candidacies per electoral list, adjusted for parliament size, categorized by reform status 
(0 = pre-reform or no reform, 1 = post-reform). The formula used for the variable on the y-axis is (cand_num_ce / 
lists_num) × (1 / parl_size). 
How is political competition developing in the cantons? Figure 52 illustrates trends for both the number of 
candidacies and the number of lists. There are significant differences across cantons. In smaller, rural can￾tons with traditionally small parliaments (e.g., Appenzell Ausserrhoden, Obwalden, Schwyz), political com￾petition remains relatively stable and at a lower level over time. Conversely, in more populous cantons, there 
is generally higher political competition and greater dynamics over time. 
The trends in the two largest cantons by population, Bern and Zurich, are particularly notable. In Bern, the 
number of electoral lists submitted is relatively low at around 20, yet the number of candidacies has nearly 
doubled over the past 40 years. This sharp increase is striking, as no other canton has experienced a similar 
rise within the same timeframe. Since cantonal elections have so far been largely neglected in Swiss election 
campaign research, the causes can only be speculated upon. It is conceivable that due to the strong mediati￾zation and politicization of national elections, along with the significant increase in submitted lists and can￾didacies for National Council elections over the past five decades (Lutz & Selb 2006: 431-432) and consid￾ering that Bern is the seat of the national parliament, spillover effects may have occurred in cantonal elec￾tions in Bern and spurred cantonal competition. However, this remains just an assumption. 

=== PDF PAGE 152 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
141 
Figure 52: Time trends of number of candidacies (y-axis on the left) and electoral lists (y-axis on the right) in can￾tonal PR-based parliamentary elections. The dashed lines show the observed values; the solid lines show the locally 
weighted linear regression lines with 95% confidence intervals. AI and GR are excluded due to missing data.
In contrast, Zurich has seen a relatively stable number of candidacies, ranging between 1500 and 2000. How￾ever, the number of lists dropped sharply after 2007, coinciding with the introduction of biproportional 
apportionment. This decline is somewhat mitigated by the fact that the number of lists during the 2003-
2007 legislative period was exceptionally high and far above the cantonal average. Nevertheless, the sharp 
decline in the number of lists is likely due to the reform that abolished apparentements and mandated uni￾form list group labels. A similar, though less pronounced, trend is observed in other reformed cantons such 
as Aargau, Uri, Zug, and Schaffhausen. In contrast, no effect is evident in the cases of Schwyz, Valais, and 
Nidwalden, possibly because the number of lists in these cantons was already relatively low before the re￾form, ranging between five and ten lists. 
The question now arises whether the biproportional apportionment significantly increased political com￾petition, measured by the number of candidacies and electoral lists. To estimate the causal reform effect, the 
following variables are included: the reform dummy as the binary treatment indicator, the effective number 
of legislative parties, voter turnout in cantonal elections, the range between the minimum and maximum 
district sizes, the least squares index, the number of candidacies for the National Council elections, and the 
effective electoral threshold as covariates.97 For the first model, where the outcome variable is the number 
of candidacies, the MSPE-based cross-validation procedure suggests using the interactive fixed-effects

counterfactual model (IFEct) with two latent factors (r = 2). For the second model, with the number of elec￾toral lists as the outcome variable, the cross-validation procedure favors the matrix completion method. 
Figure 53 illustrates the reform effects on candidacies in the top two panels. In the upper left panel, the 
number of candidacies in reformed cantons has risen sharply since the reform – from around 500 before 
the reform to just over 1000 in the most recent post-reform period. However, the increase would have been 
even more pronounced without the reform, resulting in a negative ATT, as shown in the upper right panel. 
The mean ATT for the entire post-reform period is -162.6, with a p-value of 0.262. This suggests that, on 
average, around 163 more people would have stood for election without the reform, holding all other factors 
constant. However, this effect is subject to high statistical uncertainty. 
The lower two panels present an even clearer picture regarding the number of lists: the number of submitted 
electoral lists decreased by an average of 6.71 (ATT) with a p-value of 0.063. This effect is relatively con￾sistent over time but shows a stronger negative trend in the most recent post-reform years. The coefficients 
for all variables for both outcome variable are shown in Table 46 in the appendix. 
Figure 53: Observed and estimated average electoral competition in cantonal parliamentary elections (left panels) 
and the corresponding ATT for treated cases (right panels) before (t < 0) and after (t ≥ 0) treatment/reform. AI, GE, 
TI, and UR are excluded from the counterfactual estimation due to missing data on the covariates. The bar plot in the

There is a strong, positive statistical correlation between the two outcome variables and the covariates ENP 
and the number of candidacies in the respective canton in the National Council elections. In the first model, 
which considers the number of candidacies, the disproportionality index is also positively significant at the 
5% level. All other covariates fall outside the range of statistical significance. 

=== PDF PAGE 154 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
143 
Upon examining the robustness tests (Figure 57 in the appendix) and comparing them with previous tests 
for other outcome variables, no placebo effect or problematic pretrend is observed. However, the residual 
averages in the pretreatment period deviate significantly from zero, as indicated by the high p-values from 
the TOST equivalence tests, especially regarding the reform effect on the number of candidacies. Although 
the tests do not suggest problematic pretrends, the model could benefit from incorporating more and better￾correlated covariates. 
Overall, the model does not indicate a positive reform effect on electoral competition, whether measured by 
the number of candidacies or the number of electoral lists. In fact, the results suggest that biproportional 
reforms might reduce electoral competition, leading to the rejection of hypothesis 7a, which assumed a pos￾itive effect. Conversely, the results support hypothesis 7b, positing a possible negative and immediate reform 
effect on the number of electoral lists. After the reform, the average number of lists dropped by about five, 
further decreasing to below ten lists in the last post-treatment period, with p-values below the 10% thresh￾old. 
The significant decrease in the number of electoral lists post-reform can likely be attributed to the reform’s 
design, which regulates electoral competition in terms of electoral lists. Parties can no longer split their main 
list into multiple, differently labeled sub-lists to strategically target and mobilize voters based on specific 
demographics. Although parties can technically still separate a branch and run it as an independent list, 
empirical evidence does not support the existence of such behavior. Fractionalizing a party increases the 
risk of losing non-integer seat entitlements to rival parties, even with standard rounding. As a unified party, 
the risk of yielding a quotient of less than 0.5 occurs only once, but this risk increases with each additional 
split. Party strategists are likely aware of this. Given that all lists within electoral districts must conform to 
the same canton-wide label to be treated as a list group in the super-apportionment of seats, there is an 
incentive for a cross-district presence of competing parties. The potential benefit of “electoral omnipres￾ence” outweighs the costs, even if the additional votes gained from the extra list in a district contribute only 
marginally to the overall result. This shift from focusing on individual districts to targeting the canton-wide 
electorate may, over time, lead to a “de-regionalization” or “cantonalization” of the entire party system. 
However, further research is needed to investigate this causal link. 
3.6 Conclusion 
The third part of this dissertation aimed to examine the impact of reforms on cantonal politics. The focus 
was on whether and how biproportional reforms influence macro-level characteristics such as voter turnout, 
political competition, and the effective number of political parties. The hypothesis posits that lowering the 
threshold implied by the constituency structure contributes to a significant increase in vote-seat propor￾tionality. This increased mapping accuracy of the electoral system is expected to benefit marginalized voters, 
particularly those supporting smaller fringe parties. The assumption is that these voters are encouraged to 
participate more actively, either by voting when they had previously abstained or by voting more sincerely 
instead of settling for their second preference. Based on relevant theory, it is expected that the increased 
proportionality translates to a “democratic surge”: higher participation rates, greater political competition, 
and an increase in the number of effective parties in parliaments. 
The analysis shows that vote-seat proportionality significantly decreases, nearly halving on average post￾reform. This effect is both immediate and consistent over time, with the proportionality increase being more 

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
144 
pronounced in cantons with smaller average constituency sizes. Biproportionality thus ensures a high de￾gree of accuracy, largely independent of prior constituency structures. 
Regarding secondary effects presumed to result from reduced disproportionality, the study presents mixed 
findings. There is no evidence that the reform significantly increases the number of effective parties. The 
number of parties in the cantons continues to rise, irrespective of whether a reform occurs. For political 
participation, a direct negative effect of the reform is observed; however, this effect is not strong and dimin￾ishes over time. A statistically insignificant negative effect on the number of candidacies is noted for political 
competition. The effects on the number of lists are more pronounced, with the reforms clearly leading to a 
decline in the number of electoral lists submitted. Thus, biproportional reforms and the improved chances 
of fair electoral representation do not necessarily stimulate political competition. The negative effect on the 
number of lists can be explained by specific reform provisions that create incentives to consolidate electoral 
lists at the cantonal level and encourage their presence in as many constituencies as possible. This example 
highlights how even seemingly minor technical changes can carry significant strategic implications for po￾litical parties, which are forced to navigate the efficient use of their limited resources. 
The results indicate that political institutions, such as electoral systems, engage in a complex, reciprocal in￾teraction with voters and party strategists. Party strategists, well-versed in the technical details of new elec￾toral rules, anticipate political effects and react accordingly. Conversely, the average voter lacks such spe￾cialized knowledge and resources, which may limit awareness of how the reform affects their personal op￾tions and the prospects of success of these options. The absence of a democratic surge, despite improved 
proportionality, could be attributed to various factors, including: 
- Voters are unaware of the improved proportionality. 
- Voters recognize the improved proportionality but do not significantly alter their behavior. 
- The reform includes elements that counteract the expected behavioral changes. 
- The secondary effects may only become evident after a longer period. 
At this point, I highlight some limitations of the analysis. Firstly, the number of cases is rather small for a 
large-N study. Although the methodology used can handle low case numbers and complex, even incomplete 
data structures, a higher number of cases would yield more robust results. However, increasing the number 
of cases is feasible only in a context beyond the Swiss case. Since the reforms in Switzerland have a very 
specific and unique design, extending the study to other, similar electoral reforms might face criticisms of 
lack of comparability. Instead of expanding the number of cases, increasing the number of observations, 
such as including additional election years, would also enhance robustness. Since this study has exhausted 
all available data, such optimizations in the longitudinal dimension will only be possible in the future with 
new electoral data. 
Another limitation is that some non-reformed cases used for the counterfactual estimation partly respond 
to the FSC ruling by optimizing proportionality through alternative reforms. Additionally, other institu￾tional reforms, such as parliamentary reductions, took place in numerous cantons. While such concurrent 
reforms limit the validity of the study, considering all these circumstances would significantly complicate 
the study design and might ultimately require the exclusion of some cases. An avenue for optimization could 
involve incorporating additional covariates into the analysis, particularly exogenous variables. However, the 

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
145 
process of data collection has revealed significant challenges in identifying suitable variables. These chal￾lenges arise from both data availability constraints and theoretical complexities, particularly in finding var￾iables that conform to strict exogeneity. 
In summary, the biproportional reforms resulted in immediate, albeit primarily mechanical, effects. How￾ever, the anticipated psychological response from voters did not materialize as expected, leading to a lack of 
significant political effects. This outcome is disappointing for those who anticipated that the reforms would 
invigorate cantonal politics. Conversely, it offers reassurance to those concerned about potential drawbacks 
such as an excessive fragmentation of legislative parties and the alleged inefficiencies in parliamentary work 
that could result from it. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
146 
4. Concluding remarks 
In this final chapter, I synthesize the three parts of the dissertation to draw a cross-chapter conclusion of the 
thesis. This study represents the first comprehensive analysis of the biproportional apportionment method 
in Switzerland. I assess three central questions: How do political parties involved in the reform process affect 
the outcome, and what patterns emerge? How do conflict patterns during decision-making shape the final 
reform design? And how do the biproportional reforms impact cantonal political landscapes? 
The analysis of political parties’ strategic behavior highlights a critical aspect of electoral reform: the inter￾play of power and interest. The study confirms the hypothesis that parties benefiting from the existing elec￾toral system tend to resist reforms, while those disadvantaged are more likely to advocate for change. This 
finding aligns with broader theories of institutional change, which posit that established beneficiaries often 
oppose alterations to maintain their advantages (Lipset & Rokkan 1968, Tsebelis 1990, Boix 1999, Rokkan 
2009, Mahoney & Thelen 2010). However, the research also uncovers deviations from this pattern. For in￾stance, certain parties, despite being advantaged by the current system, support reforms due to legal pres￾sures, political ideals, or a commitment to democratic principles. These deviations suggest that while stra￾tegic interests play a significant role, they are not the sole determinants of party behavior during the reform 
process. 
The study’s examination of conflict patterns within the decision-making process reveals that the nature and 
intensity of these conflicts are pivotal in shaping the final design of the biproportional reform. Contrary to 
the traditional emphasis on consensus as a desirable outcome in policy-making, the research indicates that 
conflict can play an equally important role in driving substantive changes. The conflicts between political 
actors, especially between those advantaged and those disadvantaged by the existing laws, are instrumental 
in achieving a more equitable outcome. This illustrates how political contention, rather than consensus, can 
drive substantive change and lead to more equitable outcomes, ultimately aligning reforms more closely 
with voter preferences and democratic ideals. 
Evaluating the political effects of the biproportional reforms shows that biproportionality significantly en￾hances vote-seat proportionality, resulting in more representative electoral outcomes – a valuable achieve￾ment in its own right. However, some anticipated secondary effects do not materialize as expected. The 
expected increases in voter turnout, political competition, and the number of effective political parties are 
limited. Instead, the reforms lead to a decrease in the number of electoral lists submitted, suggesting that 
political parties are fast in responding to the new guidelines and adapt their electoral strategies accordingly. 
This finding underscores the complex strategic implications of electoral reforms, as parties navigate these 
changes to enhance their positions. Additionally, the study indicates that the expected psychological impact 
of the reforms on voters – intended to boost engagement and trust in the electoral process – is somewhat 
muted. This limited effect may be attributed to factors such as insufficient public awareness or understand￾ing of the reforms, pointing to a need for improved communication and education about electoral changes. 
Several limitations of this study should be acknowledged. The focus on the biproportional method within 
Switzerland means that the findings may not be fully applicable to other countries or electoral systems with 
different political contexts and institutional structures. This concentration limits the generalizability of the 
results and suggests the need for comparative studies. Furthermore, the analysis relies on data from cantonal 

=== PDF PAGE 158 ===
August 2024 Redeeming the Pledge of Electoral Equity? Sandro Lüscher 
147 
elections where the biproportional system has been in place since 2005. This temporal limitation may con￾strain the study’s ability to capture long-term effects and evolving trends in voter behavior and party strat￾egies. 
Future research could address these limitations by exploring the long-term political effects of biproportional 
apportionment. Investigating how the method influences party strategies, voter behavior, and political sta￾bility over time would provide a more in-depth understanding of its impact. Comparative analyses with 
other PR-based systems used globally could offer valuable insights into the relative advantages and disad￾vantages of the biproportional method, potentially informing the adaptation and implementation of similar 
systems in other electoral contexts. 
Additionally, to gain more nuanced insights, it would be valuable to examine voter and party behavior at a 
more granular level, such as within individual constituencies. For instance, how does the voting behavior of 
minority party supporters in single-member districts in the canton of Graubünden change? Are fringe par￾ties now more inclined to field candidates in such districts? Are former abstainers motivated to vote because 
their vote now carries more weight? Do previous strategic voters now cast their ballots according to their 
first preference? 
Furthermore, do the procedural changes contribute to the centralization of parties’ electoral efforts, as some 
political actors have speculated? If so, does this pooling of resources at the cantonal level undermine the 
practical relevance of regional party sections, possibly even leading to their disappearance as feared by some? 
Exploring these and other questions would offer fascinating insights into the manifold consequences of in￾stitutional reforms.

merging partner BDP in some cantons)

new party label is disregarded.
